Programming Language
Explorations
Programming Language Explorations helps its readers gain proficiency in programming language practice and theory by presenting both example-focused, chapter-length explorations
of fourteen important programming languages and detailed discussions of the major concepts
transcending multiple languages. A language-by-language approach is sandwiched between an
introductory chapter that motivates and lays out the major concepts of the field and a final chapter that brings together all that was learned in the middle chapters into a coherent and organized
view of the field.
Each of the featured languages in the middle chapters is introduced with a common trio of
example programs and followed by a tour of its basic language features and coverage of interesting aspects from its type system, functional forms, scoping rules, concurrency patterns, and
metaprogramming facilities. These chapters are followed by a brief tour of over 40 additional
languages designed to enhance the reader’s appreciation of the breadth of the programming
language landscape and to motivate further study.
Targeted to both professionals and advanced college undergraduates looking to expand the range
of languages and programming patterns they can apply in their work and studies, the book pays
attention to modern programming practices, keeps a focus on cutting-edge programming patterns, and provides many runnable examples, all of which are available in the book’s companion
GitHub repository. The combination of conceptual overviews with exploratory example-focused
coverage of individual programming languages provides its readers with the foundation for more
effectively authoring programs, prompting AI programming assistants, and, perhaps most importantly, learning—and creating—new languages.
Ray Toal is Professor of Computer Science at LMU in Los Angeles where he’s been teaching
since 1986. He received his Ph.D. from UCLA in 1993 in Semantics, with minors in theoretical computer science and database systems. His current research interests are in programming
language design, language translation, APIs, and computer security. He has consulted for several
companies in the Los Angeles area, including Citysearch, Medaxis, Friendbuy, Handmade Mobile, M-GO, Criteo, Zoodiker, and (currently) Undivided, a startup with the mission of assisting
parents of special needs children in helping their children lead their best lives. Ray has authored
three books on programming languages and has been involved with projects at the Human Advancement Research Community (HARC). At LMU he enjoys interdisciplinary research and
working on creating new cross-college programs such as the Interactive Gaming and Immersive
Media minor, a collaboration between College of Science and Engineering and the School of
Film and Television. Since 2017, he has been the lead instructor for the LMU cohort of Google’s
Computer Science Summer Institute.

Preface
Much has happened since the question “Why would you want more than machine language?”
was answered.
• 1950s–60s: Several computing professionals, most notably Grace Hopper, John McCarthy, and John Backus, create high-level, machine-independent programming languages. We soon have Fortran for scientific computing, COBOL for business, Algol
for computing research, and Lisp for artificial intelligence. In the mid-1960s, PL/I and
Algol 68 emerge for multi-purpose computing.
• 1960s–1970s: With the realization that tangled control flow negatively impacts project
success, the structured programming revolution begins. Languages providing information hiding, such as Modula, CLU, Simula, Mesa, and Euclid address the “software
crisis.” Structured and modular features are bolted on to earlier languages. We also
see a golden area of alternative programming paradigms and groundbreaking research
yielding goal-driven computation, pattern matching, local and wide area networking,
interactive graphics, and spatial representations of code.
• 1980s: Object-oriented programming (OOP) takes over the world. Though begun in the
1960s with Simula and refined in the 1970s at Xerox PARC with Smalltalk, OOP—
or approximations to it—explodes in the 1980s with C with Classes (since renamed
C++), Objective-C, Eiffel, and Self. Earlier languages such as Lisp and Pascal gain
OO features, becoming Common Lisp and Object Pascal, respectively.
• 1990s: The World Wide Web appears and Perl becomes popular. Java, JavaScript,
and PHP are created with web applications in mind.
• 2000s: With machine speeds increasing, interest in established dynamic languages such
as Ruby takes off. Scala shows that static languages can feel dynamic. Clojure arrives
as a dynamic, modern Lisp, leveraging Java’s virtual machine.
• 2010s: Old things become new again. Multicore processors and “Big Data” revive
interest in functional programming. Older languages such as Python and R, and the
newer Julia language, find use in data science. Net-centric computing and performance
concerns make static typing popular again as Go, Rust, Kotlin, Swift, and Zig challenge
C and C++ for native applications.
• 2020s: The trend of marrying performance-intensive systems-oriented forms with expressive dynamic constructs continues and finds applications in programming AIpowered systems, as evidenced by languages like Mojo.
Each of the thirty-five languages we’ve mentioned, and the tens of thousands we did not, is
created for some purpose. New creations may address a particular problem domain, improve
upon old languages, or help us express computations in exciting new ways. There’s no single
best language for all possible tasks, so we learn many of them.
xiii

xiv ■ Preface
This book aims to acquaint you with a number of programming languages and with fundamental concepts transcending multiple languages. Language tours will present these concepts augmented with plenty of runnable scripts. We hope to provide you with a foundation
for more effectively authoring programs, prompting your AI programming assistants, and,
perhaps most importantly, learning—and creating—new languages.

ORGANIZATION
In order to become proficient in multiple languages, you must learn fundamental language
concepts. Conversely, mastering concepts requires that you learn multiple languages. Both
directions of influence are important! We’ve chosen to sandwich a language-by-language
approach between an introductory chapter that lays out the major concepts of the field
and a final chapter that brings together all that was learned in the middle chapters into a
coherent and organized view of the field, with the goal of facilitating future study.
As we present each language in the middle chapters, we’ll introduce new concepts as they
appear, and revisit familiar ones, comparing their implementation with those from languages
we’ve seen in prior chapters. We introduce each with a common trio of example programs,
take a brief tour of the basic language features, and highlight interesting aspects from its
type system, functional forms, scoping rules, concurrency patterns, and metaprogramming
facilities.
We’ve carefully chosen the order of languages to make a story out of the book. We start with
Lua for its elegant design and relatively small but powerful feature set. JavaScript follows
with similar features to Lua (such as first class functions and prototypal inheritance), while
adding a host of modern features and a powerful event-driven, asynchronous programming
model. As perhaps the world’s most popular language, JavaScript has influenced hundreds of
successors, the most popular of which, TypeScript, comes next, along with an introduction
to static types.
The next two languages, Python and Ruby, are general-purpose scripting languages, in
which we focus on first-class types and object-oriented programming, respectively. We then
encounter Java, an extraordinarily popular language in the enterprise computing space.
Java was introduced along with the Java platform, which includes a virtual machine and
an extensive set of powerful libraries for just about every task imaginable. Hundreds of
languages are targeted to this platform, including our next case study, Kotlin. This pair of
languages provides us with an opportunity to study how expressive forms in one language
are improved upon in a successor language.
Next comes Julia, a modern language for scientific computing featuring multidimensional
arrays and mechanisms for concurrent and distributed programming. It also introduces an
approach to object orientation—multimethods—shared by the next stop on our tour, Clojure. Clojure is a modern dialect of the venerable Lisp, famous for its hygienic macros,
support for functional programming, and concurrency. Next up is another functional language, Haskell, which while less popular, is essential to visit: its powerful type inference
mechanism and support for pure functions and lazy computing are not found in quite the
same way anywhere else on our tour. Our next language, Go, introduces explicit pointers, an interesting convention for package export, and its signature feature, the goroutine,
which enables large-scale concurrent and distributed applications rather unlike those of our
previous three languages.
Our last three languages place the burden of memory management knowledge on the programmer, forgoing the tracing garbage collector taken for granted in our first 11 languages.

Preface ■ xv
First is the venerable C, an intentionally small and unsafe system-level language, but
worth studying to see low-level details every other language hides from application programmers. Next up is Swift, exemplifying many of the modern trends in programming
language design—it is static, safe, and expressive—and achieves memory safety not with a
garbage collector but via reference counting. The much-loved Rust closes the tour with its
elegant approach to compile-time guarantees of memory and thread safety that surprisingly
maintain zero-cost abstractions.
Each language chapter ends with a summary, a link to the companion website where further
readings and references to open source projects can be found, and a collection of exercises,
designed as further explorations. The exercises motivate you to find the language’s online
playgrounds, REPLs, or downloadable installation, and to try out various interesting snippets of code and muse on what they say about the language. Occasionally we’ll suggest that
you write more substantial scripts, research various aspects of the language not covered in
the chapter proper, or ask you to do some comparisons between languages.
Following our featured language chapters, we provide a brief tour of a couple dozen or so additional languages, grouped into twelve thematic sections including classic languages, stylistic exemplars, web-oriented languages, concurrency-focused languages, and even esoteric
languages. We close with a chapter covering the major concepts of the field in a languageindependent fashion. The concepts are organized around three major themes:
• Classification. How are the information units manipulated by our programs represented? How are low-level notions of information chunked into units that humans can
process? How are these units of information classified? How do they behave? Answering these questions motivates the creation and development of type systems.
• Structure. How are programs—expressions of computations—linguistically structured? What are the major architectural elements, or entities from which programs
are formed? Answering these questions leads us to notions of naming and reference,
expressions, statements, objects, routines, modules, and more.
• Execution. How do computations unfold in time? How are processes and threads
implemented and how do they communicate? We’ll consider sequential, conditional,
iterative, synchronous and asynchronous flows, disruption, nondeterminism, concurrency, distribution, and parallelism.
By bringing the concepts together after introducing them in the context of whole-language
overviews with runnable programs, we can provide a technical overview of the entire landscape of the programming languages field to readers with a fair amount of existing, practical
knowledge. Our goal is to provide the strongest possible foundation to undertake deeper
study into programming language theory and implementation.

A NOTE ON THE SELECTED LANGUAGES
No choice of languages can possibly satisfy everyone; you will undoubtably see your favorite
languages missing, or wonder why the great influencers—Algol, Lisp, Smalltalk, Prolog, ML,
etc.—lack their own special chapter. Generally, the influencers have modern descendants:
Clojure is a modern Lisp; Ruby borrows much from Smalltalk; Haskell owes much to the
venerable ML family. Python, Ruby, and Java, each over 25 years old, continue to evolve in
significant ways. Recent languages, such as TypeScript, Julia, Go, Kotlin, and Swift, collect
many of the best ideas of the last fifty years. In the systems programming landscape, we
would be remiss to not cover C, but we’ve found Rust to cover most, and in some cases
more, of the interesting concepts found in the more popular C++.

xvi ■ Preface
We will generally emphasize ideas over features. Alan Kay [60] has written:
Programming languages can be categorized in a number of ways: imperative,
applicative, logic-based, problem-oriented, etc. But they all seem to be either an
“agglutination of features” or a “crystalization of style.” COBOL, PL/I, Ada,
etc., belong to the first kind; LISP, APL—and Smalltalk—are the second kind.
We haven’t selected only languages of the second kind, but we will try to emphasize big
ideas, insights, and idiomatic expression in addition to features. While we focus on today’s
languages, many of the great classics, along with a handful of newcomers and even a few
esoterics, are highlighted briefly in our “Additional Languages” chapter.

AUDIENCE
This book is targeted to both professionals and advanced college undergraduates looking to
expand the range of languages and programming patterns they can apply in their work and
studies. We’ve paid attention to modern programming practices, covered some cutting-edge
languages and patterns, and provided many runnable examples so readers will discover new
skills to apply to their craft. In addition, we’ve slipped in a bit of academic terminology
because knowledge of foundational principles is required to take the leap from hobbyist to
professional builder of scalable, eﬀicient systems. However, the book remains unapologetically an exploration of high-level language practice, placing it squarely between a tutorial
and a reference: we make no attempt to teach any particular language from scratch, nor to
provide complete descriptions of any language. Indeed, we’ve kept the focus on the concepts
and practices underlying programming language design and usage, many which are covered
within multiple whole-language overviews.
If you are an instructor looking for material for a programming languages or compiler
construction course, you may at first find our emphasis on a language tour unusual, but, we
hope, also quite useful (as we expect most students will). The traditional topics—binding,
expressions, control flow, types, modularity, concurrency, and metaprogramming—appear
several times to reinforce student learning: they are introduced in the opening chapter,
covered repeatedly within the context of whole-language overviews, then summarized in the
final chapter. Students have ample scenarios to explore how the various features within a
given language work together as well as how a given feature works across languages. You
can, however, open a traditional concept-oriented course with the introductory chapter,
then “drive” the course from the final chapter, selecting content from the middle chapters
as desired. You will find many, though intentionally not quite all, of the squares in a grid
similar to Figure 1 targeted.

PREREQUISITES
This book is not for beginning programmers. We’re assuming you know at least two
languages pretty well, and hopefully a couple more. You should know the basic concepts
surrounding variables, expressions, operators, functions, and basic data structures, and have
experience writing nontrivial applications—this book is a tour, not a tutorial. We will be
covering many interesting (and often powerful) features, and purposely do so with code
that is often very dense, and sometimes cryptic, even when presenting unfamiliar
paradigms for the first time. Be on the lookout for new concepts that are introduced within
the code examples themselves, rather than in the surrounding text. Don’t be surprised if bits
of unexplained syntax appear in the examples—welcome this chance to do some research
or converse with a colleague, mentor, or well-trained chatbot about the code.

Figure 1

Languages and concepts

We don’t enforce any particular style for running the book’s examples. Feel free to use your
favorite cloud platforms for authoring and running code, or find and install the software
needed to run the examples locally on your own machine. You may find the latter approach better allows you to keep up with the latest versions of each language and configure
your personal IDE or text editor with your favorite plugins for the most enjoyable coding
experience, but doing so is not required.
We’ve chosen to favor runnable scripts with assertions throughout the text, as opposed to
transcripts of sessions with a REPL. The former was chosen as it allowed us to run each
of the code examples through an automated test suite. However, when practicing with a
new language, we would very much encourage exploration and experimentation with REPLs
and similar playgrounds. Notes on REPL usage for various languages can be found on the
book’s companion website.

SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIAL
All of the code from the book, together with test scripts that show how to run the examples
locally, can be found in the online repository at https://github.com/rtoal/ple. We’ve
also included notes in the repository for installing and working with the languages featured
in the text, since this information changes too frequently to be mentioned in print. The
repository also contains additional code of interest, and will grow and be updated as the
programming languages evolve. Errata, additional exercises, and bonus material can be
found at the companion website at http://rtoal.github.io/ple/.

CHAPTER

1

Welcome

Hello! It appears that you might be interested in languages of the programming kind.
Whether you are certain that you are, or are not so sure that you are, we’ll try, in this
brief but very dense chapter, to pique your interest and make the study of programming
languages look not only fun, but worthwhile.

1.1 THE STUDY OF PROGRAMMING LANGUAGES
Learning new programming languages enables you to think about, and express, computations in new and sometimes surprising ways.
How so? Try writing a function to sum the squares of the even numbers in an array. If all
you know is C, you might think through this exercise as follows:
int sum_of_even_squares(int* a, unsigned int length) {
int total = 0;
for (unsigned int i = 0; i < length; i++) {
if (a[i] % 2 == 0) {
total += a[i] * a[i];
}
}
return total;
}
But this is unsatisfying: why is the variable i there? Why do we care about the indexes
of the array? Why does the function have to be passed the number of items in the array?
Certainly, there must be more concise and expressive ways to perform this task. In Swift,
we can iterate over the array elements directly:
func sumOfEvenSquares(of a: [Int]) -> Int {
var sum = 0
for x in a where x.isMultiple(of: 2) {
sum += x * x
}
return sum
}

1

2 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Slightly better, perhaps, but we can express a solution in a completely different way. Rather
than the for-loop putting each individual element in charge of the overall computation, we
can let the array do the work, by:
1. Selecting, or filtering by, its even elements,
2. Mapping the square operation over the selected elements, then finally
3. Reducing the squares to a single value by summing them together.
Many popular languages have these operations. Ruby does:
def sum_of_even_squares(a)
a.select{|x| x % 2 == 0}.map{|x| x * x}.reduce(0, :+)
end
And so does Clojure:
(defn sum-of-even-squares [a]
(->> a (filter even?) (map #(* % %)) (reduce +)))
We should be fair and show that Swift is just as capable:
func sumOfEvenSquares(of a: [Int]) -> Int {
return a.filter{$0 % 2 == 0}.map{$0 * $0}.reduce(0, +)
}
Java, too, can filter, map, and reduce:
public static int sumOfEvenSquares(int[] a) {
return IntStream.of(a).filter(x -> x%2==0).map(x -> x*x).sum();
}
Python expresses mapped and filtered sequences with a generator expression:
def sum_of_even_squares(a):
return sum(x*x for x in a if x % 2 == 0)
K uses powerful array operators to yield a remarkably terse one-liner:
sumofevensquares: {+/x[&~x!2]^2}
There’s more. Ask yourself: What happens after the assignment x = y + z? The variable
x is updated and the old value is lost forever, right? Not so fast! You may encounter
languages in which the “value” of x is its entire history of values, or in which x automatically
updates whenever y or z are subsequently changed. There are even languages that prohibit
assignment altogether!
The fun doesn’t stop there. In learning new languages, you’ll find lists that aren’t physically stored but produce their elements on demand, and models of the world where every
piece of data, even small integers, can act as “little computers” that can send and receive
messages. You may run into languages that can perform computation during type checking
and languages whose processes restart after they die. Language models and ideas such as
these are not only fascinating, but worthy of study. In [112], Bret Victor shows how work in
the 1960s and 1970s created programming models that manipulate data rather than code,

Welcome ■ 3
are parallel rather than sequential, use goals (what is needed) instead of procedures (how
to compute what is needed), and are represented spatially rather than with lines of text.
It would be a tragedy, he says, if people were to miss these and other ideas, or worse, to
simply master one way of programming and forget that they could ever have new ideas
about programming models.
Exposure to new languages can help you write better code in the languages you do use
every day as you discover ways to simulate features that your language lacks. Learning the
vocabulary of the concepts common to multiple languages may help you better understand
obscure error messages presented to you by a compiler. You’ll also acquire the foundation
to learn new languages more easily, and even design your own language. Even if you don’t
create the next Python or C++, remember that language design happens at small scales
too. You can create little languages to control robots or painting tools, structure commands
in a conversation-based game, describe formulas to input into a calculator or spreadsheet,
or specify questions to ask of a search engine or database.

1.2 PROGRAMMING LANGUAGE CONCEPTS
What are some of the concepts that will help guide our study of programming languages?
We can look to the various theories of computation have developed over the last century:
language theory, concerned with how computations are expressed, automata theory,
concerned with how computations are carried out, computability theory, concerned with
what can and cannot be computed, and complexity theory, concerned with quantifying
the resources required for computation.
Programming languages enable the communication of computational ideas between humans
and among humans and machines. While the question “what is computation?” is a little outside the scope of this book, we’ll assume that it involves the processing of information over
time. We can then ask questions to encourage various concepts to emerge in our attempts
to find answers:
• What kinds of information units do we wish to store and process, and what are their
behavioral capabilities? (Typing)
• How do we denote, or encode, these values? (Data Representation)

• From what kinds of entities can we build our programs, and how do we name these
entities? (Binding)
• How do we “chunk” information and computational procedures into larger units to
better manage large systems? (Abstraction)
• How can we build entities that are customizable in some way? (Parameterization)
• How do entities emit and respond to events? (Communication)

• How do we synchronize various sub-computations? When do we require sequential
execution and when do we allow true parallel execution? (Coordination)
• How can computations know about themselves? (Metaprogramming)

• How can we know our programs always do what they should? (Correctness)
• How do we know they never do what they should not? (Security)

4 ■ Programming Language Explorations
This list of questions is not intended to be complete. In fact, as our understanding of computation evolves over time, some of the questions and the areas of concern may even change.
What is important, though, is that these questions necessarily have multiple answers, and
it is precisely the variations in these answers that give rise to different programming languages.

1.3 PROGRAMMING LANGUAGE ELEMENTS
To understand the principles and concepts that facilitate the expression of computation,
we must go beyond general questions surrounding the nature of computation, and perform
the actual work of inventing and discovering the basic linguistic elements that underlie
these concepts. These elements provide us with the working vocabulary needed to describe
programming principles and compare and contrast languages in meaningful ways.
Computations process information units we call values. Values can be numeric, textual,
logical, computational, groupings of values, indirect references to other values, or indications
of missing or unknown information. A program manipulates values through entities—
structures that describe, store, or transform values in desired ways. Let’s explore a few of
the more popular kinds of values and entities.

1.3.1 Literals
A literal is a (direct) representation of a value. A few examples follow:
• 95 is a literal for the value ninety-five. Alternate representations for this value include
0x5F (0x is a common prefix for hexadecimal numerals), 0b1011111 (0b for binary),
and 0.0095E4 (E means “times-ten-to-the”).
• true (sometimes True or T) is the literal representing truth; false (or False or F) is
the literal representing falsity.
• '*' is a character literal, denoting the character named asterisk.

• The quoted sequence of characters "Hello, how are you?" is a string literal representing a common English greeting.
• :alice (rendered in some languages as just alice) is a kind of literal known as an
atom, or symbol. Atoms stand only for themselves and nothing else. Unlike strings,
they are not decomposed into character sequences.
• [0, true, 98.6] is a literal for the sequence of three values: zero, truth, and ninetyeight point six.
• {latitude: 29.9792, longitude: 31.1344} is a literal representing the location of
the Great Pyramid of Giza.
• x => x / 2 is a literal representing a function that halves its argument.

• null (sometimes nil or None) is a literal indicating the intentional absence of a value.

• undefined is a literal used to indicate some desired value is unknown or purposely
not being divulged. Think of this as “I don’t know,” “I don’t care,” or “None of your
business.”

Welcome ■ 5

1.3.2

Variables

A variable provides access to a value through a name. Do not confuse variables with
values: variables may stand for, own, or hold values, they are not themselves values. Nor
should you confuse a variable with its name. Instead, a variable is a container into which a
value can be stored. If a variable is allowed to store different values throughout a program’s
execution, it may be more properly called an assignable [42]. We often think of variables as
boxes; for example, the sequence of JavaScript assignments x = 1; y = true; z = "so "
+ y.toString() produces the useful rendering:
y

x 1

true

z "so true"

Compound values, generally known as objects, store values in “internal” variables, called
properties, fields, slots, or attributes. For example, we may have a variable named
airport holding an object with two internal variables, one of which is itself an object:
airport

city 'Utqiaġvik'
location

latitude 71.2874
longitude -156.7803

Many languages provide the ability to mark a variable immutable, prohibiting the variable
from ever containing a value other than its initial value. Variables that are allowed to store
new, updated, values are mutable. We use the term constant for an immutable variable
whose value is known before the program is run.1 If values in at least one of the internal
variables (fields) of an object may be changed, we say the object itself is mutable, otherwise
immutable.

1.3.3

References

Interestingly, some languages have prohibitions on the kinds of values that variables can
store! For example, Lua and JavaScript allow variables to hold numbers, booleans, and
strings, but do not permit variables to hold object values directly—they must instead hold
a special kind of value, known as a reference, which provides an additional level of access
to a value. So, although the syntax of these languages make it appear that an object is being
stored, as in the following Lua script:
a = {x=3, y=5}
b = a.y
c = true
d = {x=3, y=5}
e = d

-- creates an object, stores reference in a
-- directly stores 5 into b
-- directly stores true into c
-- creates an object, stores reference in d
-- does not create an object, induces sharing

1 While this definition may be technically precise, do note that vocabulary sometimes, unfortunately,
varies from language to language. You may hear the term “constant” used to denote any immutable variable,
even one initialized during program execution. Sometimes the term will even be used to describe a literal.

6 ■ Programming Language Explorations
the variables a, d, and e hold reference values, not object values, like so:
a

x 3

b 5

y 5

c true
d

x 3

e

y 5

References can be very helpful! For example, assignments, such as e = d above, avoid the
cost of copying (potentially large amounts of) internal object data, which can translate into
significant performance gains.
However, references come with security implications. Assigning references creates aliasing:
d.x and e.x are now two names for the same variable. An assignment to one updates the
other! This sharing of objects through references can lead to accidental (or malicious) modification through these aliases. Programmers, therefore, have the responsibility for knowing
exactly when references are used and when object internals are copied—rules that, for better or worse, vary from language to language. They must also ensure references cannot be
manipulated by attackers into exposing sensitive data.
The target of a reference—the referent—requires storage space known as memory. If
variable holding a reference is reassigned, there is a possibility that the previously referenced
value is no longer accessible. Here’s another example in Lua:
p = {x=3, y=5}
p = {x=2, y=8}
The second assignment renders the first object inaccessible:
p

x 3

y 5

x 2

y 8

Inaccessible storage is oﬀicially known as garbage as it wastes system resources. Different
languages deal with garbage in different ways. Many languages (including those in Chapters 2 through 12 in this text) provide a garbage collector that runs behind the scenes,
picking up after the programmer. Some track, or even count, references to data, reclaiming
inaccessible storage when the last reference goes away. Some give the programmer no support at all, requiring the programmer to both request storage when needed and give back,
or free, storage that is no longer needed. Freed storage can be reallocated by the runtime
for future requests. Attempting to use freed storage or freeing the same storage area twice
can lead to serious problems; we’ll cover these in our chapter on C.

Welcome ■ 7
In some, but not all, languages, you will encounter this:

This is a reference to nothing, known as a null reference. It is not a reference to the value
null, nor even to undefined. There’s nothing on the receiving end—no referent. Nothing
to dereference. What could go wrong? Apparently, quite a bit. The inventor of the null
reference, Sir Charles Anthony Richard Hoare, back in 2009, stated:
I call it my billion-dollar mistake. It was the invention of the null reference in
1965.... My goal was to ensure that all use of references should be absolutely
safe, with checking performed automatically by the compiler. But I couldn’t
resist the temptation to put in a null reference, simply because it was so easy
to implement. This has led to innumerable errors, vulnerabilities, and system
crashes, which have probably caused a billion dollars of pain and damage in the
last forty years.
Despite the number of potential disasters surrounding references, they are essential to modern programming. Language designers have taken note and, perhaps with the exception of
C, have gone to great lengths to make their use relatively safe, without sacrificing too much
convenience. But even in safe languages, programmer vigilance is required, especially when
dealing with shared references to mutable entities.

1.3.4

Types

A type, roughly, determines a collection of values with some prescribed behavior. That a
value v has type T means that only certain operations may be applied to v. The notion of
“type” in programming language theory is extremely deep and has filled entire books, for
example [89]. Typing is central to the study of languages, as the point of a language’s type
system is to provide (meaningful) constraints on what we can and cannot say.
We have an intuitive sense that values belong to types—true to the boolean type, 0.3 to
some kind of numeric type, and "Hello" to the string type. Exactly how these types are
defined, though, can vary significantly by language.
Some have bounded integer types (see Table 1.1) that map directly to eﬀicient hardware
arithmetic units, but must deal with overflow. Consider the 8-bit signed integers, bounded
to the range −128 . . . 127. What is 123 + 8? 131 is not in the range! Should the operation
cause an error, saturate to 127 (“as high as it can go”), or “wrap around” to −125 (with
modular arithmetic)? Some languages provide an unbounded integer type to sidestep
this problem.
For floating-point numbers, many languages use the IEEE-754 [51] standards binary32
and binary64. The former represents some 4 billion values between −3.4 × 10−38 and
−3.4 × 1038 ; the latter represents some 18 quintillion values between −1.7977 × 10−308 and
−1.7977 × 10308 . Each also includes −∞, +∞ and a special value NaN, meaning not-anumber—a useful result for operations such as 0/0 and ∞ − ∞. Since floating-point number
schemes must choose which numbers to represent and which not to, floating-point arithmetic
is not exact. Even the high-precision binary64 standard is subject to round-off error (e.g.,
0.1 + 0.2 = 0.30000000000000004). Some languages allow the programmer to bypass these
errors with arbitrary-precision real number types (often called decimal types) and with
ratio (a.k.a. rational) types that avoid round-off errors in integer division.

8 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Bits
8
16
32
64
Table 1.1

Signed
−128 . . . 127
−32768 . . . 32767
−2147483648 . . . 2147483647
−9223372036854775808 . . . 9223372036854775807

Unsigned
0 . . . 255
0 . . . 65535
0 . . . 4294967295
0 . . . 18446744073709551615

Fixed-size integers

For text, units of writing, called graphemes, are made from units of textual information,
called characters. For example, the grapheme for (the emoji) Woman scientist is composed
of three characters: woman, zero-width joiner, and microscope. Languages often provide a string type for character sequences, and allow extraction of code points and the
underlying bytes that encode characters. Grapheme extraction usually requires external
libraries.
You may often see hierarchies of types, where a value of a subtype (e.g., Dog) can be used
wherever a value of a supertype (e.g., Animal) is expected. You may find languages with only
a single list type, and others that distinguish, say, lists of integers from lists of strings. In
the latter, a generic, or parametric, type includes a type parameter (e.g., List<T>), and
concrete types instantiate the parameter (e.g., List<String>). Function types are similar:
some languages place all functions into a single type, while others feature a generic function
type parameterized on the types of arguments and permitted return values.
While every value has a type (or even multiple types, when supertypes and subtypes are
present), many languages allow the imposition of type constraints on variables. For example, the Java declaration
var message = "Please wait, this may take a minute";
constrains the variable message to values of type String. When a language enforces constraints at compile-time (before the program is run), the constraint defines the variable’s
static type. If it is possible to compute the type of a variable’s value at run-time (during
program execution), the computed type is a dynamic type. Some languages have one type
system for the static and dynamic types. Some, including TypeScript, Python, and Java
have a rich set of static types and a weaker set of dynamic types.
Closely related to the notion of type is that of class, which, roughly, is a kind of factory
for instantiating objects that have a particular internal structure. Some languages have no
classes, some conflate the notion of class and type, and some take great care to distinguish
classes from types. There’s more: interfaces and typeclasses are types that other types
can conform to. For example, a type whose instances can be tested for equality might
belong to the typeclass Equatable; if its instances can be compared via <, it might belong
to the typeclass Comparable. Types with least and greatest values may be members of the
Bounded typeclass; collection types supporting iteration might belong to Iterable. Going
meta, in some languages the type of type is just a type; in others, it’s called a kind.
When values are combined in a way that is inconsistent with their types, such as dividing a
number by a string, a language definition must specify whether to (1) declare the program
erroneous, or (2) find replacement values to make things work. JavaScript, for example,
evaluates 15 / "3" as 5, as the string "3" looks like a number. Languages that favor
replacements, known as coercions, are weakly typed; those that are stricter and favor
reporting type errors are strongly typed.

Welcome ■ 9

1.3.5

Expressions

An expression is a combination of literals, variables, and operators that is evaluated to
produce a value. Some operators require all of their operands to be evaluated; others, like
the popular && (“and then”) and || (“or else”), do not. An example of expression evaluation
(using JavaScript for concreteness), where s holds the value "car", y holds the value 100,
and found holds the value true, follows:
7 * s.indexOf('r') + Math.sqrt(y) / 2 <= 0 || !found
⇒ 7 * 2 + Math.sqrt(y) / 2 <= 0 || !found
⇒ 14 + Math.sqrt(y) / 2 <= 0 || !found
⇒ 14 + Math.sqrt(100) / 2 <= 0 || !found
⇒ 14 + 10 / 2 <= 0 || !found
⇒ 14 + 5 <= 0 || !found
⇒ 19 <= 0 || !found
⇒ false || !found
⇒ !found
⇒ !true
⇒ false
Operators with a single operand are unary operators; those with two operands, binary,
and three, ternary. Unary operators generally appear in the prefix position, as in the
expressions -x or ~x, though postfix is sometimes seen. Binary operators often appear as
infix operators (between the operands). In general, the number of operands is known as
the operator’s arity and the position of the operator is known as its fixity.
The precedence of an operator determines whether its application to its operands precedes
applications of operators appearing at the same level in the source code. To say operator ∗
has higher precedence than operator + means that a + b ∗ c parses as a + (b ∗ c), that is,
multiplication precedes addition in the evaluation. Associativity describes the structure
of expressions among operators of the same precedence: given the expression a • b • c, a
left-associative • would cause the expression to parse as (a • b) • c (leftmost operation
applied first), right-associativity would induce the parse a • (b • c), and if the operator were
nonassociative, the expression would be illegal.2

1.3.6

Statements

A statement performs an action. Common statement types include (1) declaration statements to bring named entities (e.g., variables, functions, types) into existence, (2) expression
statements to evaluate expressions, (3) assignment statements, (4) invocation statements
to execute chunks of code, (5) conditional statements (if, unless, switch, match, when)
to execute code only under certain conditions, (6) iteration statements (loop, while, for,
repeat) to execute code repeatedly, and (7) disruptive statements (break, continue, retry,
throw, return, yield, resume) for changing the “normal” control flow.
Unlike expressions which produce values, statements are executed for their effects (sometimes called side-effects), such as updating the values held in variables, or writing to files
or databases. Statements are often combined to run sequentially (run s1 then s2 ), nonde2 For example, many languages make relational operators such as < nonassociative, since the expression
x<y<z does not have an obvious interpretation: is it (x<y)<z or perhaps (x<y)and(y<z)?

10 ■ Programming Language Explorations
terministically (run either s1 or s2 ), or in parallel (run s1 and s2 “at the same time,” either
on separate processor cores or with interleaved execution on a single core).

1.3.7 Routines
A routine is a (possibly parameterized) unit of code. Routines that can only start from
the beginning when called are known as subroutines; those able to resume from where
they last left off are known as coroutines. Some languages use the term function in
place of subroutine. Others use the term only for subroutines that return values, while
using procedure for subroutines returning no values. Routines that are properties of—
or directly associated with—an object, whose execution reads or manipulates the object’s
state, are often known as methods.
A running program is known as a process. The operating system will allocate one or more
threads (lines of execution) to the process, onto which subroutines and coroutines are
mapped. In some cases, the programmer may access these platform threads directly; in
others, the runtime system chooses its own strategy to map routines onto threads. The
terms thread and process may also be used for concurrently executing units within a program: threads communicate via shared memory, while processes maintain their own local
memory and communicate exclusively via messages. While coroutines must interleave their
execution to form a single sequence of instructions and explicitly yield control to each other,
threads and processes run independently and may be preempted by the operating system
when their number exceeds the number of available hardware execution units.
Routines may be invoked directly in code or be triggered by events. Events may originate
outside of a program, via a user action (mouse click, form text entry, button press, device
rotation, sensory input from a vision or haptic system), or within the program via event
emitters.

1.3.8 Errors
When (not if) things go awry, it is often best to fail fast and terminate the current thread
of execution, so that inconsistencies do not spread throughout the computation and cause
significant damage down the line. But if a problem can be dealt with within a program
(and possibly recovered from), we may benefit from errors being instances of a special error
type.
Many languages provide a means for throwing, or raising, error objects to signal a problem, and a means to catch, or handle, thrown errors. Throwing and catching, while sometimes convenient, disrupt the normal control flow and are not universally adopted. As an
alternative, some languages permit operations to return two values, one indicating success
or failure, and the other for the expected value on success. You may also find result values which contain the expected value or an error. Some languages offer direct support for
the “error” condition of an entity not being found in a search through the use of option
values—containers that either hold the found value or are empty, indicating the desired
value is not present.

1.3.9 Modules
Modern languages support the notion of programming in the large via modules or packages, which encapsulate strongly related entities of a library or application. Modules may

Welcome ■ 11
bundle types, objects, functions, coroutines, or even other modules. The programmer may
export some of the entities to the rest of the program, leaving the others private to the
module. Modules may also import entities from other modules. Import and export are usually explicit but can be implicit as well. Often, but not always, modules correspond to files.
Modules are somewhat related to namespaces, which generally refer to ad-hoc groupings
of names that need not be tied to any particular module.

1.4

PROGRAMMING LANGUAGE EVALUATION

Why do people sometimes argue over their favorite languages, often claiming “their” language is better than “your” language? All languages have their flaws, which can be a
source of great humor: Gary Bernhardt’s WAT [11], Melissa Elliott’s PHP Manual Masterpieces [25], and Eric Wastl’s PHP Sadness [114]. But most languages actually do some
things well and other things not so well. You might be able to rate a language’s suitability for
certain tasks, but an overall language rating is not worth looking for. To judge a language,
you must know why it was designed. Let’s take a look at a few of these reasons:

Programming machine learning applications

It’s a good bet that a language designed expressly for the purpose of formatting printed
documents might not be good for writing web servers. So why argue whether PostScript
is “better than” Go? The correct retort to “A is better than B” is “for what, exactly?”
Languages more often complement each other rather than compete with other.

1.4.1

Classification

In addition to understanding the kinds of problems a language was designed to solve, evaluation demands also that we understand how a language solves these problems. After all,
programming languages exist so we humans can express computational ideas in ways that
make sense to us. But there is a great diversity in the ways we can organize units of information and units of computation. Many of these forms can be characterized in a manner
that is useful for comparing and contrasting languages. Unlike book outlines and company
organization charts, we can’t fit programming languages into a neat, single, hierarchical
ontology. This isn’t too surprising: in many knowledge disciplines, hierarchies simply don’t
exist. Clay Shirky makes this point beautifully in his essay Ontology is Overrated [102].

Welcome ■ 13
When there is no hierarchy, we employ tags. Examples of common tags used to label languages follow:

A given language will generally have quite a few tags. Java, for example, was introduced to
the world as simple, object-oriented, familiar, robust, secure, architecture neutral, portable,
high-performance, interpreted, threaded, and dynamic [33]. And several languages do their
best to blend the functional and object-oriented styles.

1.4.2

Criteria

Now what might the criteria be upon which we evaluate languages? We may look at technical
reasons:
• Readability: A significant portion of a programmer’s time is spent trying to figure
out existing code. Code must be understandable enough to be validated, fixed, or
adapted to new requirements.
• Writability: Sometimes a language makes programming a chore, requiring one to
fit a solution into the language’s “opinionated” constraints. James Hague calls these
puzzle languages, writing: “if it takes focused thought to phrase that solution into
working code, you go down one path then back up, then give up, then try something
completely different—then you’re almost certainly using a puzzle language.” [40]
• Expressiveness: It’s quite lovely when we can express ourselves without a lot of code,
such as writing A=B+C when A, B, and C are matrices, or when we can employ highlevel constructs to make concurrent programming safe without us having to remember
to acquire and release locks.
• Guidance: It can be very frustrating when a programmer uses a value of the wrong
type, tries to use an uninitialized variable, forgets a case in a switch statement, or
mis-indents, and the code happily executes—with surprising results. Some languages
try to make common mistakes impossible to write, guiding you in the direction of
constructs more likely to be correct.
• Security: The field of secure software development is concerned with ensuring external inputs cannot cause a program to divulge confidential information, corrupt data,
slow to a crawl, or crash. “Safe” languages that make it easy to set and check bounds,
control failures, create immutable values, and set privilege levels go a long way to
minimizing attack surfaces that lead to costly exploits.
• Eﬀiciency: Many languages have been expressly designed to allow for eﬀicient compilation, execution, or both. Who wants to sit around waiting for code to compile,4
or run?
4 Obligatory xkcd link: http://xkcd.com/303/

14 ■ Programming Language Explorations
These technical criteria comprise the core focus of this book. But let’s not forget that
language preference, for us human beings, is often driven more by “softer,” criteria. Perhaps,
for language X:
• It might be the only language suitable for a specific problem.
• You just like it. We all have our own preferences.

• You need to hire enough people that already know it. Some languages are very popular,
some are not. The more popular a language is, the larger, too, the training set for AI
code assistants is likely to be, making your organization more productive.
• There is a wealth of development tools (IDEs, fast compilers), or resources (books,
blog posts, and activity on programming Q&A sites) for it.
• A government or notable company created or backed it (PL/I—IBM, Java—Sun, Go—
Google, TypeScript—Microsoft, Rust—Mozilla, Swift—Apple, Verse—Epic Games).
• Everyone else is using it. There was once an old saying: “No manager ever got fired
for choosing Java.”
• Your company already invested too much in it, and it’s too expensive to change.

• You’re sticking with it because you’re too tired, or don’t have enough time, to learn
something new.

1.4.3 Tradeoffs
When you finally go out on a limb and proclaim your love of language A, or that your team
should use language B for task C, you should be ready with an evidence-based rationale,
perhaps informed from the criteria above. If your decision is less than popular, you may be
challenged with questions such as why your preferred language X does not have feature Y .
The answer may be “because it has feature Z instead.” For instance:
• The expressive power of dynamic typing, persistent data structures, functions as firstclass values, higher-order functions, and closures (all topics we’ll introduce in the
book) saves development time and makes it easier to reason about program behavior,
but may negatively impact runtime performance.
• Automatic garbage collection, where unreachable memory is automatically freed up
without direct programmer intervention, may save thousands of hours of programmer
time, but should not be used in embedded, life-critical, real-time systems. We do not
want a collector to pause your program to free up space while your code is performing
a time-sensitive complicated maneuver in a passenger aircraft or delivering radiation
to a patient.
These tradeoffs, and others like them, tell us why there cannot be a best language for
all situations. But this makes life better, because we get so many choices, as we will see
throughout this book. The next fourteen chapters will take you through fourteen modern
languages and the choices made by their designers. We’ll then present a chapter containing
brief highlights of a few dozen more. We’ll see design tradeoffs time and time again, and
summarize and expound on them in the book’s final chapter. We hope that the book will
not only dramatically increase your programming proficiency, but also provide you the tools
necessary to design languages of your own. Have fun!

CHAPTER

2

Lua

Lua is a “powerful, fast, lightweight, embeddable scripting language.” [67]
First appeared 1993
Creators Roberto Ierusalimschy, Waldemar Celes, Luiz Henrique de Figueiredo
Recognized for Tables, Interoperability with C
Notable uses World of Warcraft, Angry Birds, Scripting
Tags Imperative, Prototypal, Scripting
Six words or less Lightweight, fast, powerful scripting language
Lua was born in 1993 at PUC-Rio, Pontifícia Universidade Católica do Rio de Janeiro. It
has been evolving steadily but is still quite small in terms of the number of concepts and
basic features. It is therefore a natural place to start our tour.
The language comes with a small number of types and operators, the usual statement repertoire (assignments, conditionals, loops, calls), flexible functions and coroutines, and a single
data structure—the table—that does double duty representing both traditional arrays and
key-value dictionaries. Lua’s small size should not be confused with a lack of power; on
the contrary, Lua is extensible. Lua tables can have metatables, providing the ability to
customize lookup and override operators to build expressive user-defined datatypes. Lua’s
functions can be assigned to variables and passed to and returned from other functions.
It can interoperate with other languages, including C, C++, Java, Fortran, Smalltalk, and
Erlang.
As the first language on our tour, we’ll use Lua to introduce many of the basic elements of
programming, such as values, types, variables, expressions, operators, statements, and the
all-important references. We’ll cover various aspects of functions: argument passing, scope,
the handling of free variables, closures, and anonymous and higher-order functions. We will
then go into some depth on its signature type, the table. We’ll explain how metatables
provide a mechanism for creating new datatypes. We’ll see how Lua allows its operators to
take on new meanings under programmer control. We’ll close with Lua’s implementation of
a popular mechanism for concurrency: coroutines.

for c = 1, 40 do
for b = 1, c-1 do
for a = 1, b-1 do
if a * a + b * b == c * c then
print(string.format("%d, %d, %d", a, b, c))
end
end
end
end
We’re terribly sorry for the flashback to high school math, but this code serves as a more
interesting opener than “Hello, world.” It outputs all possible integer-valued right triangles
with side lengths through size 40 (as seen in Figure 2.1).

Lua ■ 17
As mentioned in the preface of this book, we’re expecting you to have programming experience, so you can probably figure out what the for-loops and the if-statement do, and
surmise that print writes to standard output. Unlike many popular languages, though,
Lua’s for-loop range bounds are inclusive on both ends, so side lengths of length 40 are covered. String formatting interpolates the second through final arguments into placeholders in
the first argument according to a given format specifier. The specifier %d in our example
renders the corresponding argument as a decimal integer.
Continuing our sneak peek, here’s a script that writes the permutations of its command line
arguments to standard output. Permutations are generated via Heap’s Algorithm [120] to
illustrate that Lua is just fine with recursion:
function print_permutations(a, n)
if n == 0 then
print(table.unpack(a))
else
for i = 1, n-1 do
print_permutations(a, n-1)
local j = n % 2 == 0 and i or 1
a[j], a[n] = a[n], a[j]
end
print_permutations(a, n-1)
end
end
print_permutations(arg, #arg)
Here the built-in table arg contains the command line arguments and # is the length operator. The function table.unpack returns all of the table’s elements (as Lua functions can
return multiple values) and print outputs them separated by tabs. Parallel assignments
make an appearance here too: the expressions on the right are evaluated then assigned to
the variables on the left, so swapping array elements can be written concisely.
$ lua permutations.lua I like carrots

We wish to write a script to display the top ten scorers by points per game across all players
appearing in at least 15 games:
players = {}
for line in io.lines() do
team, name, games, points = line:match("([^,]+),([^,]+),(%d+),(%d+)")
if tonumber(games) >= 15 then
table.insert(players, {name=name, team=team, ppg=points/games})
end
end
table.sort(players, function (p1, p2) return p1.ppg > p2.ppg end)
for index, player in ipairs(players) do
if index > 10 then
break
end
format = "%-22s%-4s%8.2f"
print(string.format(format, player.name, player.team, player.ppg))
end
The details of this rather dense script will become apparent through the remainder of the
chapter, but a few are worth a brief mention. Lines of standard input are iterated with
io.lines; at each line, we extract the four comma-separated fields using a pattern and
populate the players table with a subtable holding the name, team, and points per game

Lua ■ 19
of any player appearing in 15 or more games. After sorting the table by points-per-game
descending, we print the items of the table, terminating (via break) the loop after 10 players
have been displayed. Lua, like most programming languages, features mini-languages for
both pattern matching and output formatting. Our pattern for matching input lines is one
or more noncomma characters ([^,]+), a comma, one or more noncomma characters, a
comma, a sequence of one or more digits, a comma, and finally one or more digits. The
output format string specifies a 22-character left-justified string field (%-22s), a 4-character
left-justified string field (%-4s), and an eight-character field holding a floating point number
with three digits after the radix point (8.3f).
Assuming the data above is stored in the file wnba.txt, we can use command line redirection
(a feature of most standard operating system shells) to run our script:
$ lua top_ten_scorers.lua < wnba.txt

These three scripts were meant only to give a sneak peek of the language, leaving details
to the remainder of the chapter. We now turn to our technical overview of Lua, beginning
with a look at the basic language elements.

2.2

THE BASICS

Lua scripts are sequences of statements. Statements include assignments, function declarations, function calls, if-statements for conditional execution, blocks (introduced with the
do keyword), and three kinds of loops: while, repeat, and for. The return and break
statements allow exiting from functions and loops, respectively.
All values in Lua belong to one of eight types:
• nil, whose only instance is the value nil

• boolean, with exactly two instances, true and false

• number, whose instances are convenient numeric values of the underlying hardware
• string, whose instances are sequences of 8-bit bytes

• table, whose instances are key-value pairs, described in Section 2.6

• function, whose instances are parameterized code blocks, callable as subroutines
• thread, whose instances are parameterized code blocks, callable as coroutines

• userdata, whose instances hold data from a host language (as Lua programs are often
embedded within programs written in other languages)

20 ■ Programming Language Explorations
We can explore values and types in the Lua REPL (read-eval-print loop), accessible from
the command line:
$ lua
> 9 ^ 2
81.0
> type(false)
boolean
> #"hello"
5
While we encourage you to use a REPL for exploration and practicing your skills, we’ll be
illustrating code with runnable scripts featuring assertions. When run, these scripts will
either satisfy all assertions and exit cleanly or crash if any assertion were to fail. We’ve
pre-run each of the scripts in this book, so we can be confident that the evaluations shown
are correct. Here’s an illustration of the Lua built-in function type:
function first_few_primes()
return {2, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13}
end
assert(type(7.299E-3) == "number")
assert(type(true and false) == "boolean")
assert(type("café") == "string")
assert(type(nil) == "nil")
assert(type(first_few_primes) == "function")
assert(type(first_few_primes()) == "table")
assert(type(coroutine.create(first_few_primes) == "thread"))
Lua (as of version 5.4) has 25 operators. Table 2.1 lists them from highest to lowest precedence, and the following script shows them in action:
assert(8 + 5 * 3 == 23)
assert(8 - 5 - 2 == 1)
assert(3 ^ 5 == 243)
assert(19 / 5 == 3.8)
assert(19 // 5 == 3)
assert(0x1000 == 4096)
assert(9.3E-5 == 0.000093)
assert(3 ~= 5)
assert(-10 <= 3)
assert("dog" < "dogs")
assert(#{5, 8, 13} == 3)
assert("Got" .. "7" == "Got7")

Though users only “see” a single number type, Lua uses different internal representations for
integers and floating-point numbers. The operators &, |, ~, << and >> only accept integers;
furthermore, arithmetic works a bit differently between the two subtypes. The technical
details describing working with the two subtypes, and the rules for arithmetic, can be found
in the Lua Reference Manual [68].

Strings are written as graphemes enclosed in single or double quotes. A grapheme is userreceived unit of writing, made up of one or more units of textual information, known as
characters. Lua implementations are likely to use the Unicode standard, which assigns
to each character a name and a code point in the range 0..1114111.1 A few assignments,
showing code points in hexadecimal per custom, are:

22 ■ Programming Language Explorations
String literals can also contain escape sequences for convenient representations of nonprintable characters or characters that are inconvenient to type or are not available in many
fonts. For example, the string literal "\t\n" consists of the two characters U+0009 character tabulation (the “tab” character) and U+000A line feed. Using \u you can
insert characters via code point: the string "\u{1F1F1}\u{1F1E7}" is a string containing
the flag of Lebanon. It consists of one grapheme, and two code points. So what is the length
of this string? Experimenting in the REPL we see it is 8:
> s = "\u{1F1F1}\u{1F1E7}"
> #s
8
Although you can write strings with graphemes, Lua strings are, internally, byte sequences.
The length of the string is the number of bytes required to encode the grapheme sequence.
The popular UTF-8 encoding scheme encodes code points into 1–4 bytes as follows:
Code points
0...7f
80...7ff
800...ffff
10000...1fffff

Encoding
0x7 ...x0
110x10 ...x6 10x5 ...x0
1110x15 ...x12 10x11 ..x6 10x5 ...x0
11110x20 ...x18 10x17 ...x12 10x11 ..x6 10x5 ...x0

Let’s explore with a detailed example. The string literal "Ć←afé" contains five graphemes,
(and coincidentally five code points), but is stored as a sequence of 9 bytes. We can easily
work with both code points and byte values. These can be injected into strings using the
\u and \x escape sequences, respectively. The code points of a string are accessed via
utf8.codepoint and its bytes via string.byte. The latter two functions return each of
the code points or each of the bytes within a given range of the string. Range bounds are
inclusive and start at 1, so 1 through #s covers the entire string.
s = "Ć←afé"
-- Character
CodePoint
-- LATIN CAPITAL LETTER C WITH ACUTE
262
-- LEFTWARDS ARROW
8592
-- LATIN SMALL LETTER A
97
-- LATIN SMALL LETTER F
102
-- LATIN SMALL LETTER E WITH ACUTE
233
assert(#s == 9)
assert(utf8.len(s) == 5)

Bytes
0xc4 0x86
0xe2 0x86 0x90
0x61
0x66
0xc3 0xa9

-- counts bytes
-- counts code points

-- Strings can be assembled from code points in two ways
assert(utf8.char(262, 8592, 97, 102, 233) == s)
assert("\u{106}\u{2190}\u{61}\u{66}\u{e9}" == s)
-- Or assembled from UTF-8 bytes (must be in hex)
assert("\xc4\x86\xe2\x86\x90\x61\x66\xc3\xa9" == s)
print(utf8.codepoint(s, 1, #s))
print(string.byte(s, 1, #s))

-- print each code point
-- print each byte

Lua ■ 23
Variables directly hold values of the types nil, boolean, number, and string, but hold references to values of types table, function, thread, and userdata. Lua variables come in three
flavors: global variables, local variables (discussed in Section 2.4), and table fields (discussed
in Section 2.6). Variables always hold a value; there is no concept of an “undeclared” or
“undefined” variable. The value of any variable that has never been previously assigned is
simply nil.
print(x)
assert(x == nil)
local y
assert(y == nil)
t = {a=1, b=2}
assert(t.c == nil)
print(y, c, t.d)

-- this does not fail!
-- unassigned global x is simply nil
-- also fine
-- missing initializer is implicitly nil
-- unassigned table fields are just nil
-- happily prints three nils

Assignments are not limited to binding a single value. In the statement
x, y = 0, 0
we have a name list on the left and an expression list on the right. An expression list
isn’t a value in its own right: it just identifies expressions that are evaluated then assigned
to the variables on the left side. “Extra” expressions on the right are ignored; extra names
on the left become nil.
a, b = 5, 6, 7
c, d, e, f = 5, 6, 7

-- extra results ignored
-- extra vars become nil

assert(a == 5 and b == 6)
assert(c == 5 and d == 6 and e == 7 and f == nil)
Lua sometimes enforces the expected types of operands, but at other times will coerce
a value of the unexpected type to a similar value of the expected type. Anything can be
coerced to a boolean, with nil and false becoming false, and everything else true, though
that works one-way only: true and false are not coerced to anything (not even 1 and 0,
common in other languages). Strings and numbers are coercible to each other; however, the
attempt to coerce a nonnumeric-looking string to a number generates an error.
-- strings are coerced to numbers
assert("55" + "3" == 58)
-- because + is only for numbers
assert("3" * 5 == 15)
-- because * is only for numbers
-- and numbers to strings
assert(55 .. 3 == "553")

-- because .. is only for strings

-- and anything to a boolean
assert(3 or false)
assert(0 or false)
-- yes, 0 is a true value
assert("" or false)
-- yes, the empty string is a true value
assert({} or false)
-- yes, the empty table is a true value
assert(not (nil or false)) -- Only nil and false are false values

24 ■ Programming Language Explorations
-- The following are errors
-- false + 1
-- cannot do arithmetic on booleans
-- "the answer is" .. true -- cannot concatenate booleans
-- false < true
-- cannot compare booleans
-- {} < {1, 2}
-- cannot compare tables
-- nil .. 3
-- cannot concatenate nil
Generating errors when operands have unexpected types is known as strong typing; applying coercions is known as weak typing. While many languages try to employ strong-typing
everywhere and others favor weak typing nearly everywhere, Lua is quite happy mixing
things up.

2.3 FUNCTIONS
Lua functions can be named via function declarations or appear anonymously within function expressions. When called, the arguments are first fully evaluated and their values
assigned to the called functions’s parameters. If you pass too many arguments, the extras are ignored; pass too few and the extra parameters hold nil. Function values can be
freely passed as arguments and returned from functions. While there is nothing particularly special about this behavior, functions that accept functions as parameters or return
function values have a special name in programming language theory: they are known as
higher-order functions.
-- Function values can be declared or assigned
function odd(x) return x % 2 == 1 end
square = function (x) return x * x end
twice = function (f, x) return f(f(x)) end
-- We can call anonymous functions
assert((function (x) return x + 5 end)(10) == 15)
-- We can pass function values to other functions
assert(twice(square, -3) == 81)
assert(twice(function (x) return x + 1 end, 5) == 7)
-- We can create and return new functions on the fly
function compose(f, g)
return function (x) return f(g(x)) end
end
is_odd_when_squared = compose(odd, square)
assert(is_odd_when_squared(7))
assert(not is_odd_when_squared(0))
-- Too many arguments are fine, extras are ignored
assert((function (x) return x + 1 end)(8, 2) == 9)
-- Not passing enough arguments is fine, extras get nil
do (function (x, y) assert(y == nil) end)(1) end

Lua ■ 25
Parameters are always freshly allocated when a function is called. They are distinct variables
from the arguments, so changing a parameter will not affect its corresponding argument.
However, as Lua requires that for certain types, including tables, variables hold references
only, passing a table as an argument results in the parameter and argument holding a
reference to the same table. Therefore fields of a table argument may be changed through
the parameter:
x = {1, 2, 3}
y = {4, 5, 6}
function f(a, b)
a = 10
assert(x[1] == 1)
b[2] = 20
assert(y[2] == 20)
end

-- changing the parameter
-- ...does NOT affect the argument
-- but changing a field
-- ...DOES affect the argument

f(x, y)
We can better understand the effects of passing references through the visualization in
Figure 2.2. At the point of call (the left side of the figure), references to the tables are
passed into parameters a and b. The function updates the value of a (leaving x unchanged),
but updates a field of the shared table, leaving the effect visible to the argument y (on the
right side of the figure).
