Arguments passing and updating through parameters

When errors occur, whether from adding booleans, using nil as a function, or explicitly
calling the error function, they are propagated together with some optional diagnostic
information to the caller of the failing operation. If you wish to trap an error, make a
protected call. Invoking pcall(f,x,y) performs f(x,y) and returns two values: a success
indicator (true or false), and the proper result or error message. If an error is trapped,
the first returned value will be false.

26 ■ Programming Language Explorations
function add(x, y)
return x + y
end
success, result = pcall(add, 5, 3)
assert(success == true and result == 8)

-- Success! 5 + 3 = 8

success, result = pcall(add, 5, false)
assert(success == false)
-- Fail! Can't add booleans
assert(string.match(result, "arithmetic on a boolean"))

2.4

SCOPE

In language theory, the scope of a binding is the region of program text in which that
binding is active. Informally, a language’s scope rules tell us which entity a name refers to.
The rules are needed because a name can refer to different things in different places.
As mentioned earlier, Lua variables are global, local, or table fields. Variables are global
unless defined using local or inside a table. The reference manual tells us exactly how local
variables are scoped: “The scope of a local variable begins at the first statement after its
declaration and lasts until the last nonvoid statement of the innermost block that includes
the declaration.” [68] Blocks are the bodies of do, if, while, repeat, and for statements, as
well as function bodies. The use of a name prior to its declaration in a block (or within the
initializing expression of its declaration) will refer to a nonlocal (“outer”) entity currently
bound to the name, if any:
x = 1
do
assert(x == 1)
local x = x + 2
assert(x == 3)
end
assert(x == 1)

-- global x because local not yet seen
-- uses global x on right-hand side
-- now, FINALLY, we see the local x
-- back in the global scope!

The declaration of a local variable with the same name as an “outer” variable currently in
scope is known as a shadow.
Because a local variable is not in scope until after its declaration, the following attempt to
define a factorial function fails, because the helper function, f , is not recursive:
factorial = function (n)
local f = function (n, a)
return n==0 and a or f(n-1, a*n)
end
return f(n, 1)
end

-- WRONG !!!!
-- refers to GLOBAL f, crashes
-- because nil is not callable
-- calls local f

ok, reason = pcall(factorial, 10)
assert(ok == false)
print(reason)

-- hoping for 3628800
-- but it fails!
-- your output will vary

Lua ■ 27
This can be fixed by declaring a variable then assigning the function to the variable in a
subsequent statement:
local f
f = function (n, a) ... end
Because recursion is common, and the fix a little awkward, Lua provides an equivalent
sugared form:
local function f (n, a) ... end
Since the scope of a variable extends to the end of its block, it by definition encompasses
local functions following the declaration. This impacts the behavior of free variables—
variables used but not declared within a function. Consider:
x = 'MAIN';
function second() print(x) end
function first() local x = 'FIRST'; second() end
first()
The variable x within second is free. When printed, does x take on the value from its caller,
("FIRST") or from the enclosing source text ("MAIN")? Languages that use caller’s values
for free variables are dynamically scoped; those that look outward to textually enclosing
regions are statically scoped. Lua is statically scoped.

2.5 CLOSURES
The ability to nest functions inside other functions comes with interesting philosophical
questions but also enables new programming techniques. Consider this script:
function second(f)
local name = "new"
f()
-- how do we handle free variables in f?
end
function first()
local name = "old"
print_name = function () print(name) end
second(print_name)
end
first()
The function assigned to print_name in first has a free variable; it is passed to second
(as f ) and then executed, printing the value of name. When the function was created, it
sees name defined within first, but when called (as f ), might it see the name variable in
second? If a system binds the free variables of a passed function after passing, we speak of
shallow binding; if bound where the function is defined, we have deep binding.
What does Lua do? When a nested function with free variables is sent outside its environment, either by being passed to or returned from another function, the function carries the
bindings of those variables from the enclosing environment of its definition with it. These

28 ■ Programming Language Explorations
bindings “close over” the inner function (in our example, print_name), so the function, together with its bindings, is called a lexical closure, or closure for short. Lua thus exhibits
deep binding.
Closures can be used to make generators.2 A generator function produces a “next” value
each time it is called. A generator for a sequence of squares would produce 0 on its first
call, 1 on its second, then 4, 9, 16, and so on. Implementing a generator with a function
incrementing a global variable prior to returning its square would be insecure because other
parts of the code could change the global variable, disrupting future calls to the generator!
Fortunately, we can use the fact that a function’s local variables are invisible to code outside
the function:
next_square = (function ()
local previous = -1
return function ()
previous = previous + 1
return previous ^ 2
end
end)()
assert(next_square() == 0)
assert(next_square() == 1)
assert(next_square() == 4)
The value assigned to next_square is the result of calling an anonymous function. We call
the right-hand side of the assignment an immediately invoked function expression, or
IIFE. The call returns a closure. The variable that holds the number to be squared is local
to the enclosing function. The generator (next_square) can see this value, but no other
parts of the code can. The generator is secure.

2.6

TABLES

Lua provides a single structure that can be used to hold both named and numbered fields,
known as the table. While tables may frequently contain only numbered fields or only
named fields, field types can be mixed. To access table fields, place the field in square
brackets. If the field name is a literal string, Lua allows a convenient dot notation.
colors = { "red", "green", "blue" }
-- numbered only
circle = { x = 0, y = 0, radius = 10 }
-- named only
choices = { "kim", "sam", "maria", correct = 3 } -- mixed
assert(colors[2] == "green")
assert(circle["radius"] == 10)
assert(circle.radius == 10)
-- dot notation is convenient
assert(choices[1] == "kim")
assert(choices.correct == 3)
assert(choices[choices.correct] == "maria")
2 Generators are more commonly created with coroutines, but we’re using a low-level approach here simply
to illustrate closures in action.

Lua ■ 29
Here is a contrived example to show how complex tables are created and iterated over.
The functions pairs and ipairs produce iterators. Iterators are not tables, but rather
functions. You don’t call them directly; instead, you place them in your for-loop and let
them work their magic.
producer_key = "maufacturer"
widget = {
weight = 5.0,
["part number"] = "C8122-X",
"green",
"round",
[4] = "magnetic",
imported = false,
"metal",
[producer_key] = "Acme"
}

-- key is "weight"
-- key is "part number"
-- key is 1
-- key is 2
-- key is 4
-- key is "imported"
-- key is 3
-- key is "manufacturer"

print("pairs iterates through ALL pairs in arbitrary order")
for key, value in pairs(widget) do
print(key .. " => " .. tostring(value))
end
print("ipairs iterates integer-keyed pairs from 1 in order")
for key, value in ipairs(widget) do
print(key .. " => " .. tostring(value))
end
Fields can be specified in the form [key] = value or just value. The former evaluates the
expression key and uses the result as the field’s key. You can omit the brackets if the key
is a simple string (letters, digits, and underscores, not beginning with a digit). Unspecified
keys are auto-generated as the integers 1, 2, 3, and so on, as you likely surmised by reading
through several earlier examples in this chapter.
Keys needn’t be only strings and numbers—they just cannot be nil. Values can’t be nil
either, since nil is the response to requesting a value at a nonexistent key: a field value of
nil would be indistinguishable from the key not being present. This implies that to remove
an element from a table, set the value at the desired key to nil.
The length of a table (obtained via operator #) is roughly the number of contiguous integerkeyed fields starting at key 1. A table of only string-valued keys will have a length of 0;
to count the number of pairs in a table, a manual count is necessary. In other words, the
length operator makes sense only for tables that function as traditional lists.
As mentioned earlier, variables are not permitted to hold tables directly; they may only
hold references to tables. Therefore tables are shared when assigning, passing as arguments,
or being returned from functions. Sharing can be avoided by making copies of objects, but
note that simply copying each pair will lead only to a shallow copy if a pair’s value is
itself a reference! Performing a deep copy requires a recursive walkthrough of a structure.
The following script’s shallow copy is illustrated in Figure 2.3:

30 ■ Programming Language Explorations
a = {{x=0, y=0}, {x=8, y=21}, {x=3, y=5}}
b = a;

-- copies the reference: b and a share

c = {}
for key, value in pairs(a) do
c[key] = value
end
-- makes a shallow copy of a into c
b[1] = 100
assert(a[1] == 100)
c[2] = 200
assert(a[2].x == 8)
c[3].x = 300
assert(a[3].x == 300)

a

c

-- changed because copy was not deep

3

x 0

x 8

x 3

y 0

y 21

y 5

1

Figure 2.3

-- c[2] is a copy, so a is unaffected

2

1

b

-- demonstrate a and b are shared

2

3

A Shallow copy

Lua defines a pre-existing table called the global environment, containing:
• A string at index _VERSION
• Functions, including assert, pairs, ipairs, print, pcall, tonumber, tostring,
require among others
• Tables, including coroutine, io, debug, string, utf8, table, math, os, package,
among others
When you create your own global variables, they are added to this table.

2.7 METATABLES
Tables are the go-to structure for representing structured objects in any Lua program, so
we might try to use them to encode new data types. But how? The following attempt feels
like something is missing—the types don’t appear to be “real”:

Lua ■ 31
-- An unsatisfying way to represent datatypes
east = {type="vector", x=1, y=0}
west = {type="vector", x=-1, y=0}
me = {type="player", level=90}
world = {type="rectangle", height=5000, width=5000}
We would like direct language support for making our own datatypes. Lua provides the
metatable for just this purpose. By attaching a metatable to a single table, we can customize the way the table behaves. By attaching the same metatable to multiple tables, the
tables in effect become members of the same datatype. Let’s see how.
To attach metatable m to a table t, invoke setmetatable(t,m).3 Every metatable contains
zero or more of the following fields, which will define how t behaves:
__add __sub __mul __div __mod __pow __unm __idiv __band __bor __bxor
__bnot __shl __shr __concat __len __eq __lt __le __index __newindex
__call __mode __tostring __metatable __gc
When evaluating x+y, Lua first checks if x has a metatable m with an __add entry, and if
so, produces m.__add(x,y). If not, y is checked in a similar fashion. The next 18 properties
work in much the same way: __sub is used for subtraction, __unm for unary minus, __concat
for .., __len for # and so on.4 The __index entry is magical as well: its value is a table
of additional key-value fields for the tables attached to this metatable. That is, any table
attached to a metatable with an __index property inherits these fields. For example, here
are two circle objects, derived from a “prototype” unit circle:
unit_circle = {x = 0, y = 0, radius = 1, color = "black"}
c = {x = 4, color = "green"}
setmetatable(c, {__index = unit_circle})
assert(c.x == 4 and c.radius == 1)
As multiple circle objects sharing a metatable each inherit fields from a prototypal unit circle, we have the beginnings of a custom circle data type. Recall that types encapsulate both
state and behavior, so let’s now see how to build a complete “type” of two-dimensional vectors useful in graphics applications. We’ll use metatable features to make + and * represent
vector addition and vector dot product (operator overloading), and to make the global
tostring render vector descriptions. Figure 2.4 provides an illustration of the variables and
types from the script, where thick red arrows are used to show an object’s metatable):
Vector = (function (class, meta, prototype)
class.new = function (i, j)
return setmetatable({i = i, j = j}, meta)
end
prototype.magnitude = function (self)
return math.sqrt(self.i * self.i + self.j * self.j)
end
3 Actually, in Lua every value, not just every table, can have a metatable. However, setting the metatable
for nontables requires the C API, which is beyond the scope of our overview.
4 There are some restrictions here and there for some of the operator stand-ins; details are in the Lua
Reference Manual.

32 ■ Programming Language Explorations

meta.__index = prototype
meta.__add = function (self, v)
return class.new(self.i + v.i, self.j + v.j)
end
meta.__mul = function (self, v)
return self.i * v.i + self.j * v.j
end
meta.__tostring = function (self)
return string.format('<%g,%g>', self.i, self.j)
end
return class
end)({}, {}, {})
u = Vector.new(3, 4)
v = Vector.new(-5, 10)
assert(tostring(u) == "<3,4>")
assert(tostring(v) == "<-5,10>")
assert(u.j == 4)
assert(u:magnitude() == 5.0)
assert(tostring(u + v) == "<-2,14>")
assert(u * v == 25)
fun

Vector

new

i 3

u

fun

__index

j 4

v

Figure 2.4

magnitude

__add

fun

i -5

__mul

fun

j 10

__tostring

fun

Vectors in Lua

Each call to Vector.new produces a vector instance, namely a table with i and j components whose metatable is shared among all vector instances. The metatable contains

Lua ■ 33
operator and tostring implementations directly, and holds a reference to a separate table for custom vector behavior—in our case the single magnitude property. To invoke the
magnitude function on vector v we can write:
v.magnitude(v)
As the repetition of v is clunky, Lua allows:
v:magnitude()
as sugar for this expression, which is better because it evaluates v only once.

2.8 COROUTINES
Functions run until they terminate (normally or with via an error), then return to the
caller. A coroutine can do the same, but also yield, in which case it will be resumed where
it left off when next called. to create a coroutine, pass a function to coroutine.create.
Coroutines can implement on-demand sequences:
nextSquare = coroutine.create(function ()
for value = 1, 5 do
coroutine.yield(value * value)
end
return "Thank you"
end)
for i = 1, 8 do
local status = coroutine.status(nextSquare)
local success, values = coroutine.resume(nextSquare)
print(status, success, values)
end
A call of coroutine.resume returns a success flag (whether the coroutine has yielded or
returned without error) followed by the values that were yielded or returned. The script
above produces:
suspended
suspended
suspended
suspended
suspended
suspended
dead
dead

true
true
true
true
true
true
false
false

1
4
9
16
25
Thank you
cannot resume dead coroutine
cannot resume dead coroutine

A coroutine’s status is one of:
• suspended, if it has not yet started or has yielded and is waiting to be resumed,
• running, if it’s running (and is the caller to status)

• normal, if it has resumed another coroutine and is waiting to be resumed itself, and
• dead, if it has terminated via a return or unprotected error.

34 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Rather than explicit calls to coroutine.resume, you will often find coroutines created with
coroutine.wrap, which returns a function that, when called, resumes the coroutine. You
won’t have direct access to the coroutine in this case; instead, you will a slightly simpler
interface where the function invocation produces the next yielded value.
nextSquare = coroutine.wrap(function ()
for value = 1, 5 do
coroutine.yield(value * value)
end
return "Thank you"
end)
for i = 1, 8 do
local success, value = pcall(nextSquare)
print(success, value)
end
Coroutines allow us to structure an application into concurrent activities, each written independently of each other. A game might contain coroutines for players, volcanos, tornados,
waterfalls, and an event manager to listen for user input. The program runs on a single
thread of execution: each coroutine must contain explicit yield and resume calls, and not
execute too long between these calls. This architecture is known as cooperative multitasking, and differs from the alternative preemptive multitasking, where the operating
system can preempt a thread at any time and give control to another thread.
As Lua is designed to be lightweight and eﬀicient, the language does not provide direct
support for preemptive multitasking. All coroutines interleave their execution on a single operating-system managed thread. Managing multiple “real” threads requires some
overhead, which Lua prefers to avoid. One of Lua’s targeted application areas is interactive gaming, where coroutines spend a fair amount of time waiting for user and system
interaction—application areas in which sharing coroutines on a single thread is more performant than having the operating system juggle threads. We’ll have more to say in future
chapters, where we’ll encounter languages that allow both preemptive and cooperative multitasking.

LUA WRAP UP
In this chapter, we were introduced to Lua. We learned that:
• Lua is a relatively small language, with only a few statements, operators, and types—
and only one data structure.
• Variables store nil, booleans, numbers, and strings directly; for all other types, only
references are stored.
• Both strong and weak typing are evident: anything can be coerced to booleans (false
and nil to false, everything else to true), and strings and numbers are somewhat
interchangeable.
• Lua functions may both accept multiple values and return multiple values. The multiple return value capability is used prominently in the pcall function, which is Lua’s
preferred mechanism for error checking and handling.

Lua ■ 35
• Lua variables come in three flavors: (1) global, (2) local, and (3) table fields. Local
variables are introduced with the keyword local. Their scope begins on the statement
after their declaration and runs until the end of the block. While this rule is simple,
it does mean that local functions can only be made recursive either by declaring the
function prior to assignment or using the sugared form local function.
• Lua uses static scoping and dynamic binding.

• Tables are used for both classic arrays (1-based sequences of numerically indexed values) and dictionaries (sets of key-value pairs). Both numeric and nonnumeric indexed
values can be mixed in a single table; it is up to the language implementation to keep
the “array-part” access eﬀicient.
• Lua’s standard library is actually a collection of tables (e.g., io, os, math, etc.) These
tables, along with predefined functions and variables, are stored in a special table
called the global environment.
• A metatable can be attached to a table. The metatable can customize the manner in
which table fields are read and written, and redefine the meaning of nineteen operators,
including those for arithmetic, comparison, and logic.
• Metatables can also be used to build a prototypal inheritance mechanism, effectively
adding new user-defined data types. This is done by assigning a table of the shared
properties and methods to the __index property of the metatable.
• Lua directly supports coroutines: calling coroutine.create on a function object produces a coroutine object of type thread. Coroutines can yield and be resumed, and
their status can be queried.
For further study in Lua, including language features not covered in this chapter as well as
links to articles, tutorials, oﬀicial documentation, and open-source projects using Lua, see
the Lua page in the book’s companion GitHub repository at https://github.com/rtoal/
ple/tree/main/lua.

numbers in a certain range. Arithmetic on integers may result in values outside of
the specified range, in which case Lua finds a suitable value within this range as the
result. On one machine, for example, evaluating 2000000000000 * 2000000000000
produced the value 8015056820827586560, while evaluating 2E12 * 2E12 produced

it did.

2.24 True or false: A function is just a coroutine that doesn’t call yield. Explain.

into its constituent values. Write another coroutine to add the player to the table of
players appearing in 15 games or more. The first coroutine should be able to send a
signal to the second to tell it there are no more lines to be read. When the second
coroutine has added each player, it can call a (noncoroutine) function to sort, and
output the result table.
2.26 Lua is often used in applications that are partially written in C (or C++) and partially
in Lua. Find an online tutorial or reference on embedding Lua in C++ applications.
Write a small example program showing how a Lua function can be called from C++,
and a C++ function called from Lua.

CHAPTER

3

JavaScript

JavaScript is, by some measures, the most popular programming language in the world, powering browser-based applications, high-performance servers, and mobile applications.
First appeared 1995
Creator Brendan Eich
Recognized for First-class functions, Weak typing, Prototypes
Notable uses Web application clients, Asynchronous servers
Tags Imperative, Functional, Dynamic, Prototypal, Event-Driven
Six words or less Not just for the web anymore
JavaScript was designed and implemented in ten days in 1995 by Brendan Eich, as an
amateur-friendly scripting language embedded into a web browser. The rapid production
and novice programmer focus led to some clean, expressive syntax, but also to some notorious features, including weak typing, where expressions of the wrong type are automatically coerced to “something that works,” strings built from 16-bit character codes, and a
typeof operator that behaves oddly. Douglas Crockford [17] has cataloged these and a few
of JavaScript’s other “Bad Parts” and “Awful Parts,” while at the same time praising it
for “[having] some extraordinarily good parts. In JavaScript, there is a beautiful, elegant,
highly expressive language that is buried under a steaming pile of good intentions and
blunders.” [17, p. 2]
In the three decades since, the language has grown extremely powerful and has been used
in thousands of successful, sophisticated applications. Every major web browser comes with
a JavaScript engine, and many products convert JavaScript web applications into native
applications running on iOS, Android, and other mobile platforms. Beyond the browser
and mobile devices, the language powers server-based applications supporting thousands of
concurrent users, making JavaScript very close to a “run anywhere” language.
This chapter will have much to say about this large and sophisticated language. We’ll
see how higher-order functions, pattern-matching, and generators qualify JavaScript as a
modern language. We’ll show how prototypes and their sugared class-syntax can create userdefined datatypes, and explore the omnipresent this expression. We’ll cover asynchronous
programming in some detail, as it may be JavaScript’s most signature feature.
39

40 ■ Programming Language Explorations

3.1

HELLO JAVASCRIPT

Let’s get a feel for JavaScript with three teaser programs, the first of which prints some
lucky numbers:
for (let c = 1; c <= 40; c++) {
for (let b = 1; b < c; b++) {
for (let a = 1; a < b; a++) {
if (a * a + b * b === c * c) {
console.log(`${a}, ${b}, ${c}`)
}
}
}
}
To iterate through a range of numbers, we employ a for-statement with three clauses in
its header: the first executes before the loop begins, the second tells us what must hold
to keep going, and the final clause runs at the end of each loop iteration. The spelling of
the equality operator is somewhat unique: x === y is true if and only if x and y have the
same value and the same type. We don’t use the == operator, as it only computes whether
two objects are similar to, rather than equal to, each other, and can behave unexpectedly
(e.g., [] == ![] and false == " \t "). Within backquote-delimited strings, the construct
${e} interpolates the stringified value of expression e into the string.
Our second teaser writes the permutations of its command line arguments to standard
output. Access to the command line arguments is provided through the array process.argv.
The first two elements (at positions 0 and 1) of this array are reserved for the program runner
and the name of the program;1 the “real” arguments therefore come from slicing this array
beginning at index 2.
function printPermutations(a, n) {
if (n === 0) {
console.log(a.join("\t"))
} else {
for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
printPermutations(a, n - 1)
const j = n % 2 === 0 ? 0 : i
;[a[j], a[n]] = [a[n], a[j]]
}
printPermutations(a, n - 1)
}
}
const args = process.argv.slice(2)
printPermutations(args, args.length - 1)
This example illustrates combining array elements into a string with join, assignment
via pattern matching, and the conditional operator (?:). Also snuck into the example is
1 A typical invocation for this script is node permutations.js I like carrots, in which we see
process.argv === ["node", "permutations.js", "I", "like", "carrots"].

JavaScript ■ 41
an artifact of automatic semicolon insertion. JavaScript requires most statements to
be terminated with a semicolon unless ending with a right brace (}); however, you need
not include them. JavaScript will be able to insert some, but not all, of them for you.
In particular, it does not insert a semicolon between the declaration of j and the array
swap, since it leaves open the possibility that we actually meant to write the array indexing
expression i[a[j], a[n]]], which while legal, is not at all what we want. We must include
an explicit semicolon.
Our third teaser is the JavaScript version of the “data science” program introduced in the
previous chapter on page 17:
import readline from "readline/promises"
const reader = readline.createInterface(process.stdin)
const players = []
for await (const line of reader) {
let [team, name, games, points] = line.split(",")
if (games >= 15) {
players.push({ name, team, ppg: points / games })
}
}
const topTen = players.sort((p1, p2) => p2.ppg - p1.ppg).slice(0, 10)
for (let { name, team, ppg } of topTen) {
console.log(
`${name.padEnd(22)}${team.padEnd(4)}${ppg.toFixed(2).padStart(8)}`
)
}
The script instantiates a line reader object tied to standard input. Readers can be iterated with the for await statement to produce each line of the file. The keyword await is
used because lines are read asynchronously, delivered to our program only when ready.
We’ll have much to say later in this chapter about JavaScript’s support for asynchronous
programming, which is likely one of its most recognizable features.

3.2

THE BASICS

JavaScript programs consist of scripts and modules, each containing a sequence of statements and function declarations. Statements include variable declarations, assignments,
function calls, conditionals, loops, breaks, continues, returns, and a few others. The language has exactly 8 types:
• The type containing the sole value undefined.
• The type containing the sole value null.

• Boolean, the type containing the two values true and false.

• Number, the type containing all values from the IEEE 754 binary64 specification, [51]
including −98.88, 22.7 × 10100 , Infinity, -Infinity, and the infamous NaN, the
number meaning “not a number.” Whole numbers can be written in binary notation,
e.g., 0b01101001, and in hexadecimal, e.g., 0x1a4.

42 ■ Programming Language Explorations
• BigInt, the type of integers whose size is bounded only by the amount of memory in
the runtime system, e.g., 9812319182192315125312712753812919102193231288882n.
• String, roughly, the type of character sequences. String literals are delimited by either
single quotes, double quotes, or backquotes, with the latter allowed to span lines and
contain interpolated expressions.
• Symbol, the type of symbols (not covered in this chapter).

• Object, the type of all other values, including arrays and functions. Objects have
named properties each holding a value, for example {x: 3, y: 5}. Properties of an
array include 0, 1, 2, and so on, and length.
Variables and object properties hold values of nonobject types directly, but only hold references to objects, so the expression a === b where a and b are objects compares two
references and not object properties.
You’ll find the usual arithmetic operators for negation (-), addition (+), subtraction (-),
multiplication (*), division (/), and exponentiation (**). The Math object provides dozens
of more functions. Interestingly, the bitwise (&, |, ~) and shift operators (<<, >>, >>>) can be
applied to noninteger numbers, when doing so, the operand is coerced (somehow) to an integer in the range −2147483648 to 2147483647. Many of the numeric operators are extended
to instances of BigInt, though regular numbers and BigInts cannot be mixed:
import assert from "assert/strict"
assert(3 + 2 ** (3 ** 2) - 10 === 505)
assert(13 / 5 === 2.6)
assert(Math.floor(13 / 5) === 2)
assert(Math.hypot(3, -4) === 5)
assert(Math.random() < 1.0)
assert((237462938920 | 0) === 1239737640)
assert(3n + 5n === 8n)
assert.throws(() => 3 + 5n, TypeError)
Strings can be uppercased, lowercased, split, spliced, concatenated, padded, trimmed, and
searched. Strings internally use an obscure encoding known as UTF-16 [125], yielding
strange results for the length method. The two-character string consisting of SUSHI and
QUARTER NOTE has a “length” of three, despite being made up of two Unicode characters and having a length of 7 bytes when UTF-8 encoded! Obtain code points or UTF-8
encoding by converting the string into an array or buffer, respectively:
import assert from "assert/strict"
const s = "\u{1f363}\u{2669}" // SUSHI, QUARTER NOTE
assert(s.length === 3) // based on internal storage units
assert(Array.from(s).length === 2) // ok, 2 code points
assert(Buffer.from(s, "utf8").length === 7) // ok, 7 bytes utf-8
Strings natively support Unicode normalization to convert between different code point
sequences that are semantically equivalent. For example, the sequence U+0065 latin small
letter e U+0303 combining diaeresis represents the same “text” as the single code point

JavaScript ■ 43
U+00EB latin small letter e with diaeresis (ë). Combining many to fewer characters is called a canonical composition (denoted NFC in Unicode); breaking down few
into many is a canonical decomposition (NFD). Normalization also applies between
code point sequences that, while different, should behave the same in search. For example,
U+01F2 latin small ligature ff (ff) has a compatibility decomposition (NFKD) into
the two-character sequence U+0066 latin small letter f U+0066 latin small letter
f. Similarly, the single character U+01C4 latin capital letter dz with caron decomposes via NFKD to (in order) D (U+0044), Z (U+005A), and the combining caron (U+030C).
Compatibility compositions (NFKC) apply a canonical composition after a compatibility
decomposition, so DŽ normalizes via NFKC to D and Ž. Let’s explore how normalizations
are expressed in JavaScript:
import assert from "assert/strict"
// Canonical composition and decomposition
const zWithDotAbove = "\u{017c}"
const zThenCombiningDotAbove = "z\u{0307}"
assert(zWithDotAbove !== zThenCombiningDotAbove)
assert(zWithDotAbove.normalize("NFD") === zThenCombiningDotAbove)
assert(zThenCombiningDotAbove.normalize("NFC") === zWithDotAbove)
// Compatibility decomposition of ligatures and archaic letters
const ffiLigature = "\u{fb03}"
assert(ffiLigature !== "ffi")
assert(ffiLigature.normalize("NFKD") === "ffi")
const longS = "\u{017f}"
assert(longS !== "s")
assert(longS.normalize("NFKD") === "s")
// NKFC is actually two steps: compatibility decomposition
// followed by canonical composition
const dzWithCaron = "\u{01c4}"
const dThenZThenCaron = "DZ\u{030c}"
const dThenZCaron = "D\u{017d}"
assert(dzWithCaron !== dThenZThenCaron)
assert(dzWithCaron.normalize("NFKD") === dThenZThenCaron)
assert(dThenZThenCaron.normalize("NFKC") === dThenZCaron)
Use arrays for positionally-indexed structures and objects when properties are named. Both
arrays and objects can be spread into other arrays and objects:
import assert from "assert/strict"
const weekdays = ["Mo", "Tu", "We", "Th", "Fr"]
const days = ["Su", ...weekdays, "Sa"]
assert.equal(days.join(""), "SuMoTuWeThFrSa")
const center = { x: 3, y: 5 }
const circle = { ...center, radius: 13, color: "blue" }
assert.deepEqual(circle, { radius: 13, x: 3, y: 5, color: "blue" })

44 ■ Programming Language Explorations
The for-of statement iterates array elements and object properties:
import assert from "assert/strict"
const colors = ["red", "green", "blue"]
const tiles = { e: 1, q: 10, k: 5 }
let scratchPad = ""
for (let color of colors) {
scratchPad += color
}
for (let [character, score] of Object.entries(tiles)) {
scratchPad += character + score
}
assert(scratchPad === "redgreenbluee1q10k5")
The value undefined is used when something is unknown, or known but irrelevant. Use
null to be explicit about the nonexistence of something:
// This means my supervisor is Tina:
let supervisor = "Tina"
// This means I absolutely do not have a supervisor:
supervisor = null
// This means I might have a supervisor but know nothing
// about them, or I may or may not have a supervisor, or
// that I don't even know that I have one, or I have one
// but it is none of your business who it is:
supervisor = undefined
We call undefined and null the nullish values; they interest us because as representations
of a lack of information, they cannot have properties. Accessing properties of null and
undefined throws a TypeError:
import assert from "assert/strict"
assert.throws(() => null.toString(), TypeError)
assert.throws(() => undefined.toString(), TypeError)
In practice, nullish values often appear assigned to variables whose data is optionally present,
as opposed to being required. For example, we may be interested in the population of
the capital of a winner’s country. But we may not have a winner, the winner may not
be associated with any country, the database may not hold a country’s capital, or the
population may be unknown. We avoid type errors via optional chaining:
winner?.country?.capital?.population
where o?.x produces the x property of o if o exists and undefined if o is nullish. This makes
sense: if we don’t know the winner, we don’t know the winner’s home country.
Interestingly, variables and properties, though they both hold values, differ in one respect.
Referencing an undeclared variable throws a ReferenceError, while referencing a missing
property produces undefined:

JavaScript ■ 45
import assert from "assert/strict"
const p = { x: 3, y: 5 }
assert(p.z === undefined) // There's no p.z
assert.throws(() => z, ReferenceError) // No variable z
The JavaScript runtime comes with a global object containing dozens of properties including isFinite, String, Object, and Date, as well as a property that references the global
object itself, called globalThis. Properties of the global object are exactly the global
variables of the language.
JavaScript gives us a few options for specifying function values, including the arrow (=>),
the construct function(params){body}, and the standard function declaration. Functions
that accept functions as parameters or return functions are called higher-order functions.
Higher-order functions facilitate a style of coding—called functional programming—in
which function composition replaces assignment statements and explicit loops.
import assert from "assert/strict"
// Three ways to introduce a function value
function square(x) {
return x * x
}
const odd = x => Math.abs(x % 2) === 1
const lessThanTen = function (x) {
return x < 10
}
// Essentially: { let x = 10; x + 5 }
assert((x => x + 5)(10) === 15)
// We can pass function values to other functions
const twice = (f, x) => f(f(x))
assert(twice(square, -3) === 81)
assert(twice(x => x + 1, 5) === 7)
// We can create and return new functions on the fly
const compose = (f, g) => x => f(g(x))
const isOddWhenSquared = compose(odd, square)
assert(isOddWhenSquared(7))
assert(!isOddWhenSquared(0))
// Array functions often take the place of loops
const a = [9, 7, 4, -1, 8]
assert(!a.every(odd))
assert(a.some(odd))
assert(a.every(lessThanTen))
assert.deepEqual(a.filter(odd), [9, 7, -1])
assert.deepEqual(a.map(square), [81, 49, 16, 1, 64])

46 ■ Programming Language Explorations
In JavaScript, arguments are fully evaluated before the call and their values are assigned
(copied) to the parameters left-to-right. If you pass too many arguments, the extras are
ignored; pass too few and the extra parameters begin as undefined. You can, however,
mark your final parameter with ... to pack extra arguments into an array; this is known
as a rest parameter. You may also use ... on the argument side, where it is called the
spread operator, to unpack an array into multiple parameters:
import assert from "assert/strict"
const f = (x, y) => [x, y]
const g = (x, ...y) => [x, y]
assert.deepEqual(f(1), [1, undefined]) // too few args
assert.deepEqual(f(1, 2, 3), [1, 2]) // too many args
assert.deepEqual(g(1, 2, 3), [1, [2, 3]]) // args packed
const args = [1, 2]
assert.deepEqual(f(...args), [1, 2]) // args unpacked
Missing arguments can take on defaults other than undefined:
import assert from "assert/strict"
function f(a, b = 3, c, d = 8) {
return [a, b, c, d]
}
assert.deepEqual(f(1, 2), [1, 2, undefined, 8])
JavaScript favors weak typing, because more often than not, values of one type can appear
where a value of another type is expected. For example, almost anything can be coerced
to a boolean, number, or string:
• In if and while statements expecting a boolean condition, any value can appear. 0,
null, undefined, false, NaN, and the empty string act as false and are called falsy;
all other values act as true and are called truthy.
• When a string is expected, undefined acts as "undefined", null acts as "null",
false acts as "false", 3 acts as "3", and so on. To use an object x in a string
context, JavaScript evaluates x.toString().
• When a number is expected, undefined acts as NaN, null as 0, false as 0, true as 1,
and strings act as the number they “look like” or NaN. To use an object x in a numeric
context, JavaScript evaluates x.valueOf().
Table 3.1 provides concrete examples.
While coercions predominate, some elements of strong typing do exist: TypeErrors do occur
when using a symbol as a number, when using null or undefined as an object, when mixing
numbers and bigints, or when attempting to call a nonfunction.

3.3 DESTRUCTURING
How do names receive values in a program? For many novice programmers, the answer “Assignment statements!” may be the first thing to pop into their heads. With a little thought,
passing arguments to parameters in function calls may come up, too. But assignment and
parameter passing are simply specific instances of the generalized concept of binding, where
variables take on values based on their appearance in some matching expression—you may
have noticed that for-of statements introduce bindings as well. How might this look in
general? Here’s a hypothetical construct:
{x:1, y: [z, "hello"]} <=> {y: [2, q], x: p}
The idea is to match up a variable (on either side) with the corresponding value on the
other side. Our example would bind 1 to p (look at the values of the x keys on both sides),
2 to z (first element of the y-arrays), and "hello" to q. JavaScript doesn’t go quite so far
as binding variables on both sides, but it does allow an expressive pattern on the left.
The entire right-hand side is evaluated first (as is the case in classic assignment), then the
variables on the left become bound. Some examples follow:
import assert from "assert/strict"
let [x, y] = [1, 3]
let { a, b: z, c } = { a: 5, b: 8, c: 13 }
// Note the shorthand property syntax: {a} means {a:a}
assert.deepEqual([x, y, a, z, c], [1, 3, 5, 8, 13])
This pattern-matching mechanism is called destructuring assignment because the runtime breaks up (destructures) the pattern to find the variables to be bound.
Destructuring is particularly powerful and useful in argument passing! Using an object
pattern as a sole parameter gives you the effect of indexargument labelargument labels

48 ■ Programming Language Explorations
at the point of call, which not only increases readability but gives you some flexibility in
ordering the arguments:
function drawLine({ from: p, to: q, withStyle: style }) {
// Code using p, q, and style
}
drawLine({ from: [3, 5], to: [8, -13], withStyle: "dashed" })
drawLine({ to: [3, 55], from: [8, 1], withStyle: "dotted" })
drawLine({ withStyle: "solid", from: [1, 2], to: [8, 21] })

3.4 GENERATORS
JavaScript’s treatment of free variables and lexical closures is similar to Lua’s, which we
discussed in the previous chapter. Static scoping and deep binding are the law of the land.
So we could, in principle, implement secure objects to generate values on demand using
closures, as we did in Lua on page 28.
But just as in Lua, it is more common to use a higher-level structure for on-demand sequence
generation. A JavaScript generator is a function built from a function*, that suspends
execution via a yield expression and resumes where it left off when called again. Here’s the
JavaScript version of the first-five-squares generator we previously built in Lua on page 33.
Observe the three ways to use JavaScript generators:
import assert from "assert/strict"
function* firstFiveSquares() {
for (let i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {
yield i ** 2
}
}
// Three ways to use a generator
// 1. Obtain the values on demand
const g = firstFiveSquares()
assert.deepEqual(g.next(), { value: 1, done: false })
assert.deepEqual(g.next(), { value: 4, done: false })
assert.deepEqual(g.next(), { value: 9, done: false })
assert.deepEqual(g.next(), { value: 16, done: false })
assert.deepEqual(g.next(), { value: 25, done: false })
assert.deepEqual(g.next(), { value: undefined, done: true })
// 2. Use for-of to iterate through generated values
for (let s of firstFiveSquares()) {
console.log(s)
}

JavaScript ■ 49
// 3. Roll up generated values all at once
assert.deepEqual([...firstFiveSquares()], [1, 4, 9, 16, 25])

3.5 METHODS
An object can have properties whose values are functions:
const circle = {
radius: 10,
area: function () {
return Math.PI * this.radius * this.radius
},
circumference: function () {
return 2 * Math.PI * this.radius
},
expand: function (scale) {
this.radius *= scale
},
}
When we call a function via property access notation (e.g., circle.area()), we say the
function is a method and the object is the receiver. Using the long syntax for function
values (function ( params ) { body }) enables the special expression this to refer to the
receiver. Functions defined with ( params ) => { body }, or arrow functions, do not get
a special this. Because the use of long-form, or nonarrow, function values is so common
within JavaScript object literals, a shorthand notation is available:
const circle = {
radius: 10,
area() {
return Math.PI * this.radius * this.radius
},
circumference() {
return 2 * Math.PI * this.radius
},
expand(scale) {
this.radius *= scale
},
}

3.6 PROTOTYPES
We’ve just seen how to create one circle object. To create several circle objects in JavaScript,
we start with an initial object then derive additional objects from it. These new objects
have the original object as their prototype. Prototypes allow us to inherit properties:
when we encounter the expression q.x, we look for an x property in q. If found, we have our
value; if not, we look for the property in q’s prototype, and if necessary, in the prototype’s
prototype, and so on, until we find the property or reach the end of the “prototype chain.”
If no object on the chain has the property, the lookup produces undefined.

50 ■ Programming Language Explorations
import assert from "assert/strict"
const unitCircle = {
x: 0,
y: 0,
radius: 1,
color: "black",
area() {
return Math.PI * this.radius * this.radius
},
circumference() {
return 2 * Math.PI * this.radius
},
}
const c1 = Object.create(unitCircle)
c1.x = 3
c1.color = "green"
const c2 = Object.create(unitCircle)
c2.radius = 5
const c3 = Object.create(unitCircle)
assert.deepEqual([c1.x, c1.y, c1.radius, c1.color], [3, 0, 1, "green"])
assert.deepEqual([c2.x, c2.y, c2.radius, c2.color], [0, 0, 5, "black"])
assert.deepEqual([c3.x, c3.y, c3.radius, c3.color], [0, 0, 1, "black"])
assert(c2.area() === 25 * Math.PI)
assert(c3.area() === Math.PI)
The expression Object.create(unitCircle) creates a new object whose prototype is
unitCircle. Our script creates a black circle of radius 1, centered at the origin, as the
prototype of three other circles. JavaScript’s delegation of property lookup conveniently
gives each circle four properties, without the need to store all four properties in each object.
In the object referenced by c1, x and color are called own properties, while y and radius
are called inherited properties.
Note that in each of the newly created objects, we store only those properties whose values
differ from those in the prototypal circle. Thus we can have each circle inherit all of the
methods from the prototype: since every circle computes its area and circumference the
same way, it would be wasteful to store copies of these functions in each circle.
Figure 3.1 shows the four circles and the prototype links to unitCircle. For simplicity,
our diagram omits the prototype links from unitCircle to its prototype, and from the two
functions to their prototype.
In practice, programmers will want a function to construct each instance of a family of
objects that each share a prototype. Interestingly, JavaScript provides a mechanism to do
just that. We’ll illustrate the technique with an example, and then discuss:

JavaScript ■ 51

Figure 3.1

function

Objects sharing a prototype

import assert from "assert/strict"
function Circle(centerX = 0, centerY = 0, radius = 1, color = "black") {
this.x = centerX
this.y = centerY
this.radius = radius
this.color = color
}
Circle.prototype.area = function () {
return Math.PI * this.radius * this.radius
}
Circle.prototype.circumference = function () {
return 2 * Math.PI * this.radius
}
const c = new Circle(1, 5)
assert(c.x === 1 && c.y === 5 && c.radius === 1 && c.color === "black")
assert(c.area() === Math.PI)
assert(c.circumference() === 2 * Math.PI)
assert(Object.getPrototypeOf(c) === Circle.prototype)
assert(c.constructor) === Circle
assert(typeof c === "object")
This simple looking script illustrates a lot of JavaScript magic. Every JavaScript function
has three properties, name, length (the number of parameters), and prototype, the object
that will be assigned as the prototype of all objects created by calling the function with the
operator new. It’s primed for you with a constructor property, referencing the function.
This allows you to determine the function that created an object as a kind of run time
“type check,” since, as the last line of our example shows, Circle isn’t a JavaScript type.
Figure 3.2 illustrates the relationship between these objects.

52 ■ Programming Language Explorations
function

Construction of Objects using new

The declaration let c = new Circle(1, 5); creates a new object with prototype Circle.prototype, passes it to the function Circle as this, and binds the now-initialized
object to the variable c. The area and circumference methods are stored as properties in
the prototype, just as in our earlier example.
Creating a constructor function to build instances of a user-defined type, and loading up
the shared state and behavior into a common prototype occurs so often in JavaScript that
there is a shorthand syntax for this pattern:
import assert from "assert/strict"
class Circle {
constructor(centerX = 0, centerY = 0, radius = 1, color = "black") {
this.x = centerX
this.y = centerY
this.radius = radius
this.color = color
}
area() {
return Math.PI * this.radius * this.radius
}
circumference() {
return 2 * Math.PI * this.radius
}
}

JavaScript ■ 53
const c = new Circle(1, 5)
assert(c.circumference() === 2 * Math.PI)
assert(typeof Circle === "function")
The last line of the script above shows us that the class keyword does not create a “class
object”—Circle is still a function—and the “typecheck” c.constructor === Circle still
holds. The class construct is syntactic sugar—a syntax that makes the standard form
easier to read. It does no more and no less than defining the function and assigning methods
to the prototype.
The class syntax makes it convenient to express IS-A relationships, which we can illustrate
with some friendly animals. All animals have a name, and when asked to speak, they say
their name and make a species-specific sound. Animals come in three kinds: cows, horses,
and sheep:
import assert from "assert/strict"
class Animal {
constructor(name) {
this.name = name
}
speak() {
return `${this.name} says ${this.sound()}`
}
}
class Cow extends Animal {
sound() {
return "moooo"
}
}
class Horse extends Animal {
sound() {
return "neigh"
}
}
class Sheep extends Animal {
sound() {
return "baaaa"
}
}
const h = new Horse("CJ")
assert(h.speak() === "CJ says neigh")
const c = new Cow("Bessie")
assert(c.speak() === "Bessie says moooo")
assert(new Sheep("Little Lamb").speak() === "Little Lamb says baaaa")

54 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Read the extends keyword as “is a”—that Horse extends Animal means a horse is an
animal. Since an animal can speak, so can a horse. The internal workings of the class
extension is quite elegant: Animal.prototype is simply made the prototype of Horse.prototype (see Figure 3.3), so horses naturally speak.

Implementation of IS-A in JavaScript

Properties whose names begin with a # are not visible outside the class, and are often used
to hide an object’s internal representation, and define methods that should not be part of a
public interface. In the following example, queue objects can only be added to and removed
from with the add and remove methods; no outside code can maliciously or accidentally
modify the private #data array directly, as doing so would violate the first-in-first-out
protocol expected of a queue.
class Queue {
#data
#validateNotEmpty() {
if (this.size <= 0) throw new Error("Queue empty")
}
constructor() {
this.#data = []
}
add(item) {
this.#data.push(item)
}
remove() {
this.#validateNotEmpty()
return this.#data.shift()
}
peek() {
this.#validateNotEmpty()

JavaScript ■ 55

}

3.7

return this.#data[0]
}
get size() {
return this.#data.length
}

SCOPE

JavaScript has a lot of scope rules, influenced by whether variables are truly global (properties of the global object), defined at the top level of a module, local to a function or block,
or declared with let, const, or the obsolete var. We don’t have the space to cover every
rule, but we do wish to highlight a significant difference from Lua. Scopes do not begin
at the point of declaration; rather, bindings introduced with var are scoped to the entire
innermost function, and bindings introduced with let or const are scoped to the entire
innermost enclosing block.
With such wide scopes, you may ask: what if one tries to use a variable in its scope but prior
to its declaration—in its so-called temporal dead zone (TDZ)? The following exploration
illustrates that the TDZ rules differ between var and let/const:
import assert from "assert/strict"
const [a, b] = [1, 2]
;(function () {
assert(a === undefined) // the local `a` is in scope
assert(b === 2) // we see the outer `b`
if (true) {
var a = 100 // scoped to whole function!
let b = 200 // scoped only inside this block
const c = 300 // scoped only inside this block
}
assert(a === 100) // it's been initialized
assert(b === 2) // outer, because local used `let`
assert.throws(() => c) // there's no `c` out here at all
})()
We see that temporal dead zone access for var produces undefined and for let and const
a ReferenceError is thrown.2

3.8

PROMISES

For programs to run eﬀiciently, they should not stop and wait for users to click buttons,
move cursors, or enter keyboard data. They should not stop and wait for file and network
accesses to complete. How can a language guarantee programs never wait idly, and stay
running while a user ponders their next action or a network request has yet to be answered?
That is, how can we specify that computation is asynchronous?
2 Since the introduction of let and const in 2015, there is no reason to ever use the var keyword, but it
is quite prominent in old JavaScript code and remains an oﬀicial part of the language.

56 ■ Programming Language Explorations
In an asynchronous architecture, code responds to events such as button clicks, cursor
movements, or data becoming ready from a file. When fired, an event is added to the
event queue. When an event is eventually pulled from the queue, it is passed to any
event handler function that has been registered with the type of the event. In a web
browser, handlers are registered on document objects with the addEventListener method.
The following web document contains a little canvas you can sketch in (run it by giving this
file an .html extension and simply opening the file in a browser):
<canvas width="500" height="500"></canvas>
<script type="module">
const canvas = document.querySelector("canvas")
const ctx = canvas.getContext("2d")
let drawing = false
canvas.style.border = "2px solid purple"
canvas.addEventListener("mousedown", e => {
drawing = true
ctx.moveTo(e.offsetX, e.offsetY)
})
canvas.addEventListener("mousemove", e => {
if (drawing) {
ctx.lineTo(e.offsetX, e.offsetY)
ctx.stroke()
}
})
const stopDrawing = e => (drawing = false)
canvas.addEventListener("mouseup", stopDrawing)
canvas.addEventListener("mouseout", stopDrawing)
</script>
Mouse event objects hold the cursor position in the offsetX and offsetY properties; touch
events for phones and tablets work in a similar fashion. Each of the handler functions is run
to completion, one after the other. That is, all the JavaScript handlers “run on the same
thread” though they may run in parallel with any operating system-level operations.
In practice, we do not always register events explicitly—many functions only “kick off”
a single long-running operation and immediately return a promise object. Generally, we
attach success and failure callbacks to the promise object to run after the long-running
operation completes. Node’s standard filesystem module (fs/promises) has many functions
that work this way. For a trivial example, let’s print the contents of a file:
import { readFile } from "fs/promises"
const promise = readFile("/etc/profile", "utf8")
promise.then(
data => {
console.log(data)
console.log("This is printed only AFTER the file contents")
},
error => console.error(`${error}`)

JavaScript ■ 57
)
console.log("This is always printed BEFORE the file contents")
The function readFile kicks off the reading operation and does not wait for the read to
complete. Though the code following the call and the actual reading take place concurrently,
the code following the call will run to completion before the JavaScript engine examines the
event queue. Therefore, the callbacks do not run until the file has been read and the currently
executing handler has finished. The example script emphasizes this important JavaScript
behavior with its “printed before” message.
A promise object begins life in the pending state and eventually settles to either the fulfilled
or rejected state. Invoking p.then(f,g) registers f to run after promise p enters the fulfilled
state and g after it enters the rejected state. The invocation p.catch(g) registers an error
callback only. Both then and catch return promises, so asynchronous operations can be
chained elegantly. Promise rejections propagate through the promise chain and only need
to be handled once, at the end of the chain. As a contrived example, we’ll create a folder,
copy the file /etc/profile into this folder, and print the copied file:
import { copyFile, mkdir, readFile } from "fs/promises"
mkdir("backups")
.then(() => copyFile("/etc/profile", "backups/profile"))
.then(() => readFile("backups/profile", "utf8"))
.then(data => console.log(data))
.catch(error => console.error(`${error}`))
A promise-returning function can be invoked with the await expression. This effectively
treats the code following the invocation as a success callback, and, if invoked within a try
statement, the catch clause as a failure callback:
import { copyFile, mkdir, readFile } from "fs/promises"
try {
await mkdir("backups")
await copyFile("/etc/profile", "backups/profile")
const data = await readFile("backups/profile", "utf8")
console.log(data)
} catch (error) {
console.error(`${error}`)
}
You may recall the teaser program early in this chapter, where successive lines of the file
were read. Rather than explicitly await each line, JavaScript’s for-await statement allowed
a much cleaner expression of the sequencing of asynchronous operations.
For web applications, browser-side JavaScript has a global function fetch whose simplest
invocation returns, for a given URL, a promise that settles to an HTTP response3 . Responses
can be parsed with various methods depending on the type of response data. Most web
services return data in the JSON format [52]. The response’s json() method parses the
all-text HTTP response body into an object. Parsing is a potentially long-running operation
3 See [109] for detailed information on the HTTP protocol.

58 ■ Programming Language Explorations
so it too returns a promise. The typical pattern for retrieving data from a Web API thus
features a promise chain, as illustrated in the following HTML document that displays an
image of Pikachu retrieved from the free and open Pokémon API:
<img alt="pikachu" />
<script type="module">
const picture = document.querySelector("img")
const url = "https://pokeapi.co/api/v2/pokemon/pikachu"
const response = await fetch(url)
const pokemon = await response.json()
picture.src = pokemon.sprites.front_default
</script>
We’ve only scratched the surface of JavaScript asynchronous programming. In the end-ofchapter exercises you will research and explore questions such as “how do I execute code
after all of a set of asynchronous operations finishes?” or “how do I execute code after
just one of a set of asynchronous operations finishes?” We’ll also have the chance in future
chapters to look at other mechanisms to describe asynchronicity.

JAVASCRIPT WRAP UP
In this chapter, we were introduced to JavaScript. We learned that:
• All modern web browsers run JavaScript; in addition, engines such as Node.js run
JavaScript on the command line or server. Scripts are either classic scripts or module
scripts. Modules run in the safer “strict mode” and allow several features unavailable
in classic scripts.
• JavaScript expressions manipulate values of exactly eight types: Undefined, Null,
Boolean, Number, BigInt, String, Symbol, and Object. Variables store values of the
first seven types directly, but store references to objects.
• JavaScript generally employs weak typing. Values coerced to false are called falsy; all
other values are called truthy. The only falsy values in JavaScript are undefined,
null, 0, NaN, the empty string, and the empty object.
• The values null and undefined are called nullish and are frequently operated upon
by the ?. and ?? operators.
• When calling functions, arguments are fully evaluated and then passed to parameters by copying values. Extra arguments are ignored; extra parameters are assigned
undefined, or a default value if specified.
• Variable bindings can be created not only by simple assignment and pattern passing
but by a rich pattern-matching facility known as destructuring.
• JavaScript is statically scoped and uses deep binding. It supports both function-scoped
(var) and block-scoped (let, const) bindings.
• Object properties are either own properties or inherited properties. Property
lookup traverses the prototype chain, if required.
• The value of the expression this is context-dependent, and is frequently seen in
method definitions and constructors.

JavaScript ■ 59
• The keyword class provides sugar for defining a constructor function and methods
that populate a prototype object.
• The design of JavaScript facilitates asynchronous, event-driven programming. Events
are fired by actions within a web browser as well as within server applications dealing
with system resources and networking. Events are generally handled with promises
with explicit promise methods or the await keyword.
For further study in JavaScript, including language features not covered in this chapter as
well as links to articles, tutorials, oﬀicial documentation, and open-source projects using
JavaScript, see the JavaScript page in the book’s companion GitHub repository at https:
//github.com/rtoal/ple/tree/main/js.
