4

TypeScript

TypeScript is a superset of JavaScript with the capability to detect type errors before a
program is ever run.
First appeared 2012
Creator Microsoft
Recognized for Adding static types to JavaScript
Notable uses Web application clients
Tags Imperative, Functional, Event-Driven
Six words or less “Typed JavaScript at Any Scale”
JavaScript’s status as one of the world’s most popular languages has led to a variety of
so-called “compile-to-JavaScript” languages that seek to improve upon JavaScript while
still making use of the massive ecosystems of the browser, Node.js, and NPM. Several of
these, including CoffeeScript, Elm, and PureScript, depart widely from JavaScript’s syntax.
TypeScript takes a different approach: it is a pure superset of JavaScript. Every JavaScript
program is also a TypeScript program. If you know JavaScript, you can start using TypeScript right away.
As you can guess from the name, TypeScript’s additions to JavaScript mainly involve support for types. Programmers can annotate their code with type expressions, allowing the
compiler to check the type safety of most (or sometimes all) expressions before the program is even run. The type system is quite rich, with support for interfaces, generics, union
types, and more. TypeScript’s ability to infer types is quite impressive, even adjusting type
constraints on variables depending on a program’s control flow. In practice, you tend to get
away with far fewer type annotations than you might expect.
In this chapter, we’ll tour TypeScript the language, making heavy use of what we learned in
our study of the language of the previous chapter. After our standard teaser programs, we’ll
provide an overview of the type system, followed by coverage of selected topics, including
type validation, type annotation, the flexibility one has in typing objects, contextual typing
with narrowing and widening, type guards, generics, index signatures, utility types, and
type assertions.

63

64 ■ Programming Language Explorations

4.1

HELLO TYPESCRIPT

Before getting to the technical overview, we’ll look at our usual three example scripts. First
up is our script to generate integer right triangle measurements.
for (let c = 1; c <= 40; c++) {
for (let b = 1; b < c; b++) {
for (let a = 1; a < b; a++) {
if (a * a + b * b === c * c) {
console.log(`${a}, ${b}, ${c}`)
}
}
}
}
Exactly like JavaScript! TypeScript is, after all, a superset of JavaScript so every JavaScript
program is also a TypeScript program. However, TypeScript will, should you choose to allow
it, analyze your program for potential errors—especially type errors—before your program
is even run!
Having type checks performed before execution saves the programmer from having to
track down type clashes that occur at run time—or worse, subtle errors that arise from
JavaScript’s silently applied coercions. But to ensure all JavaScript programs are TypeScript
programs, the programmer must opt-in to type checks, and different degrees of checking are
possible. In this book, we will always assume that we have opted into the maximal degree
of type analysis.1
With type analysis turned on, variables are constrained to hold values of a certain type.
In the triple script above, TypeScript noticed that we initialized a, b, and c with values
of type number, then verified that all subsequent uses of these variables were consistent
with the number type. Inferring type constraints is neither required nor always possible, so
TypeScript permits type annotations, as shown in our second teaser:
function printPermutations(a: string[], n: number) {
if (n === 0) {
console.log(a.join("\t"))
} else {
for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
printPermutations(a, n - 1)
const j = n % 2 === 0 ? 0 : i
;[a[j], a[n]] = [a[n], a[j]]
}
printPermutations(a, n - 1)
}
}
const values = process.argv.slice(2)
printPermutations(values, values.length - 1)
1 See [74] for a detailed look at the various levels of type checking and how to employ them.

TypeScript ■ 65
Annotating a function’s parameters and return type is considered a best practice as it adds
documentation regarding the function’s intent. Parameter types are not always inferable
anyway, so annotating all parameters gives your entire script a consistent look and feel.
For local variables initialized at the point of declaration, however, annotations are generally
omitted when the inferred type is more-or-less obvious.
Our third teaser adds a couple annotations to its JavaScript counterpart and introduces a
TypeScript-only construct, the interface:
import * as readline from "readline"
const reader = readline.createInterface(process.stdin)
interface Player {
name: string
team: string
ppg: number
}
const players: Player[] = []
for await (const line of reader) {
let [team, name, games, points] = line.split(",")
if (Number(games) >= 15) {
players.push({ name, team, ppg: Number(points) / Number(games) })
}
}
const topTen = players.sort((p1, p2) => p2.ppg - p1.ppg).slice(0, 10)
for (let { name, team, ppg } of topTen) {
console.log(
`${name.padEnd(22)}${team.padEnd(4)}${ppg.toFixed(2).padStart(8)}`
)
}
Here any object with a string name property, a string team property, and a number ppg
property conforms to the Player interface and can be added to the players array, as this
array is explicitly constrained to hold Player elements.
Our technical overview of TypeScript follows. As we covered JavaScript in the previous chapter, our overview will focus exclusively on the differences between the two languages.

4.2 THE BASICS
TypeScript values belong to same the eight types as JavaScript. These types are accessible
to the program at runtime, and are thus known as dynamic types. TypeScript works with
a large set of additional types at compile-time, known as static types. These include:
• Literal Types. Every value in TypeScript gives rise to a type of one instance—
the value itself. For example, 3 is the type containing only the value 3, true is the
type containing only the value true, undefined is the type containing only the value
undefined, and null is the type containing only the value null.

66 ■ Programming Language Explorations
• Numbers, Strings, and Symbols. The static types number, bigint, string, and
symbol correspond exactly to the runtime types of similar names.
• The Object Type. All objects belong to the static type object.

• Object Types. The type expression {x1 : T1 ; . . .; xn : Tn } specifies the type of all
objects having properties xi with corresponding static types Ti .
• Interface Types. Like object types, interfaces describe the “shape” of an object,
namely the (typed) properties that objects of the interface type must have. The differences between interface types and explicit object types will be covered later in the
chapter.
• Classes. A class gives rise to its own static type.

• Union Types. T1 |. . .|Tn is the static type containing all of the elements from each
static type Ti . These are commonly used to form types with a small, fixed set of values,
such as "north"|"east"|"south"|"west", or “optional” types such as number|null.
The union type true|false (where true and false are literal types), goes by the
name boolean.
• Tuple Types. [T1 ,. . .,Tn ] is the type containing all ordered n-tuples made from each
static type Ti . For example, the type [boolean, 1|2|3] has the six values [true,1],
[true,2], [true,3], [false,1], [false,2], [false,3].
• Array Types. T [] is the type containing all arrays whose elements belong to static
type T .
• Intersection Types. T1 & . . . & Tn is the type containing the elements that belong to
each static type Ti . These are most useful when combining interfaces, as we will see
in more detail later in this chapter.
• Function Types. A function’s static type is denoted (p1 : T1 , . . . pn : Tn ) ⇒ T0 , where
the p1 are the parameter names and the corresponding Ti are the parameter types,
with T0 being the return type of the function.
• unknown. Every value belongs to the type unknown.

• void. TypeScript uses void as the return type of functions without an explicit return.
For example, a function that does nothing if its argument is true and throws an error
if false might be typed (condition: bool) -> void.
• never. TypeScript introduces never as the return type of functions that never return,
perhaps due to entering an infinite loop or throwing an error.

In practice, the TypeScript programmer annotates selected variables, parameters, and functions with static type expressions, while the compiler infers the types of unannotated expressions and validates that the program conforms to the type assignments. If the program
does not conform, a compile-time error occurs and the translation to JavaScript fails.
For example, the program isFinite("1") is rejected with the error “Argument of type
'string' is not assignable to parameter of type 'number'.”
When inferring the types of compound expressions, TypeScript will compute the best
common type. For arrays, this is often a union type formed from its elements; for example,
the value [3, "dog"] is typed as (number|string)[]. The best common type for functions
with multiple return statements is computed a little differently (see [62] for details).

TypeScript ■ 67
When performing type validation, TypeScript checks whether an expression of type t can be
sensibly used in a context in which an expression of type u is expected. In some theoretical
contexts, we write this relation as t <: u. Generally, this relationship is rather intuitive, with
t <: u whenever t is u, or t is literal type whose value is an instance of u, (e.g. 3 <: number)
or when t is a class extending u. Things get more complicated when dealing with arrays,
tuples, and other collections; we’ll cover those cases later in the chapter.
There is a special annotation that does not conform to an actual type (though it certainly
looks like one). Annotating a variable, parameter, property, or return type with any tells
the compiler not to type check its use at all, pushing the type checking to run time. The
following program throws (and catches) a TypeError at run time.
import { strict as assert } from "assert"
function next(n: number): any {
return n + 1
}
assert.equal(next(1), 2)
assert.throws(() => next(1)(2), /TypeError/)
When using the maximal level of static checking, TypeScript infers the types of expressions
without annotations, but when being loose, nonannotated expressions are implicitly typed
with any. It is this “implicit any” mode which allows us to say that all JavaScript programs
are TypeScript programs. When transitioning from JavaScript to TypeScript, a programmer
can choose which parts of the program require static type checking and leave the rest of
the program unchecked, while gradually adding more type-checking over time (allowing
TypeScript to be called gradually typed). Of course, it is recommended that maximal
checking always be employed (except in rare instances that existing JavaScript code is
slowly being ported to TypeScript), as the benefits of static type checking are often well
worth the effort.
As type annotations can get rather large, you can employ a type alias to give a name to
a type expression. The alias can be used as an annotation, for example:
import { strict as assert } from "assert"
type smallPrime = 2 | 3 | 5 | 7 | 11 | 13
type Point = { x: number; y: number }
type NumberPredicate = (n: number) => boolean
const five: smallPrime = 5
function checkYValue(test: NumberPredicate, p: Point): boolean {
return test(p.y)
}
assert(checkYValue(n => n > 3, { x: 1, y: five }))
When determining compatibility of an object compatible with an object type, the object
must have at least the properties mentioned in the type:

68 ■ Programming Language Explorations
function move(entity: { x: number; y: number }, dx: number, dy: number) {
entity.x += dx
entity.y += dy
}
const robot = { name: "Mari", x: 2, y: 2 }
const mars = { type: "Planet", x: 3, y: 20, radius: 100 }
move(robot, 7, 20)
move(mars, 55, -13)
assert(robot.y == 22)
Interestingly, however, object literals have an extra compatibility rule: they cannot have
excess properties! So directly invoking move({ name: "Mari", x: 2, y: 2 }, 7, 20)
would produce an error, even though passing this value through a variable (as in the example
above) succeeds.
Object types can be introducted with interfaces. Unlike aliased object types (e.g. type
Point = { x: number, y: number}), interfaces gain new properties from subsequent declarations:
interface Coordinate {
latitude: number
longitude: number
}
interface Coordinate {
when: Date
}
const party: Coordinate = {
latitude: 33.931999,
longitude: -118.437333,
when: new Date("2022-08-25T00:30:00Z"),
}
Rather than adding properties to an interface, you can also create a new interface type from
an existing interface with the extends keyword:
interface Person {
name: string
}
interface Employee extends Person {
title: string
}
let e: Employee = { name: "Allie", title: "CFO" }
An intersection type also combines all the properties of existing types:

TypeScript ■ 69
type Duration = {
seconds: number
}
type Instant = {
when: Date
}
type Period = Instant & Duration
let p: Period = { seconds: 120, when: new Date(1969, 6, 20) }
TypeScript has JavaScript’s classes, but adds new syntax for placing type constraints on
properties. In addition, at the maximally strict setting, all properties must be initialized
either at the property declaration or in a constructor.
class Ball {
radius: number = 1
color: string = "white"
}
let smallBall: Ball = new Ball()
assert(smallBall.radius === 1 && smallBall.color === "white")
let largeBall: Ball = { radius: 20, color: "red" }
assert(largeBall.radius === 20 && largeBall.color === "red")
class Sphere {
radius: number
color: string
constructor(radius: number, color: string) {
this.radius = radius
this.color = color
}
}
let sphere: Sphere = new Sphere(2, "red")
assert(sphere.radius === 2 && sphere.color === "red")
Properties may be marked readonly and may have an access modifier—one of public,
private, or protected. If a modifier is present on a constructor parameter, TypeScript
automatically generates and initializes a corresponding class property. The idiomatic way
to write the Sphere class in the previous example is:
class Sphere {
constructor(public radius: number, public color: string) {}
}
let sphere: Sphere = new Sphere(2, "red")
assert(sphere.radius === 2 && sphere.color === "red")

70 ■ Programming Language Explorations
A public property is visible anywhere the type is visible; a private property is visible only
within the type itself; a protected property is visible within the type or its subtypes. The
readonly modifier prohibits the property from being assigned after its initialization. An
example with all four modifiers follows:
import { strict as assert } from "assert";
abstract class Animal {
constructor(private readonly name: string) {}
protected abstract sound(): string;
public speak(): string {
return `${this.name} says ${this.sound()}`;
}
}
class Cow extends Animal {
sound() {
return "moooo";
}
}
class Horse extends Animal {
sound() {
return "neigh";
}
}
class Sheep extends Animal {
sound() {
return "baaaa";
}
}
const horse = new Horse("CJ");
assert(horse.speak() === "CJ says neigh");
const cow = new Cow("Bessie");
assert(cow.speak() === "Bessie says moooo");
assert(new Sheep("Little Lamb").speak() === "Little Lamb says baaaa");
For each of the animal types, only the public speak method is available outside the classes.
Each class has access to, and can override, the protected sound method, which is customized
for each animal. The name field is private, set only in the constructor and can subsequently
never be changed nor even read directly except within the Animal class itself. We’ve chosen
to make Animal an abstract class. Abstract classes cannot be instantiated, which models
the absence of “plain” animals: only concrete cows, sheep, and horses may exist.
In addition to being able to extend a class (a JavaScript capability), subtyping can be
achieved via the keyword implements, which enables TypeScript to check that any object
constructed with a given class will conform to a specific interface:

TypeScript ■ 71
import { strict as assert } from "assert"
interface Shape {
area: () => number
}
class Circle implements Shape {
constructor(public radius: number) {}
area() {
return Math.PI * this.radius ** 2
}
}
class Rectangle implements Shape {
constructor(public width: number, public height: number) {}
area() {
return this.width * this.height
}
}
let shapes: Shape[] = [new Circle(10), new Rectangle(5, 10)]
assert(shapes[0].area() === 314.1592653589793)
assert(shapes[1].area() === 50)
Having object types fully specified allows TypeScript to reject expressions such as c.x at
compile time if the property x is not a known property of c’s type—a powerful guard
against bugs due to mistyped property names. But what if we need to access a property
through a variable (e.g., console.log(c[prop])), or add or remove properties at run time?
TypeScript provides index signatures for this purpose. In the following example, [key:
string]: Score means that any number of addition properties with string keys and Score
values are permitted:
type Score = 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5
interface Rating {
plot: Score // a plot score is required
originality: Score // an originality score is required
[key: string]: Score // you can create other scores of your choice
}
let rating: Rating = {
plot: 5,
originality: 4,
idea: 3,
}
const metric = "clarity"
rating[metric] = 3 // Computed property access

72 ■ Programming Language Explorations
for (const key in rating) {
// Iteration through property names
console.log(`${key} score is ${rating[key]}`)
}
Index signatures do have some limitations. The only allowed key types are number, string,
and symbol; you cannot use literal types or unions, so "north" | "east" | "south" |
"west", while tempting, is not allowed. Also, TypeScript secretly adds undefined to your
value types. In the example above, although we typed all values as Score rather than
Score | undefined, you will still receive undefined when accessing a nonexistent property
of rating!

4.3 GENERICS
Occasionally we wish to write flexible code that works on a variety of types. For example,
adding an element to the beginning and end of an array, would, in TypeScript, be an infinite
number of distinct functions: one to add a number to the beginning and end of a number
array, one to add a string to the ends of a string array, and so on. Using unknown is too
permissive—the element type of the array and the type of the element to add should be the
same. TypeScript allows the use of a type variable to give us what we need:
import { strict as assert } from "assert"
function bracketed<T>(array: T[], item: T) {
return [item, ...array, item]
}
assert.deepEqual(bracketed([1, 2, 3], 0), [0, 1, 2, 3, 0])
assert.deepEqual(bracketed(["x"], "o"), ["o", "x", "o"])
// bracketed([1, 2, 3], "x") will not compile
Here bracketed is a generic function to be instantiated with concrete functions as needed,
replacing T with an actual type. We can even create generic functions with more than one
type variable. Function composition, where functions of type T → U and U → V are chained
to produce a function of type T → V , is a classic example:
import { strict as assert } from "assert"
function compose<T, U, V>(f: (x: T) => U, g: (y: U) => V) {
return (y: T) => g(f(y))
}
const lengthThenSqrt = compose((s: string) => s.length, Math.sqrt)
const encodedSquare = compose((x: number) => `${x * x}`, encodeURI)
const fourthRoot = compose(Math.sqrt, Math.sqrt)
assert.deepEqual(lengthThenSqrt("nineChars"), 3)
assert.deepEqual(encodedSquare(2.5), "6.25")
assert.deepEqual(fourthRoot(256), 4)

TypeScript ■ 73
Interfaces and classes can be generic as well:
class Queue<T> {
#data: T[] = []
constructor() {
this.#data = []
}
enqueue(item: T) {
this.#data.push(item)
}
dequeue(): T | undefined {
return this.#data.shift()
}
get size(): number {
return this.#data.length
}
}
let queue: Queue<number> = new Queue<number>()
queue.enqueue(1)
let anotherQueue = new Queue<string>()
anotherQueue.enqueue("first")
A number of built-ins in TypeScript are generic:
• Array<T> and ReadonlyArray<T>: JavaScript’s array type with elements constrained
to static type T , with mutable and immutable elements, respectively.
• Set<T> and ReadonlySet<T>: JavaScript’s Set with elements constrained to static
type T
• Map<K, V> and ReadonlyMap<K, V>: JavaScript’s Map with keys of static type K and
values of V
• Promise<T> JavaScript’s promise that resolves to a value of type T .

• Awaited<T> The type of an await expression that is awaiting an expression of type
T . If T is not a promise, then Awaited<T> is just T ; if T is Promise<U> then it’s U
and if T is a promise of a promise of a ... promise resolving to a V , then the awaited
type is V .
• Record<K, V> the type of an object whose keys are of type K and values of V . It is
typical, but not necessary, for K to be a small union of string literals—something we
saw in the last section that index signatures could not do.2

Do note that TypeScript’s constraints are not perfect. The types Array<string>,
Set<string>, Map<string, string>, and Record<string, string> can, as we saw with
index signatures in the previous section, deliver the value undefined when accessing objects of these types at nonexistent properties. You may encounter some other very surprising
cases where static typing fails to prevent run-time type errors. Here’s a surprising example
using the animal classes above:
2 You can explore the differences between records and objects with index signatures in the end-of-chapter
exercises.

74 ■ Programming Language Explorations
let animals: Animal[] = [];
let cows: Cow[] = [new Cow("Bessie")];
animals = cows;
animals.push(new Sheep("Little Lamb"));
assert(cows[1].speak() === "Little Lamb says baaaa"); // WHAT?!
Even with all checks turned on, we managed to put a sheep into an array of cows (see
Figure 4.1). How so? The first two lines constrain animals to an array of animals and cows
to an array of cows. The third assigns cows to animals which TypeScript allows because a
cow array is an animal array. Line 4 is permitted as the type checker sees only a sheep being
added to an array of animals, which makes sense because a sheep is an animal. However,
array assignment induces sharing, so the array referenced by cows now has a sheep. Not a
good look.
Where was the problem? Technically three aspects of TypeScript clashed:
1. TypeScript sees Cow[] as essentially a subtype of Animal[], allowing the assignment
of the former to a variable constrained to the latter
2. Arrays are stored by reference, so array assignment induces sharing
3. A shared (aliased) object was allowed to be mutated
animals
Bessie

Little Lamb

cows
Figure 4.1

Aliasing of arrays

The first of these, treating Array<Cow> as a subtype of Array<Animal>, is called covariance, a topic we will cover more fully in Section 7.6. When combined with aliasing and
mutation, run time type errors may occur. In fact, the TypeScript designers specifically did
not set out to create “a sound or provably correct type system. Instead [they sought to]
strike a balance between correctness and productivity.” [75] For the TypeScript programmer, this means that the static type of an expression may not reflect reality at run time. In
our example above, TypeScript will infer that cows[1] has static type Cow3 , even though
runtime value of this expression was created with the Sheep constructor.
There is much more to the study of generics that we have covered here, including generic
constraints and variance annotations on type parameters. Generic constraints, in particular,
will appear in subsequent languages on our tour.

4.4

OPTIONALITY

TypeScript, as a superset of JavaScript, provides the value null to represent the intentional
absence of information, and the value undefined to represent a lack of knowledge of a value.
If a variable or property named supervisor were null, we are certain no supervisor exists,
3 In most text editors, hovering over an expression will give you an indication of the inferred type

TypeScript ■ 75
but if undefined, there may or may not be a supervisor—we may not know or we may just
be refusing to say. If the presence of a supervisor is not required, we can use a union type
to express that it is optional:
function optionalsAsUnionTypesExample() {
type Person = {
id: number
name: string
supervisor: string | null | undefined
}

}

const people: Person[] = [
{ id: 1, name: "Alice", supervisor: "Tina" },
{ id: 2, name: "Bob", supervisor: null },
{ id: 3, name: "Carol", supervisor: undefined },
]

Here any object of this person type must have a property called supervisor and explicitly
be given a value, thought that value may be null or undefined. We can arrange to make
the property itself optional:
function optionalPropertyExample() {
type Person = {
id: number
name: string
supervisor?: string
}

}

const people: Person[] = [
{ id: 1, name: "Alice", supervisor: "Tina" },
{ id: 2, name: "Bob" },
]

TypeScript infers the property type for supervisor to be string | undefined, but the
convenience of being able to omit the property completely may express the programmer’s
intent better than explicitly supplying the value undefined. A similar situation occurs for
function declarations, where we may wish to omit an argument in a call:
function log(message: string, prefix?: string) {
if (prefix) {
console.log(`${prefix}: ${message}`)
} else {
console.log(message)
}
}
log("Hello, world!")
log("Hello, world!", "INFO")

76 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Without marking the prefix parameter optional, TypeScript would have required all calls
of this function to supply exactly two arguments. Granted, the declaration here is exactly
equivalent to writing function log(message: string, prefix: string | undefined =
undefined), but the optional parameter syntax is arguably much clearer.

4.5 NARROWING
The TypeScript compiler helps us by inferring the (static) type of each expression in a
program, preventing us (in most cases) from using an expression incorrectly. If an expression
is inferred to have type string | number, we cannot treat it as a string. However, the
following compiles and runs perfectly:
let x: string | number = Math.random() < 0.5 ? "small" : 100
if (typeof x === "string") {
console.log(x.toUpperCase())
} else {
console.log(x ** 2)
}
Here we used a type guard in the if-statement condition to narrow the type of x in the first
arm to string, and hence to number (the only remaining alternative from the original union
type) in the second. Narrowing can also occur in the presence of simple assignments:
let y: number | string
y = "hello"
console.log(y.toUpperCase())
y = 3
console.log(y ** 2)
Control flow analysis can also aid narrowing. Here TypeScript is able to determine that
the final return statement is reached only if the if-condition was false:
function nextStep(supervisor: string | null) {
if (supervisor === null) {
return "Nothing to do"
}
return `Call ${supervisor}` // supervisor is a string
}
TypeScript’s ability to narrow can seem pretty clever at times. In the following, x and y are
both narrowed to type number since this is the only type they could have when equal:
function f(x: string | number, y: number | boolean) {
if (x === y) {
console.log(x ** 2) // x and y are both numbers
}
}
Type guards for narrowing can be implemented in several ways, including typeof (for
distinguishing run-time types), instanceof (for types within type hierarchies—classes and
interfaces related via extends and implements), and the in operator (when constituents

TypeScript ■ 77
of a union can be distinguished by properties). If you would like to abstract the type guard
into its own function, you write the return type of this function as a type predicate as in
this example:
type Sphere = { radius: number }
type Box = { length: number; width: number; depth: number }
type Shape = Sphere | Box
function isSphere(shape: Shape): shape is Sphere {
return "radius" in shape
}
function volume(shape: Shape) {
if (isSphere(shape)) {
return (4 / 3) * Math.PI * shape.radius ** 3
} else {
return shape.length * shape.width * shape.depth
}
}
If the return type for our isShape function were simply boolean, the inferred type inside
the first arm of the if-statement would be Shape; the type predicate narrows this to the
Sphere.
In a similar fashion, you can abstract error checking into an assertion function, saving
your main code from noisy if-statements. Annotate your function with a condition that you
state will hold if your function returns. While type predicates return true or false, assertion
functions either return nothing or throw:
type Access = "read" | "write" | "append";
function assertRead(access: Access): asserts access is "read" {
if (access !== "read") {
throw new Error("Access is not read");
}
}
function viewAsGuest(access: Access) {
// Code looks great as the if-and-throw are elsewhere
assertRead(access);
// Actual reading would take place here
// The type of the variable `access` is narrowed to "read" here
// (Hover over both occurrences of access to see the difference)
access;
}
Sometimes annotations and type inference work together in ways you might not wish for,
but TypeScript has a convenient way to make you happy. In the code below, “things” have
a name and a color, and colors can be specified either by a name, or by their red, green,
and blue components:

78 ■ Programming Language Explorations
type Color = string | { r: number; g: number; b: number }
type Thing = {
name: string
color: Color
}
const thing1 = { name: "thing1", color: "red" }
const thing2: Thing = { name: "thing2", color: "red" }
const thing3 = { name: "thing1", color: "red" } satisfies Thing
thing1.color.toUpperCase()
// thing2.color.toUpperCase() // ERROR: thing2.color could be an object
thing3.color.toUpperCase()
We’ve left off a type annotation for thing1, so TypeScript infers a type of { name: string,
color: string }. We have the desired inferred type, so we can treat thing1.color as a
string. But the lack of an annotation means that TypeScript will not be able to check any
typos we might make when defining the thing. We do annotate thing2, enabling conformance checking, but now the inferred type is Thing, which means thing2.color is a color,
not a string. To get the best of both worlds—conformance checking against the Thing type
and inference to the narrowed color variant in the constant, we use the satisfies operator
to conformance check without widening the property types.
There’s more to narrowing than we’ve covered in this short section. For a full run-through
of all of the narrowing powers of the language, see the Narrowing section in the TypeScript
Handbook [73].

4.6

TYPE OPERATORS

TypeScript goes to great lengths to help analyze your code for type errors. It provides several
operators to create new types from existing types, enables you to work with different types
under different conditions, and predefines a decent set of utility types to specify rather
precisely what you expect of your code.
For an object type T , keyof T produces a union of T ’s keys. In a type context, typeof x,
where x is a variable or a property, produces the (static) type of its operand.
type Widget = {
id: string
name?: string
price?: number
status: "soldOut" | "inStock" | "discontinued"
}
let ex01: Widget
let ex02: keyof Widget // "id" | "name" | "price" | "status"
let ex03: typeof ex01 // Widget

TypeScript ■ 79
The type expression T [K] for object type T and a type K suitable for indexing, provides
the type(s) of all index operations on objects of type T with those keys:
let ex04: Widget["id" | "price"] // string | number
let ex05: Widget["name"] // string | undefined
let ex06: Widget["status"] // "soldOut" | "inStock" | "discontinued"
We present a handful of pre-defined utility types here:
let ex07: Partial<Widget> // all properties optional
let ex08: Required<Widget> // all properties required
let ex09: Readonly<Widget> // all properties readonly
let ex10: Pick<Widget, "id" | "name"> // only id and name props
let ex11: Omit<Widget, "id" | "name"> // all props except id and name
let ex12: Exclude<"a" | "b" | "c", "a"> // "b" | "c"
let ex13: Extract<"a" | "b" | "c", "a" | "b"> // "a" | "b"
Further utility types provide a means to describe components of function types:
function f1(w: Widget, factor: number): number {
return (w.price ?? 0) * factor
}
let ex14: Parameters<typeof f1> // [Widget, number]
let ex15: ReturnType<typeof f1> // number
A few more utility types are useful in a class context: ConstructorParameters<T>,
InstanctType<T>, and ThisType<T>!.
Not surprisingly, there are quite a few more kinds of types that can be expressed. Among
those that we’ll leave you to explore on your own are: conditional types, which can express
types with different forms based (usually) on a generic parameter, mapped types, which
are generated by property transformations on existing types, and template literal types,
which are unions of string literals computed rather than listed explicitly.

4.7 TYPE ASSERTIONS
We’ve seen how TypeScript provides extraordinary compile-time analysis by allowing annotation with a rich set of static types (including unions, interfaces, functions, and more) and
its powerful inference capabilities (including control flow analysis in narrowing). We’ve also
seen that it prioritizes convenience over 100% inference accuracy (as in the earlier covariant
array example). In the spirit of convenience, TypeScript even lets you assert the types of
expressions yourself. Perhaps you may do so for stylistic reasons:
let scores = [] as number[]
// We could have also written let scores: number[] = []
scores.push(100)
You may also have received a value from somewhere in the code that was annotated with a
type that was quite general, but you know better:

80 ■ Programming Language Explorations
function dataFromSomeoneElse(): unknown {
return "Hello friends, I am a string"
}
let shouted = (dataFromSomeoneElse() as string).toUpperCase()
// Note dataFromSomeoneElse().toUpperCase() is an error
A classic example of TypeScript not knowing the most narrow type comes from web development:
function enableGamePlay() {
// getElementById returns HTMLElement | null
let playButton = document.getElementById("play") as HTMLButtonElement
playButton.disabled = false
}
Type assertions may even be necessary when accessing properties indirectly:
let order = {
"doro wot": 2,
shiro: 5,
fatira: 3,
}
let favorite = "fatira"
const quantity = order[favorite as keyof typeof order]
// Note order[favorite] is an error!
console.log(`I ordered ${quantity} plates of ${favorite}`)
Not all assertions use the as operator. The keyof and typeof “assertions” can also appear
in a function signature:
function serve(name: keyof typeof order) {
console.log(`Serving ${order[name]} plates of ${name}`)
}
serve("doro wot")
Use type assertions with care—you are free to assert a type constraint that simply does not
hold, but TypeScript will not complain:
const offMenu = "pepperoni"
serve(offMenu as keyof typeof order) // undefined at runtime!
Remember, TypeScript is your friend and does whatever it can to help you. But it does
not force you into anything. Letting you assert types, even if you may be wrong, is a way
to ensure the programmer is always the one in control. In practice, you may never use this
great power, but it is there if you need it.

TypeScript ■ 81

TYPESCRIPT WRAP UP
In this chapter, we were introduced to TypeScript. We learned that:
• TypeScript is a superset of JavaScript. Any JavaScript program is also a TypeScript
program.
• TypeScript has a rich set of static types used by the compiler to check the typeconsistency of expressions before running the program, and a set of eight dynamic
types that can be queried at run time.
• The static type system includes literal types, basic types, object types, intersections,
unions, tuples, arrays, functions, and more. The bottom type is never and the top
type is unknown.
• In practice, very few expressions must be annotated with static types, as the compiler
does quite a good job of inferring the best type.
• Objects can be typed with object types or interfaces. Properties can be marked
readonly. In a class, the access modifiers private, protected, and public are available. Classes can also be abstract, extend other classes, or implement interfaces.
• Generic, or parameterized, types are useful in creating functions and objects that work
on multiple types.
• TypeScript arrays are simultaneously (1) mutable, (2) stored by reference, and (3)
covariant. This is an indication that the type analysis is not 100% sound at compile
time, but this is intentional, as the language prioritizes convenience and simplicity
over type soundness.
• TypeScript makes frequent use of narrowing during type inference. Narrowing is the
process of inferring a more specific type for an expression based on surrounding code.
Narrowing can be influenced by type guards, assignments, control-flow analysis, and
user-defined type predicates and assertion functions.
• TypeScript defines type operators and utility types to give your code more clarity and
more precision in type checking.
• TypeScript does not force you to accept its own inferred types—you may assert the
static type that it should use, even if that type is not correct.
For further study in TypeScript, including language features not covered in this chapter as
well as links to articles, tutorials, oﬀicial documentation, and open-source projects using
TypeScript, see the TypeScript page in the book’s companion GitHub repository at https:
//github.com/rtoal/ple/tree/main/ts.

Python

Python is “a programming language that lets you work quickly and integrate systems more
effectively.” [97]
First appeared 1991
Creator Guido van Rossum
Recognized for Expressiveness
Notable uses Web servers, Scripting, Data science
Tags Imperative, Glue, Dynamic, Scripting
Six words or less Easy to learn and extremely productive
Guido van Rossum started working on Python in 1989, and has overseen much of this
popular language’s development for three decades. The Python community bestowed on
him the title Benevolent Dictator for Life, or BDFL, which he held until 2018.
Python is known for being easy to read and write. The syntax has a light and uncluttered
feel. A large number of data types, including tuples, lists, sets, and dictionaries are built-in
with convenient syntactic forms. While particularly powerful as a scripting language, it is a
truly general-purpose language, having been used in domains from games and entertainment
to web applications to statistics and data science. The language is also notable for its
culture. Python aficionados, known as Pythonistas, take pride in writing elegant, expressive,
Pythonic code. They learn and practice The Zen of Python [87], a collection of best practices
and philosophies that help give applications a characteristic feel.
Our survey of Python will begin with our common three introductory scripts. We’ll continue with a look at Python’s rich type system, in which all types are organized into an
ontology that supports something called multiple inheritance. We’ll see how Python’s
handling of scope also differs from previous languages on our tour. We’ll cover many of
the language’s modern features including keyword arguments, comprehensions, a powerful
match statement, custom operator definition (reminiscent of Lua’s metamethods), and its
support for iterators and generators. We’ll close with a look at Python’s decorators.

83

84 ■ Programming Language Explorations

5.1 HELLO PYTHON
Let’s get started in the usual way, listing right triangle measurements:
for c in range(1, 41):
for b in range(1, c):
for a in range(1, b):
if a * a + b * b == c * c:
print(f'{a}, {b}, {c}')
Python uses indentation to define structure. The range constructor builds a range object,
inclusive at the bottom and exclusive at the top. Ranges produce their values on demand,
so there is no need to worry that large lists are being created before iteration. String
interpolation uses curly braces within f-strings.
Here’s Heap’s algorithm in action permuting command line arguments:
import sys
def print_permutations(a, n):
if n <= 0:
print("\t".join(a))
else:
for i in range(n):
print_permutations(a, n - 1)
j = 0 if n % 2 == 0 else i
a[j], a[n] = a[n], a[j]
print_permutations(a, n - 1)
print_permutations(sys.argv[1:], len(sys.argv) - 2)
Command line arguments are stored in the list argv from the sys module, which we must
explicitly import. The script name is contained in argv at index 0, so the true arguments
come from the slice of argv starting at index 1. Python’s conditional expression has the
form y if x else z—quite a contrast to JavaScript’s x ? y : z.
Here’s our third standard teaser script:
import sys
from heapq import nlargest
from operator import itemgetter
players = []
for line in sys.stdin:
team, name, games, points = line.strip().split(',')
if int(games) >= 15:
players.append((name, team, int(points)/int(games)))
for name, team, ppg in nlargest(10, players, key=itemgetter(2)):
print(f'{name:22}{team:4}{ppg:8.2f}')

Python ■ 85
The standard input stream (sys.stdin) is iterable, line by line, so it can be used in a for
statement. We split each line into its team, name, games, and points components, then
rebuild each player line into a tuple with the name, team, and ponts-per-game, and store
those tuples for players appearing in 15 or more games into a list. To get the top ten
scorers by points-per-game, we do not need to sort the entire list: we have the more eﬀicient
nlargest function from the heapq module. The fancy itemgetter indicates that we are
determining the largest elements based on the tuple value at index 2, namely points-pergame (as tuples are 0-based).

5.2

THE BASICS

Let’s begin with types. Integers (of type int) are unbounded, floating-point numbers
(float) are generally IEEE binary64, [51] the booleans (bool) are True and False, and
the utility value None has its own type NoneType. For text, we have both str for sequences
of Unicode characters and bytes for byte sequences.
Where JavaScript and Lua each have a small, fixed, number of types; Python has an unlimited number. Tuples, lists, sets, dictionaries, functions—and user-defined abstractions
such as employees, blog posts, and calendar appointments—would all have type Object in
JavaScript and type table in Lua. Not so in Python. Let’s explore:
# Many built-in types have built-in names
assert type(5) == int
assert type(True) == bool
assert type(5.7) == float
assert type(9 + 5j) == complex
assert type((8, 'dog', None, False)) == tuple
assert type('hello') == str
assert type(b'hello') == bytes
assert type([1, '', False]) == list
assert type(range(1,10)) == range
assert type({1, 2, 3}) == set
assert type(frozenset([1, 2, 3])) == frozenset
assert type({'x': 1, 'y': 2}) == dict
# Built-in vs. User-defined functions
def plus_two(x):
return x + 2
assert str(type(plus_two)) == "<class 'function'>"
assert str(type(max)) == "<class 'builtin_function_or_method'>"
# Even modules are types!
import math
assert str(type(math)) == "<class 'module'>"
# Many built-in modules define their own types
from datetime import date, datetime
assert type(date(1969, 7, 20)) == date
assert type(datetime.now()) == datetime

86 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Note that Python types are objects! Whereas in JavaScript and Lua, asking for the type of
the value 5 produces the string "number" in reply, Python gives you the object (int) that
is the type of integers. As int is an object, it has a type too:
assert type(int) == type
assert type(list) == type
assert type(type) == type

# Interesting...or obvious?

All sequences are indexed from 0, and support access (e.g., s[3]), slicing (e.g., s[3:5],
s[:3], s[2:], s[3:21:2]),1 and length (e.g., len(s)). List, dictionaries, and sets can be
written using comprehensions, in which the elements are computed by rules with for and
if clauses, for example:
assert [(x,y) for x in range(1,3) for y in range(5) if x < y] == \
[(1, 2), (1, 3), (1, 4), (2, 3), (2, 4)]
assert {x**2 for x in range(5) if x % 2 == 0} == \
{0, 16, 4}
assert {k: len(k) for k in ("red", "green", "blue")} == \
{'red': 3, 'green': 5, 'blue': 4}
To define your own types, create a class:
import math
class Circle:
def __init__(self, radius):
self.radius = radius
def area(self):
return math.pi * self.radius * self.radius
c = Circle(10)
assert type(c) == Circle
assert type(Circle) == type

# Circle is a type!
# It really is!

assert str(type(Circle.area)) == "<class 'function'>"
assert str(type(c.area)) == "<class 'method'>"
assert c.area() == math.pi * 100
assert Circle.area(c) == c.area()

# A method call...
# ...is just sugar

Here we’ve created a new type, Circle. Classes are namespaces: we did not define a global
name area, we defined Circle.area. A class is also callable; the expression Circle(10)
creates a new instance of type Circle and immediately passes it to Circle.__init__
for initialization, here setting its radius attribute. Python provides syntactic sugar very
similar to Lua’s: c.area() is shorthand for Circle.area(c).2 The sugared form gives you
1 Slice bounds i:j are inclusive of the start index and exclusive of the end. The start index defaults to

0 and the end to the sequence length. An optional third component (as in i:j:k) is a step size, selecting
“every kth element.”
2 Note that Lua’s sugared expression uses a colon (c:area()), while Python is able to simply use the dot
(c.area()). Why is Python able to do this, while Lua could not?

Python ■ 87
the feeling of a method invocation where c is the receiver; indeed, our script shows that
c.area is a method, while Circle.area is a plain old function.
Unlike JavaScript, TypeScript, and Lua, whose variables either hold direct values or store
references based on type, all Python variables store references. Every value is an object, and
every object has a unique identifier, accessible to the programmer:
a = 5
b = {'x': 5, 'y': 3}
c = "hello"
d = c
e = c.lower()
f = 8 * b['y'] - 19

# 5 is an object
# dicts are objects
# strings are objects too
# two variables sharing an object
# should generate a new object
# what happens here?

for obj in (a, b, b['x'], b['y'], c, d, e, f):
print(id(obj))
One run of this program produced bindings of objects to variables shown in Figure 5.1.
Variables c and d reference the same object, since assignment copies references. Interestingly,
we see there is only one copy of the integer 5 in the object pool; this is acceptable because
integers are immutable. Immutability allows sharing because it is impossible for a change
through one variable to change the value seen by the aliased variable. Python strings are
immutable as well, so our implementation could have decided to share a single copy of the
string "hello" among three variables, but chose not to.
a

4330887560

4329311472

Figure 5.1

"hello"

Python variables and objects

Immutable values can not only be shared, they can be stored directly inside variables, as
long as the built-in id function returns a reasonable value. This should be a big relief to
readers wondering whether Python engines actually take the time to allocate and indirectly
access simple numbers and booleans.
Python types have supertypes and subtypes. While JavaScript, TypeScript, and Lua require
the programmer to set up prototype chains to simulate IS-A relationships between types,
Python models this relationship directly:

88 ■ Programming Language Explorations
class Animal:
def __init__(self, name):
self.name = name
def speak(self):
return f'{self.name} says {self.sound()}'
class Cow(Animal):
def sound(self):
return 'moooo'
class Horse(Animal):
def sound(self):
return 'neigh'
class Sheep(Animal):
def sound(self):
return 'baaaa'
s = Horse('CJ')
assert s.speak() == 'CJ says neigh'
c = Cow('Bessie')
assert c.speak() == 'Bessie says moooo'
assert Sheep('Little Lamb').speak() == 'Little Lamb says baaaa'
Here we’ve made Animal the superclass of its three subclasses Horse, Cow, and Sheep.
Animal has a superclass too, namely object, the ultimate superclass of all Python classes.
Subclass instances inherit attributes of their superclass(es), so, any instance of Horse
has not only a sound attribute, but __init__ and speak attributes too. Classes can have
multiple direct superclasses, a condition known as multiple inheritance. Consider:
from subprocess import call
from animals import Cow
# Disclaimer: This only works on an O.S. with a say command.
class Vocalizer:
def vocalize(self):
call(['say', self.name + 'says' + self.sound()])
class VocalCow(Vocalizer, Cow):
pass
c = VocalCow('Bessie')
print(c.speak())
c.vocalize()

# speak is inherited from Cow
# vocalize is inherited from Vocalizer

A VocalCow can both speak and vocalize (see Figure 5.2). But what if we had called the
vocalizer method speak instead of vocalize? Would VocalCow have two speak attributes?
Or would one be inherited and the other not? Or would the attempt to build the class with
two superclasses fail because of the name clash?

Python ■ 89
object
Vocalizer

Animal
Sheep

Horse

Cow
VocalCow

Figure 5.2

Python animal classes

This question is ultimately one of how Python finds attributes of an object that the object’s
class does not directly define. In the languages we’ve seen previously, lookup proceeds along
a single prototype chain, but in Python we need to know whether to look first in Cow or
Vocalizer for attributes not in VocalCow. Python addresses this question by giving each
class a method resolution order, or MRO. In our case, evaluating VocalCow.mro()
produces:
[VocalCow, Vocalizer, Cow, Animal, object]
This implies attributes in Vocalizer take precedence over those in Cow. We could, of course,
modify the order to suit our needs. It can get tricky, but the mechanism is well-defined. See
[104] for a detailed description of the algorithm used to compute the MRO.
It’s helpful to see how Python’s plethora of built-in types are related (Figure 5.3). The
common built-in types are all direct subtypes of object, with one notable exception: bool
is a subclass of int. Interestingly, the various sequence types and numeric types do not share
any common supertype (other than object); we’ve called out these informal groupings in
the figure simply because programmers may find them useful.

90 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Python features two different operators for variable assignment. Use = when assignment is a
statement, and := (the walrus operator—look at it sideways, do you see the tusks?) when
an expression. The := form cannot appear in just any expression [4], and is generally used
only in certain idiomatic situations where readability is actually improved, such as:
if (offered := len(candidates)) != 3:
raise ValueError(f"Expected 3 candidates, received {offered}")
while (item := input('What do you like? ')) != '':
print(f"I like {item}, too")
for x in range(1000, 10000, 1000):
print(x, log1 := math.log(x), log2 := math.log(log1), math.log(log2))
Python has approximately 20 kinds of statements. Many are fairly common (assignment,
break, continue, return, conditionals and loops, function and class definitions, assertions,
raising and catching errors and so on), but a few have some uncommon twists. The for and
while loops have an else clause, which executes when the loop finishes normally (not via
break, return, or raise):
for place in places:
if good(place):
print(f"Ah, a good place: {place}")
break
else:
print("No good places for you")
Unlike JavaScript, Python cannot destructure function call arguments nor assignments
(other than tuples), but it does perform general pattern matching in the match statement.
def describe(e):
match e:
case 2 | 3 | 5 | 7 | 11:
return "Small prime"
case n if n % 5 == 0:
return "Multiple of 5"
case (_, x, _):
return f"A three-tuple with {x} in the middle"
case {'id': y}:
# Matches any dictionary that has an id key, binds value to y
return f"Your identifier is {y}"
case _:
return "What?"
assert describe(5) == "Small prime"
assert describe(15) == "Multiple of 5"
assert describe((1, 2, 3)) == "A three-tuple with 2 in the middle"
assert describe({"data": 2, "id": "x"}) == "Your identifier is x"
assert describe("Mu") == "What?"

Python ■ 91

5.3 TYPE HINTS
All Python expressions have a (dynamic) type available at run time, but Python’s syntax
allows for static types to be specified as well. A type checker processes these annotations,
known as type hints, and reports any type inconsistencies it can find. The set of static
types is much richer than that of dynamic types; for example, Python’s only dynamic list
type is list, but static list types can specify the element type as well. The static types
support unions, callables, sequences, generics, and even type variables. As in TypeScript,
type narrowing is supported. The following script illustrates only a few of the many features
of the static type system:
dozen: int = 12
found: bool
found = False
from typing import List, Set, Dict, Tuple
def first_two(a: List[float]) -> Tuple[float, float]:
return (a[0], a[1])
assert first_two([False, 0, 17, 2.2, []]) == (False, 0)
Type use of type hints is completely optional in Python. Variables without annotations are
assumed to belong to the type Any, compatible with every type. Even when hints are used,
they have no run-time effect whatsoever.

5.4 SCOPE
Python variables are bound within blocks, which include top-level modules, function definitions, or class definitions. There is no explicit variable declaration; assigning to a variable
inside a function or class creates a new variable, unless marked global or nonlocal. As in
previous languages, Python locals shadow in-scope variables of the same name.
a, b, c = 1, 2, 3
def f(x):
d = 4
a = 5
def g():
x = 1
nonlocal a
a = 6
global c
c = 7
g()
assert x == 10
assert a == 6
assert b == 2

# Three global variables
# Brand new local!
# Local to f, shadows global
# Local to g, shadows f's x
# allows access to f's a
# updates the nonlocal

# g's x did not overwrite f's x
# assert that it changed
# globals are visible

f(10)
assert a == 1 and b == 2 and c == 7

92 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Using a local before it is first written raises an UnboundLocalError; using a global before
it is first assigned raises a NameError:
try:
print(x)
except NameError as e:
print('NameError raised as expected')
x = "I'm global"
def problem():
print(x)
x = "I'm Local"

# Local but not bound yet

try:
problem()
except UnboundLocalError as e:
print('UnboundLocalError raised as expected')
The scope rules around comprehensions are worth noting. As stated in the Python Reference: “...aside from the iterable expression in the leftmost for clause, the comprehension is
executed in a separate implicitly nested scope.” [96] However, a variable bound with the
walrus operator in a comprehension will be bound in the outer scope [4].
x = 3
assert [x for x in range(5)] == [0, 1, 2, 3, 4]
assert x == 3 # x was not changed
print([y for y in range(5)])
try:
print(y) # will raise an error, since y did not leak
assert False
except Exception as e:
assert e.__class__.__name__ == 'NameError'
assert [(y := 1) for x in range(5)] == [1, 1, 1, 1, 1]
assert y == 1 # The := operator creates a new variable!

5.5

PARAMETER ASSOCIATION

Python passes arguments to parameters by (conceptually) passing each argument’s id. This
avoids the expense of copying large arguments and allows both the caller and callee to
reference the same object. We call this mechanism pass-by-sharing. The language supports both default arguments and argument packing and unpacking. In a definition, a
single-starred parameter packs additional arguments into a tuple (recall JavaScript’s rest
parameter). In a call, the single-starred argument unpacks a sequence to pass constituent
elements as multiple parameters (recall JavaScript’s spread operator):

Python ■ 93
def f(x, y=1000, *z):
print(f'x={x} y={y} z={z}')
f(0)
f(0,1)
f(0,1,2)
f(0,1,2,3,4)
f(*range(7))

# x=0, y=1000, z=()
# x=0, y=1, z=()
# x=0, y=1, z=(2,)
# x=0, y=1, z=(2,3,4)
# x=0, y=1, z=(2,3,4,5,6)

But there is much more to parameter association in Python.
In his essay Learnable Programming, Bret Victor lays out nine design principles for building
an environment and a programming language suitable for learning to program. One of his
principles for a language is to make meaning transparent. He motivates this principle
by showing a snippet of code (not in Python) containing the lines
fill(161,219,114);
rect(105,20,60,60);
and suggests that a learner might ask “What do the numbers after fill mean?” and “What
units are they in?” and “Why are there so many numbers?” A simple way to help with
the first question is to supply the parameter names in a call. And we do exactly that in
idiomatic Python:
set_fill_color(red=161, green=219, blue=114)
draw_rectangle(corner=(105,20), other_corner=(60,60))
Arguments passed in this fashion are called keyword arguments. They can be passed in
any order, though they must follow the nonkeyword, positional arguments. They provide
a nice alternative to passing a dictionary:
def write_rule(selector, **options):
print(selector, '{')
for prop, value in options.items():
print(f' {prop.replace("_", "-")}: {value};')
print('}')
write_rule('h1', font_family='Helvetica', size='20px')
write_rule('p.error', color='red', margin='16px', padding='0')
By default, arguments can be passed either as positional arguments or as keyword arguments. However, we can require a particular association by partitioning parameters in the
function declaration: those preceding a / must be positional; those following a * must be
keyword, as in this example from the Python documentation:
def f(a, b, /, c, d, *, e, f):
print(a, b, c, d, e, f)
f(10, 20, 30, d=40, e=50, f=60)
Positional-only arguments are useful when the parameter name is rather generic (e.g., x),
and keyword-only arguments shine when there are several arguments.

94 ■ Programming Language Explorations

5.6

SPECIAL METHODS

Python is a rich language with dozens of operators and built-in functions. It conveniently
allows you to customize nearly all of them on your own classes. As in Lua, Python provides
certain special functions that are implicitly invoked in certain contexts; Table 5.1 lists some
of the more commonly used ones.

Python special methods

Let’s make a little class for two-dimensional vectors, translating as directly as possible our
Lua program from the previous chapter:
import math
class Vector:
def __init__(self, i, j):
self.i = i
self.j = j
def magnitude(self):
return math.sqrt(self.i * self.i + self.j * self.j)
def __add__(self, v):
return Vector(self.i + v.i, self.j + v.j)
def __mul__(self, v):
return self.i * v.i + self.j * v.j
def __str__(self):
return f'<{self.i},{self.j}>'

Python ■ 95
u = Vector(3, 4)
v = Vector(-5, 10)
assert u.i == 3
assert u.j == 4
assert u.magnitude() == 5.0
assert str(u + v) == '<-2,14>'
assert u * v == 25
There are quite a few similarities with the Lua code (the use of special methods for one),
but a few differences, too. Perhaps the most significant is that Python does not require
explicit metatables or prototypes—the methods are defined directly inside the class.

5.7

ITERATORS AND GENERATORS

An iterable is an object that produces a sequence of values. Lists, tuples, sets, dictionaries,
and objects of a few other built-in types are iterables. Iterables can be used in for statements
and a number of built-in functions, including min, max, sum, all, any, filter, map, sorted,
and zip.
for x in (1,2,3): print(x)
for x in [1,2,3]: print(x)
for x in {1,2,3}: print(x)
for c in 'hello': print(c)

# elements of a tuple
# elements of a list
# elements of a set
# characters of a string

for k in {'x':1, 'y':2, 'z':3}:
print(k)

# keys of a dict

with open('colors') as f:
for line in f:
print(line.strip())

# lines of a file

When Python needs to iterate an iterable, it applies the built-in iter function to the
iterable, producing (via a call to the iterable’s __iter__ method—remember Table 5.1?)
an iterator. The iterator’s __next__ method yields the next element of the sequence or
raises a StopIteration exception when no more elements remain. This means you can
write your own iterables:
class OneTwoThree:
def __iter__(self):
value = 0
class OneTwoThreeIterator:
def __next__(self):
nonlocal value
value += 1
if value > 3:
raise StopIteration()
return value
return OneTwoThreeIterator()

96 ■ Programming Language Explorations
for i in OneTwoThree():
print(i)
Writing iterables and iterators from scratch can be a bit tedious, so Python provides generators to simplify the creation of iterable sequences. A generator results from calling
a function containing a yield statement. Executing such a function does not invoke the
function’s body, but rather returns a generator object. Here’s a generator that produces
successive powers of two, up to some limit:
def powers_of_two(limit):
value = 1
while value < limit:
yield value
value += value
# Use the generator
for i in powers_of_two(70):
print(i)
# Explore the mechanism
g = powers_of_two(100)
assert str(type(powers_of_two)) == "<class 'function'>"
assert str(type(g)) == "<class 'generator'>"
assert g.__next__() == 1
assert g.__next__() == 2
assert next(g) == 4
assert next(g) == 8
Calling a generator function creates an object and assigns to it __iter__ and __next__
methods. It assembles, on the fly, the code to raise StopIteration at the proper time.
Interestingly, we can write an iterator that never raises a StopIteration exception—in
other words, an infinite sequence.
def powers_of_two(limit):
value = 1
while True:
yield value
value += value
If the iteration scheme is suﬀiciently simple, you can create a generator with a generator expression, which looks like a list comprehension with parentheses instead of square
brackets. Generator expressions have the advantage over list comprehensions of not having
to compute and store the entire data set in memory.
# List comprehension.
# Computes all of its elements first.
# If large, it's too slow to produce and wastes memory.
bad = [x*x for x in range(10**9)]

Python ■ 97
# Generator expression.
# Produces values on demand, during iteration.
# Computed instantly and consumes almost no memory.
good = (x*x for x in range(10**9))
The module itertools from the standard library includes a number of functions for constructing and manipulating generators, including permutations, which builds a generator
that produces permutations of a sequence as tuples. Let’s use this module to rewrite our
permutations script from the beginning of the chapter:
import sys
from itertools import permutations
for permutation in permutations(sys.argv[1:]):
print(' '.join(permutation))

5.8 DECORATORS
Time for some magic. We’re going to change the behavior of a function without ever rewriting
its source code. In Python, functions (and hence methods) can be decorated, allowing us
to do some extra work when the function is called. Here is one of the simplest decorator
examples; it prints the time the function is called as well as the duration taken by the
call:
import datetime
def logged(f):
def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
start = datetime.datetime.now()
print(f'{f.__name__} started at {start}')
f(*args, **kwargs)
duration = datetime.datetime.now() - start
print(f'{f.__name__} took {duration}')
return wrapper
@logged
def say_hello(name):
print('Helllllloooooooooooo, {name}')
say_hello('Alice')
Prefixing the function definition of say_hello with the @logged decorator is equivalent to
following its definition with say_hello = logged(say_hello). So the decorator syntax is
not essential, but it adds a great deal of expressiveness: all of the logging code is isolated
from the mainline code, allowing us to see at a glance (without clutter) what our application
should be doing, and knowing it will be logged.
Decorators give us a nice solution to cross-cutting concerns such as logging, transaction
maintenance in databases, persistence, authentication, and so on. These concerns are called
“cross-cutting” because one does not organize a system around authenticated objects vs.

98 ■ Programming Language Explorations
persistent objects; instead these concerns can apply almost anywhere. You will often see
Python code with decorators such as @persistent, @transactional, and @authenticated.
And here’s another. It’s well known that the naïve application of the definition

F (n) =

{

n
F (n − 1) + F (n − 2)

n≤1
otherwise

leads to horrific performance: computing F (40) results in 331,160,281 calls to the function.
Over 40 trillion calls are needed to do F (50), and over a septillion (1021 ) calls for F (100).
The problem is that so many calls are repeated, as we can see from the evaluation:
F (40) = F (39) + F (38)
= (F (38) + F (37)) + F (38)
As F (38) is called twice, we should record its value after the first call and then simply reuse
this value rather than making the second repeated call. This trick of reusing results, known
as memoization, is applied also to F (37), F (36), and so on, reducing the number of calls
from over 331 million down to 41.
Performing memorization within the fib function would violate the separation of concerns
principle, so we write a decorator:
def memoized(f):
cache = {}
def wrapper(*args):
if args in cache:
return cache[args]
cache[args] = f(*args)
return cache[args]
return wrapper
@memoized
def fib(n):
return 1 if n <= 1 else fib(n-1) + fib(n-2)
print(fib(100))
Python decorators can also be parameterized, constructed with a class syntax (any object of a class with a __call__ method becomes a decorator) and even applied to classes
and methods. Some are part of Python’s standard library; one of the most common is
@dataclass.
The dataclass decorator enhances a class that you write by automatically adding an initializer, several special methods (such as equality testing and comparison) according your
your wishes, optionally configures it for use in a match statement, and can even ensure all
instances are immutable.3 Essentially, the decorator generates a fair amount of boilerplate
so you don’t have to. Here is a very brief example:
so.

3 Technically, you can still mutate a frozen object, but it requires writing some pretty strange code to do

Python ■ 99
from dataclasses import dataclass
@dataclass(frozen=True, order=True)
class Point:
x: float
y: float
p = Point(1.0, 2.0) # decorator autogenerates a constructor
q = Point(1.0, 2.0)
assert(p.x == 1.0)
# decorator autogenerates attribute accessors
assert(p.y == 2.0)
# p.x = 5.0
# is an error because Point is frozen
assert(p == q)
# eq=True is a default for the decorator
assert(p >= q)
# we set order=True in the decorator
match p:
# match_args=True is a default for the decorator
case (a, b):
assert(a == 1.0 and b == 2.0)

PYTHON WRAP UP
In this chapter, we were introduced to Python. We learned that:
• Python is a mature, general-purpose language popular in scripting, web frameworks,
and data science. It was created and is still overseen by Guido van Rossum.
• Python has a large number of built-in types, including many different numeric types
and over a dozen collection types.
• All values, regardless of type, are considered objects. All objects have an id and a
type. Types are objects.
• New (dynamic) types are created by defining a class.

• Classes, as well as all types in Python, are objects. (Contrast this with JavaScript and
Lua, where inquiring the type of an object produces the name of the type as a string.)
• Types in Python can have supertypes and subtypes. All paths through the chain of
supertypes of an object will end at the type object. The supertype-subtype relationships are used in attribute lookup. Subtypes inherit attributes from their supertypes.
• Because types can have multiple supertypes, the language enforces a linear order in
which all ancestor types must be searched to find inherited attributes. This is called
the method resolution order, or MRO.
• Static types can be specified and checked at compile-time.

• Python local variables are introduced by assignment in a local scope. Nonlocal variables in enclosing lexical scopes can be read; to write to nonlocals either nonlocal or
global must be used.
• Many standard operators and functions can be overloaded by defining special methods in a class. These special methods have names such as __add__, __eq__, and
__contains__.

100 ■ Programming Language Explorations
• Python supports delayed evaluation through iterators and generators. Generators can
be created by functions with a yield statement, or via generator expressions.
• Decorators enable ways to enhance the behavior of functions and classes, without
actually adding any code directly inside the function or class.
Our tour of Python has been rather brief. We did not visit any concurrency features of
the language, which are quite rich. Python has libraries for in-process threads that can
be managed via thread pools and futures, libraries for multiprocessing and spawning new
operating system processes, libraries for scheduling, and a library for asynchronous I/O.
While we will be looking into concurrency in other languages on our tour (including Java,
Go, Rust, and Swift), the reader is encouraged to look into the oﬀicial Python documentation
for examples and explanations of the language’s many features.
For further study in Python, including language features not covered in this chapter as well
as links to articles, tutorials, oﬀicial documentation, and open-source projects using Python,
see the Python page in the book’s companion GitHub repository at https://github.com/
rtoal/ple/tree/main/python.

CHAPTER

6

Ruby

Ruby’s designer, Yukihiro Matsumoto (Matz), expressed his language’s purpose as follows:
“I hope to see Ruby help every programmer in the world to be productive, and to enjoy
programming, and to be happy. That is the primary purpose of Ruby language.” [70]
First appeared 1995
Creator Yukihiro Matsumoto
Recognized for OOP, Metaprogramming, DSL construction
Notable uses Web servers, Scripting
Tags Object-oriented, Expression-oriented, Dynamic
Six words or less “A Programmer’s Best Friend”
Matz designed Ruby in the mid-1990s as a language in which one could be productive and
have fun programming. Rather than building a language with a small, simple core (for
which Lisp and Smalltalk are famous), Matz looked to be pragmatic, and aimed for humanfriendliness. The language has many fans who find the language beautiful, powerful, and
expressive.
Many programmers today know Ruby from the web frameworks built upon it. But Ruby
is truly a general-purpose language. It’s a scripting language (influenced by Perl), with
operating system interfaces, string processing, regular expressions, and various syntactic
niceties like flexible quoting syntaxes. It is object-oriented in the way (another influencer)
Smalltalk is object-oriented, expressing nearly all computation with message passing. Ruby’s
blocks come from Smalltalk, too. And the metaprogramming facilities of Ruby are, as in
Smalltalk, an area in which the language really shines. Ruby’s uncanny ability to implement
domain-specific languages (DSLs) also fuels its popularity across domains.
Our Ruby tour will begin with our standard three example programs and a brief overview
of the language. We will then introduce the object-oriented paradigm and show how Ruby
makes object-oriented programming not only possible but natural. We’ll then proceed to
two important features of Ruby, blocks and mixins, and continue with a section on access
control, a feature Ruby makes relatively easy compared to the four languages we have seen
before. We’ll round out our coverage with Ruby’s metaprogramming facilities.

103

104 ■ Programming Language Explorations

6.1 HELLO RUBY
We begin with our familiar script to list small right triangle measurements:
1.upto(40) do |c|
1.upto(c - 1) do |b|
1.upto(b - 1) do |a|
puts "#{a}, #{b}, #{c}" if a * a + b * b == c * c
end
end
end
Here we see convenient syntactic features such as string interpolation with #{} and a trailing
if-clause, as well as an iteration construct that is definitely not a traditional for-statement.
Instead, we start the script by sending the message upto to the object 1. The message
is sent with the argument 40 and a block (here delimited with do and end). The upto
method executes its block for each value in the range from its receiver up to, and including,
its argument.
Next up is the command line permutations using Heap’s Algorithm:
def print_permutations(a, n)
if n <= 0
puts a.join "\t"
else
0.upto(n-1) do |i|
print_permutations a, n-1
j = n.even? ? 0 : i
a[j], a[n] = a[n], a[j]
end
print_permutations a, n-1
end
end
print_permutations(ARGV, ARGV.length - 1)
The constant ARGV for the command line argument array does not need to be explicitly
imported or tagged with a module name. And for something new, calls do not always
require parentheses.
Like Python, Ruby already knows how to do permutations, so the entire script above could
be done in one line:
ARGV.permutation.each {|s| puts s.join "\t"}
The .permutation method produces an enumerator for all of the permutations of the command line argument array. An enumerator is not an actual array but a generator producing
values on demand. The each method requests the values, which are passed to the block
where the permuted array is joined up into a string and written on a line to standard
output.
Here is the data science application to compute the top ten scorers:

Ruby ■ 105
players = []
ARGF.each do |line|
team, name, games, points = line.strip.split ','
if games.to_i >= 15
players += [[name, team, points.to_f/games.to_i]]
end
end
players.sort {|p1, p2| p2[2] - p1[2]}[0, 10].each do |player|
puts "%-22s%-4s%8.2f" % player
end
This short, flowing script shows how writing Ruby is, for many, pleasant and productive.
ARGF was created with the scripter’s happiness in mind. If no command line arguments are
present, ARGF is standard input; if arguments are present, they’re treated as filenames from
which input will be read (in order). We pull each line from standard input, split to get the
data components, and construct an array of arrays with player information (for those players
appearing in more than 15 games). The second statement sorts by points-per-game descending, takes the first 10 players, and prints according to the desired specification.

6.2

THE BASICS

Ruby has variables, assignments, the usual control structures, and even exceptions. As in
Python, all variables store references to values; you can find the unique identifier of any
object x with x.object_id. Every object has a unique class:
fail unless nil.class == NilClass
fail unless false.class == FalseClass
fail unless true.class == TrueClass
fail unless (2**1000).class == Integer
fail unless 2.0.class == Float
fail unless :dog.class == Symbol
fail unless "dog".class == String
fail unless (1..5).class == Range
fail unless [1,2,3,4,5].class == Array
fail unless {x: 1, y: 2}.class == Hash
fail unless {'x' => 1, 'y' => 2}.class == Hash
Ruby features a large number of built-in classes. A class can have at most one superclass. Superclasses allow us to express IS-A relationships: each of the expressions
5.is_a? Integer, 5.is_a? Numeric, 5.is_a? Object, and 5.is_a? BasicObject evaluate to true. Figure 6.1 shows part of this hierarchy.
Like Python, Ruby classifies floating point numbers differently than integers, and allows
integer values to grow as large as available memory will allow them to grow: computing
2 ** 100 happily produces 1267650600228229401496703205376. The language is “strongly
typed” in the sense of allowing very few implicit coercions: you can’t, for example, add
strings and numbers. But everything can be coerced to a boolean. Like Lua, and very
unlike Python, the only expressions that are falsy are false and nil.

106 ■ Programming Language Explorations
