places order.
7.25 We may wish to finish our restaurant simulation with the message "The restaurant
is closing down" after all of the customers have gone home. Implement this feature
in two ways: (a) using join, and (b) using a CountDownLatch.
7.26 In this chapter, we saw examples of threads responding to being interrupted, but we
never explained how to interrupt a thread! Find out how this is done. Enhance the
restaurant simulation so that after launching each of the threads, the main thread
interrupts a few customers and one cook after a random amount of time has passed.
7.27 We took a little shortcut in our ReflectionExample script in this chapter, by declaring that our main method could throw any exception. In the Java culture, this is
considered bad practice. Rewrite the script so that the throws clause properly lists
the individual exceptions that may be thrown.
7.28 Find out about the concept of type erasure in Java. Why exactly did Java’s designers
make this a feature?

CHAPTER

8

Kotlin

Kotlin is a modern programming language originally targeted to the Java Virtual Machine,
advertised as being concise, cross-platform, and fun.
First appeared 2011
Created at JetBrains
Recognized for Modernizing Java
Notable uses Enterprise computing, Android applications
Tags Architecture-neutral, Multithreaded, High-performance
Six words or less “Modern, safe, concise Java successor”
Java’s mix of massive popularity and being somewhat slow to modernize has given rise
to a number of successors capable of interoperating with existing Java code, most notably
Groovy, Scala, Ceylon, and Kotlin. Each address many of Java’s shortcomings (discussed
in the last chapter) in various ways, though thanks to Google anointing it the preferred
language for Android applications [39], Kotlin, named after the island the Baltic Sea, is the
most successful.
This modern language has much to offer not only programmers that must work in the Java
ecosystem, but to anyone interested in modern language design. Kotlin supports multiple
programming paradigms (including procedural, functional, and object-oriented) and sports
many features programmers have come to expect: null safety, pattern matching, type inference, extensibility, data classes, coroutines, and anonymous functions. Kotlin programs
can compile into Java bytecode for execution on the Java Virtual Machine (alongside existing Java code) or can compile into code that runs directly within a web browser. The
standard Kotlin distribution comes with a compiler targeting LLVM, making the language
automatically suitable for native applications running on a wide variety of hardware platforms.
As Java interoperability was a design goal of the language, we’ll spend some time on comparisons of Kotlin with Java. We’ll investigate how Kotlin was or was not able to fully address
the noted shortcomings of Java outlined in the previous chapter, while not neglecting the
expressive and fun features of this popular and well-loved [53] language.

151

152 ■ Programming Language Explorations

8.1 HELLO KOTLIN
Here’s our first teaser script:
for (c in 1..40) {
for (b in 1..<c) {
for (a in 1..<b) {
if (a * a + b * b == c * c) {
println("$a, $b, $c");
}
}
}
}
While Kotlin applications normally have a function called main as their starting point,
we’ll be using Kotlin Scripting to reduce some boilerplate code. Our script has nothing
too different from previous languages on our tour, except perhaps the nice operator ..<
for ranges with exclusive upper bounds. String literals require double quotes, and string
interpolation shares JavaScript’s ${} notation, though when interpolating a single variable
we may elide the curly braces. Now to the second teaser:
fun printPermutations(a: Array<String>, n: Int) {
if (n <= 0) {
println(a.joinToString(separator = "\t"))
} else {
for (i in 0 ..< n) {
printPermutations(a, n-1)
val j = if (n % 2 == 0) {0} else {i}
a[n] = a[j].also { a[j] = a[n] }
}
printPermutations(a, n-1)
}
}
printPermutations(args, args.lastIndex)
While concise, there are a few language features worth calling out in this script. First, the
type annotations on the function parameters are required. Second, you’ll notice that named
arguments are permitted directly in the call, as we previously saw in Python and Ruby.
Third, there’s no conditional operator (such as _?_:_). Instead, the if construct can be
used both as a statement and as an expression. When used in an expression context, the
two arms produce the expression’s value. Finally, we used a rather interesting idiom for
swapping array elements. The expression x.also block first evaluates the receiver x, then
executes the block, then produces the previously evaluated value of the receiver. In our
script, this means: (1) the current value of a[j] is evaluated; (2) within the block, array slot
j is overwritten with a[n]; and (3) since the also method returns the original value of a[j],
the value of a[n] is properly updated.
The third teaser reads from standard input, made easy by the readLine() and Kotlin’s
expressive sequence operations:

Kotlin ■ 153
data class Player(
val team: String,
val name: String,
val games: Int,
val ppg: Double
) {
companion object {
fun fromLine(line: List<String>): Player {
val points = line[3].toDouble()
val games = line[2].toInt()
return Player(line[0], line[1], games, points / games)
}
}

}

fun reportLine(): String {
return String.format("%-22s%-4s%8.2f", name, team, ppg)
}

generateSequence { readLine() }
.map { it.split(",") }
.map { Player.fromLine(it) }
.filter { it.games >= 15 }
.sortedByDescending { it.ppg }
.take(10)
.map { it.reportLine() }
.forEach { println(it) }
This script is made from a single class declaration and a call to generateSequence. The
sequence sources its values by repeatedly calling readLine(), which produces the next line
from standard input, or null when no more input remains (terminating the sequence). Each
line is split and turned into a player object; the sequence is filtered to keep only the players
that have played in 15 games, is sorted and clipped to its top ten; then each remaining
player is reported with a nice output format. The anonymous functions used here all have a
single parameter and can hence use a shorthand syntax where the implicit parameter name
is it.
You may notice a similarity between the player data class in this example with the Java
player record in the previous chapter. The obvious syntactic difference is that Kotlin has no
“static methods.” Instead, a Kotlin class can be given one (and only one) companion object,
whose methods have access to that of the class itself. Data classes, companion objects, and
sequences are just a few of the many interesting Kotlin features we will now explore.

8.2 THE BASICS
Kotlin programs are built from a collection of source files, each of which consists of a sequence of declarations (variables, functions, classes, objects, etc.) and statements. Variables
marked val are read-only (can only be assigned to once), while those marked var may be
reassigned. Type constraints can usually be inferred if given an initializer.

154 ■ Programming Language Explorations
val message = "Hello World!" // Type inferred
var found: Boolean
// Type required when no initializer
// println(found)
// Will not compile! 'found' not initialized
found = true
Kotlin’s basic numeric types include the 8, 16, 32, and 64 bit signed integers Byte, Short,
Int, and Long; the corresponding unsigned integer types UByte, UShort, UInt, and ULong;
and the IEEE binary32 and binary64 types Float and Double. The boolean type is Boolean
and the String type consists of elements of type Char. Because chars are, like Java and
JavaScript, UTF-16 character codes, you’ll have to get code points via a library method,
namely codePoints:
val message = "←\uD83C\uDF53→"
// LEFTWARDS ARROW, STRAWBERRY, RIGHTWARDS ARROW
for (codePoint in message.codePoints()) {
println(Character.getName(codePoint))
}
Kotlin’s arrays are fixed-size and always mutable, so programmers will generally opt for lists
instead, as they can grow and shrink, and can even be made read-only. In addition to lists,
the standard library features a rich set of other collections, including sets and maps:
val berries = listOf("blueberry", "blackberry", "strawberry")
val team = mutableListOf<String>()
team.add("Ashleigh")
val smallPrimes = mutableSetOf(2, 3, 5, 7, 11)
var capitals = mapOf(
"Senegal" to "Dakar",
"Vietnam" to "Hanoi",
"France" to "Paris")
assert(berries.size == 3)
assert(berries[0] == "blueberry")
assert(team == listOf("Ashleigh"))
assert(3 in smallPrimes)
assert(capitals["France"] == "Paris")
Objects have properties and member functions, sometimes called methods. Properties
may optionally have initializers, getters, and setters. The latter two are useful to maintain
relationships between properties:
object Boundary {
var radius = 1.0
var area: Double
get() = Math.PI * radius * radius
set(value) {
radius = Math.sqrt(value / Math.PI)
}
fun draw() = "I can't draw yet"
}

Kotlin ■ 155

assert(Boundary.radius == 1.0)
Boundary.radius = 2.0
assert(Boundary.area == 4 * Math.PI)
Boundary.area = 64.0 * Math.PI
assert(Boundary.radius == 8.0)
assert(Boundary.draw() == "I can't draw yet")
Function parameters can have defaults (unlike Java), rest parameters are introduced with
the keyword vararg, and function arguments can be named in a call. The spread operator
is a prefix *. Type annotations on parameters are required, but if the function body is
just a single expression, the return type can be inferred if you use the shorthand definition
syntax.
// Default arguments
fun log(message: String, level: String = "INFO"): String {
return "[$level] $message"
}
// Positional and named arguments
assert(log("Starting") == "[INFO] Starting")
assert(log(message = "Starting") == "[INFO] Starting")
assert(log("Out of memory", level = "ERR") == "[ERR] Out of memory")
assert(log(level = "DEBUG", message = "Here") == "[DEBUG] Here")
// Single Expression Function
fun square(x: Int) = x * x
assert(square(5) == 25)
// Varargs
fun weightedSum(vararg numbers: Int, multiplier: Int): Int {
return numbers.sum() * multiplier
}
assert(weightedSum(1, 2, 3, multiplier = 10) == 60)
val smallPrimes = intArrayOf(2, 3, 5, 7, 11)
assert(weightedSum(*smallPrimes, multiplier = -2) == -56)
If a function has no explicit return, it is assumed to return the value () of type Unit, which
you may, but do not have to mention explicitly if your function body is a block. If the
function does not return at all, the return type is Nothing:
fun greet(name: String) { println("Hello, $name") }
fun part(name: String): Unit { println("Bye, $name") }
fun poof(): Nothing { throw Exception("poof") }
Function values may be stored in variables, and passed to and returned from other functions.
To store or pass a declared function or method, you’ll need to use the function reference
operator, ::, to obtain a function value. Function values can also be written as anonymous
functions or lambdas, as illustrated here:

156 ■ Programming Language Explorations
fun twice(f: (Int) -> Int, x: Int): Int = f(f(x))
// Function reference operator (::) for declared funs & methods
fun square (x: Int): Int = x * x
assert(twice(::square, 5) == 625)
assert(twice(Int::inc, 3) == 5)
// Anonymous functions: fun without a name
assert(twice(fun(x: Int): Int = x * x, 5) == 625)
// Lambda expressions: expression in curly braces
assert(twice({ x -> x * x }, 5) == 625)
assert(twice({ it * it }, 5) == 625)
Note that lambdas with a single parameter do not need the arrow (->) but can instead refer
to the single argument as it.
If the final argument in a call is itself an anonymous function, it can be written after the
parentheses—a syntactic device known as a trailing closure. Not only does this make
pipeline-oriented code very readable (as we saw in the third teaser program at the beginning of this chapter), it can give the appearance of extending the language with new
“statements”:
fun unless(condition: Boolean, action: () -> Unit) {
if (!condition) {
action()
}
}
unless (1 == 2) {
println("1 is not equal to 2")
}
Use if for simple conditionals and when for multiway (like Java’s switch). Both if and
when can be used in a statement context or an expression context. The when branches are
tried in order until one is successful. A when without an argument is a nice replacement for
a chain of ifs and else ifs:
// If expression
val latitude = 34.0
val hemisphere = if (latitude > 0) {"North"} else {"South"}
// When expression with no argument
val (x, y) = Pair(8, 9)
val comparison = when {
x > y -> "Greater"
x < y -> "Less"
else -> "Equal"
}

Kotlin ■ 157
// When expression with an argument
var secret = "999"
var keys = listOf(21, 8, 55)
val message = when (x) {
0, 1 -> "Zero or one"
secret.toInt() -> "You found it"
in keys -> "A key"
!in 3 until 12 -> "3 through 11"
else -> "Unexpected"
}
assert(hemisphere == "North")
assert(comparison == "Less")
assert(message == "A key")
We’ve packed a lot into our section on Kotlin basics, but there is of course much more to
cover. While we can’t look at the entire language, there are a number of language elements
worthy of a deeper look. We’ll start with classes and make our way to concurrency.

8.3 CLASSES
As in Java, a class defines a kind of factory for creating objects. The primary constructor
appears in the class header (along with any fields you want to initialize with the constructor),
while the class body holds properties, methods, secondary constructors, initializer blocks,
companion objects, and inner classes.
class Circle(
val radius: Double,
private var x: Double,
private var y: Double,
) {
val area = Math.PI * radius * radius
val circumference = 2 * Math.PI * radius
constructor(descriptor: String): this(
descriptor.split(":").map { it.trim().toDouble() }
)
private constructor(a: List<Double>) : this(a[0], a[1], a[2])
init { println("Circle created!") }

}

fun move(dx: Double, dy: Double) {
x += dx
y += dy
}
override fun toString(): String {
return "Circle with radius $radius at ($x,$y)"
}

158 ■ Programming Language Explorations

val c = Circle(2.0, 0.0, 0.0)
c.move(13.0, 21.0)
println(c)
val newC = Circle("3.0:1.0:2.0")

// use primary constructor
// invoke a method
// indirectly calls toString()
// use secondary constructor

assert(c.radius == 2.0)
assert(c.area == 4.0 * Math.PI)
assert(newC.circumference == 6.0 * Math.PI)
The circle class above has a primary constructor taking three doubles—the radius, center
x, and center y respectively. The x and y properties are mutable, but their visibility is set
to private, meaning they cannot be seen outside the class. The radius property happens
is immutable (due to val), but it has the default visibility of public. The properties area
and circumference are initialized but have no getters and setters, as their values cannot
change. The move method is able to mutate x and y, since code inside the class can access
private members.
There are two secondary constructors for enabling the creation of circles from a descriptor
string. In Kotlin, all secondary constructors must delegate to the primary constructor via
the keyword this. Our class features one initialization block. These blocks are executed
whenever an instance is created.
Every class in Kotlin is a descendant of the built-in class Any, with three methods equals,
hashCode, and toString. When defining a new class, method implementations may override ancestor implementations; such methods are marked override. Classes may not be
subclassed by default; they must be marked open to allow this. Similarly, methods may not
be overridden unless marked open:
open class User(name: String) {
open val minPasswordLength = 12
}
class Admin(name: String): User(name) {
override val minPasswordLength = 18
}
Abstract classes and interfaces are automatically open and do not require the open keyword.
Similarly, abstract methods of such classes and interfaces need not be marked open to be
overridden. Our recurring animals example provides an illustration:
abstract class Animal(val name: String) {
fun speak() = "$name says ${sound()}"
abstract fun sound(): String
}
class Cow(name: String): Animal(name) {
override fun sound() = "moooo"
}

Kotlin ■ 159
class Horse(name: String): Animal(name) {
override fun sound() = "neigh"
}
class Sheep(name: String): Animal(name) {
override fun sound() = "baaaa"
}
val h = Horse("CJ")
assert(h.speak() == "CJ says neigh")
val c = Cow("Bessie")
assert(c.speak() == "Bessie says moooo")
assert(Sheep("Little Lamb").speak() == "Little Lamb says baaaa")
In order to limit the number of subclasses to a fixed set (known at compile time), mark the
class sealed. Sealed classes, too, are automatically open. The following script limits the
number of tree subclasses to two. The script will also show that Kotlin supports generic
types, which we covered previously in TypeScript and Java.
sealed class Tree<T> {
class Empty<T> : Tree<T>()
class Node<T>(
val left: Tree<T>, val value: T, val right: Tree<T>
) : Tree<T>()

}

fun size(): Int = when (this) {
is Empty -> 0
is Node -> left.size() + 1 + right.size()
}

assert(Tree.Empty<Int>().size() == 0)
val t: Tree<Int> = Tree.Node(
Tree.Node(Tree.Empty(), 3, Tree.Empty()),
5,
Tree.Node(Tree.Empty(), 8, Tree.Empty()))
assert(t.size() == 3)
Application designers often distinguish active and passive classes. Instances of active
classes act as agents with rich behavior, initiating and carrying out complex application
functionality on their own. Instances of passive classes simply hold data. Kotlin provides
the data class for these simple containers.
You can mark a class as a data class provided the class is defined at the top level (not
nested), its primary constructor has at least one parameter, all constructor parameters are
marked val or var, and the class is also not open, sealed, nor abstract. For your data class,
Kotlin will automatically generate the methods equals, hashCode, toString, copy, and
numbered component methods. Let’s explore:

160 ■ Programming Language Explorations
// A one liner is all you need
data class User(val id: Int, val email: String)
// Three users
val friend = User(133, "alice@example.com")
val colleague = User(133, "alice@example.com")
var guest = User(205, "bob@example.com")
// Autogenerated toString
assert(friend.toString() == "User(id=133, email=alice@example.com)")
// Autogenerated equals
assert(friend == colleague)
assert(friend.equals(colleague))
// Autogenerated hashCode
assert(friend.hashCode() == colleague.hashCode())
// Autogenerated copy
val newFriend = guest.copy()
assert(newFriend.id == 205)
assert(newFriend.email == "bob@example.com")
assert(newFriend == guest)
assert(newFriend !== guest) // different instances
// Autogenerated component methods
assert(friend.component1() == 133)
assert(friend.component2() == "alice@example.com")
// Destructuring
val (id, email) = friend
assert(id == 133)
assert(email == "alice@example.com")
Autogenerating methods not only cuts down on the number of lines of code necessary to
implement a class, but frees the programmer from having to remember to update all the
boilerplate methods whenever the structure of a class changes.
We’ll close our look at Kotlin classes with something fun: member functions with a single
argument can be made to be written as infix operators:
class Employee(val name: String) {
private var manager: Employee = this
constructor(name: String, manager: Employee) : this(name) {
this.manager = manager
}
infix fun manages(e: Employee) = e.manager == this
}

Kotlin ■ 161
val alice = Employee("Alice")
val bob = Employee("Bob", manager = alice)
assert(alice manages alice)
assert(alice manages bob)
There are quite a few standard (nonsymbolic) infix operators, including to for making pairs;
until, downTo, and verb for making ranges; string functions matches, compareTo, and zip;
and the bitwise operators and, or, xor, shl, shr, and ushr. All named operators, whether
built-in or programmer-defined, exist at the same precedence level1 and are naturally leftassociative. After all, x or y and z simply sugars x.or(y).and(z).2

8.4

EXTENSIONS

In several of the languages visited so far, methods can easily be added to classes at runtime.
In Lua, you add functions to the metatable; in JavaScript and TypeScript, you add to the
prototype. Kotlin’s approach is interesting: you don’t really add properties or classes, but
you get the syntactic convenience of doing so with extension properties and methods.
Extensions can be defined for classes you write, someone else writes, or even those from the
standard library. Here are a few examples:
fun Int.factorial(): Int {
return (1..this).fold(1) { x, y -> x * y }
}
assert(0.factorial() == 1)
assert(10.factorial() == 3628800)
val String.scalarCount: Int
get() = this.codePointCount(0, this.length)
assert("".scalarCount == 0)
assert("←\uD83C\uDF53→".scalarCount == 3)
fun <T> MutableList<T>.swap(index1: Int, index2: Int) {
this[index1] = this[index2].also { this[index2] = this[index1] }
}
val numbers = mutableListOf(34, 8, 21, 5, 1)
numbers.swap(1, 3)
assert(numbers == mutableListOf(34, 5, 21, 8, 1))
Though extension functions are not strictly methods, they may be written infix if they take
a single parameter. This makes sense since extension functions, like infix operators, are
essentially syntactic sugar.
1 See [59] for the complete operator precedence chart for Kotlin.

2 Other languages, notably Haskell and Swift, allow programmers to define their own infix operators and
specify their own precedence levels.

162 ■ Programming Language Explorations

8.5 COMPANION OBJECTS
There are a variety of approaches to the problem of defining properties and objects which
logically “belong with” a class but are not tied to any instance of the class. We’ve seen
Java’s static fields and methods, Python’s @classmethod and staticmethod decorators,
and JavaScript’s ability to define properties directly on constructor functions rather than in
prototypes. Rust (which we’ll see in a future chapter) has the idea of associated functions.
Kotlin’s approach is to attach properties and functions to a companion object.
In the following exploration we have a Point data class with a property for the origin and
a function to compute the midpoint between two points. how members of the companion
object use the class name as prefix.
import kotlin.math.hypot
import kotlin.math.sqrt
data class Point (val x: Double, val y: Double) {
companion object {
val ORIGIN = Point(0.0, 0.0)
fun midpoint(p: Point, q: Point) = Point(
(p.x + q.x) / 2.0, (p.y + q.y) / 2.0)
}
fun distanceFromOrigin() = ORIGIN distanceTo this
fun mirror() = Point(-x, -y)
infix fun distanceTo(p: Point) = hypot(x - p.x, y - p.y)
}
val oneOne = Point(1.0, 1.0)
val halfHalf = Point(0.5, 0.5)
assert(Point.midpoint(Point.ORIGIN, oneOne) == halfHalf)
assert(Point(4.0, -3.0).distanceFromOrigin() == 5.0)
assert(Point(1.0, 1.0) distanceTo Point(0.0, 0.0) == sqrt(2.0))
assert(oneOne.mirror() == Point(-1.0, -1.0))
An advantage of the companion object approach is that it allows programmers to think
of all methods as instance methods, rather than mentally distinguishing class vs. instance
methods. Functions defined in a companion object are instance methods whose receiver is
the companion object. In the end-of-chapter exercises, you will explore whether the same
mental model applies to other languages.

8.6 NULL SAFETY
One of Java’s more interesting design decisions was the inclusion of the value null into
every reference type in the static type system. For example, a Java variable s declared with
type String may be assigned the value null, and the compiler will not issue an error. The
invocation s.toLowerCase() is also not a compile-time error (the phrase is—surprisingly—
statically type correct!), though a good IDE or linter may warn you of impending run-time
doom. Indeed, if the method call is executed with null as the receiver, a run-time error
occurs, as null is not part of the dynamic string type.

Kotlin ■ 163
Nearly every other language featured in this book with a static type system keeps nulls
out of other types unless the programmer explicitly creates a union type including it. Kotlin
is no exception. Kotlin calls such a union a nullable type, and comes with a nice syntax.
Variables constrained to type String may only hold strings; those constrained to String?
may hold strings or null.
// Name is required, manager is optional
class Employee(val name: String, val manager: Employee? = null) {
infix fun manages(e: Employee) = e.manager == this
}
val alice = Employee("Alice") // Alice has no manager
val bob = Employee("Bob", manager = alice)
// Compiler will not allow using an Employee? as an Employee
// println(alice.supervisor.name) // Will not compile
// println(bob.supervisor.name) // Will not compile either!
To “unwrap” the optional, you can, like in TypeScript make use of narrowing, or use the safe
call operator ?., which produces null if the receiver is null or the property value otherwise.
Suﬀixing this operator with let will unwrap, and if not null, pass the unwrapped value to
a closure:
// You can always use the "safe call" operator
assert(alice.manager?.name == null)
assert(bob.manager?.name == "Alice")
// Use the safe call operator with let
bob.manager?.let {
// Only gets here if supervisor is not null
assert(it.name == "Alice")
}
Kotlin’s Elvis operator3 coalesces nulls, that is, x?:y produces x if x is non null and y otherwise. You’ll see this operator used frequently in functions that wish to exit early on errors,
as Kotlin’s designers used throw and return for expressions rather than statements:
// Demonstrate ?: (Elvis operator)
val supervisor = alice.manager?.name ?: "YOUR NAME HERE"
assert(supervisor == "YOUR NAME HERE")
fun reportingChain(e: Employee?, from: List<String>): List<String> {
val name = e?.name ?: return from
return reportingChain(e?.manager, from + name)
}
assert(reportingChain(bob, listOf()) == listOf("Bob", "Alice"))
There’s a convenient method to remove nulls from sequences:
3 As with Python’s walrus operator, this one gets its name from looking at it sideways. You should see a
pair of eyes and wavy hair.

164 ■ Programming Language Explorations
// Demonstrate filterNotNull
val employees = listOf(alice, bob, null, bob)
val managers = employees.filterNotNull().map { it.name }
assert(managers == listOf("Alice", "Bob", "Bob"))
And if you are really sure you won’t have a null, you can use the dangerous !! operator
which unwraps and throws a runtime exception for nulls:
// The not-null assertion operator, !!, use sparingly if at all
println(bob.manager!!.name) // Will print "Alice", lucky duck
try {
println(alice.manager!!.name) // Please no
} catch (e: NullPointerException) {
println("Caught NullPointerException")
}

8.7

COROUTINES

If interoperating with Java, the Kotlin programmer has access to Java’s Thread class and
Runnable interface, and can thus write multithreaded code with Java platform threads—
albeit with a lighter syntax and a convenient thread() function for launching. However,
Kotlin provides its own lightweight concurrency primitive, the coroutine. Like those in
other languages we’ve seen, Kotlin coroutines can suspend and resume and communicate
and wait for each other; they are useful for generators, cooperative multitasking, and general
asynchronous activities. You’ll notice similarities with Java’s virtual threads, as they are
extremely lightweight and eﬀicient. We’ll provide only a simple example here, referring you
to the Kotlin Coroutines Guide [58] for a more in-depth look.
Here is a Kotlin version of the Java program in the last chapter for fetching Pokémon
information from an API, augmented with helpful log messages:
@file:DependsOn(
"org.jetbrains.kotlinx:kotlinx-coroutines-core:1.7.1",
"com.squareup.okhttp3:okhttp:4.11.0",
"org.json:json:20230618",
)
import kotlinx.coroutines.Dispatchers
import kotlinx.coroutines.launch
import kotlinx.coroutines.runBlocking
import okhttp3.OkHttpClient
import okhttp3.Request
import okhttp3.Response
import org.json.JSONObject
val baseUrl = "https://pokeapi.co/api/v2/pokemon/"
val pokemonNames = listOf("ditto", "pikachu", "mew", "weedle", "eevee")
data class PokeInfo(val name: String, val height: Int, val weight: Int)

Kotlin ■ 165

suspend fun fetchPokemon(name: String) {
println("Fetching $name on ${Thread.currentThread().name}")
val request = Request.Builder().url("$baseUrl$name").build()
val response = OkHttpClient().newCall(request).execute()
if (!response.isSuccessful) {
println("Cannot fetch $name: HTTP ${response.code}")
return
}
val jsonObject = JSONObject(response.body?.string() ?: return)
println(PokeInfo(
jsonObject.getString("name"),
jsonObject.getInt("height"),
jsonObject.getInt("weight")))
}
runBlocking {
for (name in pokemonNames) {
launch(Dispatchers.IO) {
fetchPokemon(name)
}
}
println("The fetches are underway")
}
println("The fetches are done")
In this script, runBlocking creates a coroutine and blocks its thread—the main thread,
since it is called at the top level—until completion. The function launch is also a coroutine
builder: it simply starts a new coroutine running without waiting for it to finish. Use launch
for “fire-and-forget” scenarios where the launched coroutine does not return anything. If you
do need something produced by a job, use the async coroutine builder to launch a coroutine
and immediately return a Deferred value (similar to a future or promise in other languages).
The deferred value has an await method which does a nonblocking wait for the deferred
value to become present.4 There’s an awaitAll method on sequences of deferred objects,
too.
The script launches a few coroutines, one for each Pokémon we wish to fetch from an
API. The coroutines invoke the suspendable function fetchPokemon. The first argument
to launch is the IO Dispatcher. Kotlin’s dispatchers determine which platform threads
coroutines run on. The built-in IO dispatcher has a thread pool specially designed to run
coroutines that spend a lot of time blocked on IO requests (e.g., waiting for file, database,
and network operations). You may notice that we never explicitly invoked a “join” operation
to wait for any of the launched coroutines to complete. Instead, we made use of Kotlin’s
structured concurrency feature: by defining the Pokemon-fetching coroutines inside an outer
coroutine, the outer one will automatically wait for its inner ones to finish.
One run of this program produced:
4 Note how async and await are not Kotlin keywords. The former is a function and the latter is a method
of the Deferred type.

166 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Fetching ditto on DefaultDispatcher-worker-1
Fetching pikachu on DefaultDispatcher-worker-3
Fetching mew on DefaultDispatcher-worker-2
Fetching weedle on DefaultDispatcher-worker-5
The fetches are underway
Fetching eevee on DefaultDispatcher-worker-4
PokeInfo(name=ditto, height=3, weight=40)
PokeInfo(name=weedle, height=3, weight=32)
PokeInfo(name=eevee, height=3, weight=65)
PokeInfo(name=pikachu, height=4, weight=60)
PokeInfo(name=mew, height=4, weight=40)
The fetches are done
As was our custom in this chapter, we’ve used Kotlin scripting, rather than fully-featured
Kotlin applications in order to illustrate language concepts. One obvious downside of Kotlin
scripting is apparent at the top of our script: hardcoded dependencies on external libraries.
See the Kotlin documentation for instructions on building applications with systems such
as Maven and Gradle that take care of dependency management and similar tasks.

KOTLIN WRAP UP
In this chapter, we were introduced to Kotlin. We learned that:
• Kotlin was influenced by Java and interoperates well with Java, but today runs on a
variety of platforms other than Java’s JVM.
• The language has a rich set of static types, including both signed and unsigned integers, floats, booleans, characters, and a variety of array, function, and object types.
In fact, numbers, booleans, and characters are objects, in the sense that methods may
be called on them.
• It is possible to declare an object without first declaring a class. Objects have properties and methods. Properties may have getters and setters.
• Functions are quite flexible: defaults, named arguments, and varargs are all supported.
To use functions and methods as values, the function reference operator (::) is needed.
Kotlin distinguishes anonymous functions from lambdas.
• Trailing closures allow for interesting expression forms.

• The class syntax is lighter than Java’s, with the primary constructor appearing in the
class header.
• Members of a class are public by default; the other modifiers are private, protected,
and internal.
• Classes must be marked open to be extended. Abstract and sealed classes are automatically open. (Interfaces are as well, though these were not covered in this chapter.)
• All classes are descendants of the class Any, which defines three methods that classes
can, and often do, override. Data classes automatically override them, and provide
autogenerated copy and component access methods as well.
• Extensions provide nice syntactic sugar.

Kotlin ■ 167
• Companion objects provide functionality that most languages tend to use “static” or
“type-level” members for.
• Kotlin pays a lot of attention to protecting you from nulls. To be able to use the value
null, you will need a nullable type. Operators like ?. and ?: make optional (nullable)
data relatively easy to work with.
• Coroutines are the preferred mechanism for concurrency. However, we covered a vanishingly small amount of what Kotlin offers in this area.
For further study in Kotlin, including language features not covered in this chapter as well
as links to articles, tutorials, oﬀicial documentation, and open-source projects using Kotlin,
see the Kotlin page in the book’s companion GitHub repository at https://github.com/
rtoal/ple/tree/main/kotlin.

EXERCISES
Now it’s your turn. Continue exploring Kotlin with the activities and research questions
below, and feel free to branch out on your own.
8.1 Locate the Kotlin home page and make your way to the docs page at https://
kotlinlang.org/docs/home.html. Once there, find and go through the Tour, the
Overview, and the Concepts sections. Make notes of language features not encountered
in this chapter.
8.2 Run the teaser program for generating Pythagorean triples on the oﬀicial Kotlin playground at https://play.kotlinlang.org/.
8.3 Install Kotlin on your local machine and run several of the scripts from this chapter.
8.4 Find out how to iterate through the UTF-8 bytes of a string.
8.5 Kotlin’s == operator on lists does a shallow comparison of each list element. How
do you compare the references to each list for sameness? How do you perform deep
copies?
8.6 In a Kotlin program, variables defined at the top level are called properties, the same
word used for certain members of objects. Why is this? Can top-level properties also
have getters and setters? Experiment with a small script of your own design.
8.7 Is the compiler happy with an attempt to assign a UInt to an Int-constrained variable?
An Int to a UInt? A Int to a Double?
8.8 Research the lateinit keyword and construct an example showing its use.
8.9 In this chapter, we saw an example of the spread operator used when passing arguments to a varargs parameter. Can the operator be used to construct lists, as it can
in JavaScript, Python, and Ruby?
8.10 Can named arguments appear before positional arguments in a call?
8.11 Can the function fun f() {println("ok")} be called in an expression? If so, what
value does it produce? If not, what error does the compiler report?
8.12 Find as many differences as you can between an anonymous function and a lambda
in Kotlin. Ask an AI chatbot to construct some interesting examples.

168 ■ Programming Language Explorations
8.13 Define a function called loop which takes one argument, a lambda, and executes it
repeatedly, forever. Show how calling your loop function with a trailing closure is
indistinguishable from adding a new loop statement to the language.
8.14 Research how and when functions are best defined with the inline keyword.
8.15 Research the degree to which arms of a when construct can be used to deconstruct
patterns, if at all.
8.16 In the Circle class example in this chapter, we were able to make area and
circumference properties. If we modify the class to have a mutable radius property,
would would have to rewrite these properties to be methods? Would any modifications
be required at all?
8.17 Add a few interesting methods to the Tree class from this chapter.
8.18 Extend the Tree class of this chapter to a binary search tree. Research will be required,
as you’ll need to learn Kotlin’s way of bounding the generic type to be comparable.
8.19 Write extensions for Int to (1) determine if the integer is prime and (2) produce the
string representation of the representation in any base between 2 and 36, inclusive.
8.20 Create a Kotlin class for two-dimensional vectors, similar to those we saw in the
chapters on Lua and Python, but add additional functionality to increase your practice. Note that Kotlin, like Lua and Python, treats operator invocations as sugar for
method class, so your vector addition operation will look like this:
operator fun plus(v: Vector): Vector {
return Vector(i + v.i, j + v.j)
}
Also try to use companion objects and infix methods where sensible.
8.21 Recall that the type T ? contains all and only the values of T plus the value null,
while the type Nothing contains no values at all. How exactly can you characterize
the type Nothing?? In particular, how many values belong to this type?
8.22 Port the restaurant simulation from the Java chapter to Kotlin. Do some research to
see in Kotlin’s channels are helpful in your task.
8.23 Learn how to build Kotlin command line applications with Gradle. Rewrite a handful
of scripts from this chapter as command line applications. Make sure to include the
Pokémon API example in your set of rewritten programs. Use the most up-to-date
dependencies.

CHAPTER

9

Julia

Julia is a modern language for scientific and technical computing.
First appeared 2012
Creator Jeff Bezanson, Stefan Karpinski, Viral B. Shah, Alan Edelman
Recognized for Multi-dispatch, Macros, Multicore support
Notable uses Data Science, Statistics, Numerical Analysis
Tags High-performance, Dynamic, Multi-dispatch
Six words or less A dynamic language for technical computing
Julia is targeted to high-performance numeric and scientific computing, where it competes
with Matlab, Python, R, and Fortran—languages with well-established and mature libraries.
While relatively new, Julia brings an impressive set of features not only of interest to the
statistics and data science communities (such as its math-friendly syntax, and support for
vectors and matrices), but also to those looking for an eﬀicient and powerful general-purpose
language.
In this chapter, we’ll tour this new language, beginning with our standard three introductory
programs, then dive right into Julia’s type system. We’ll see that Julia shares much in
common with TypeScript, having built-in support for abstract types, parametric types,
sum and product types, and subtyping. We’ll take the opportunity to review covariance,
contravariance, and invariance, which we previously discussed in our chapter on Java. As a
language designed with statistical computing, linear algebra, and data science applications
in mind, we will necessarily focus on Julia’s expressive and powerful array operations.
Next, we will introduce multiple dispatch, an important aspect of Julia’s support for
object-oriented programming. Previous languages on our tour select the appropriate method
to execute based on the type of a single receiver at run time, but Julia goes farther, selecting
based on the types of all of the method’s arguments. We’ll then look at metaprogramming
facilities, which go well beyond what we’ve seen in the previous chapters. Julia can introspect
not only objects and types, but also expressions and the internal structure of the program
itself. We’ll see hygienic macros for the first time. We’ll close with a look at the rather
elegant language support for parallel and distributed computing.

169

170 ■ Programming Language Explorations

9.1 HELLO JULIA
We’ll start with the usual right triangle script:
for c = 1:40, b = 1:c-1, a = 1:b-1
if a * a + b * b == c * c
println("$a, $b, $c")
end
end
Julia’s ranges (of the form x:y) have inclusive bounds, and string interpolation uses $. The
for-statement has a neat compact form that avoids the usual requirement of nested loops.
Heap’s algorithm follows:
function printpermutations(a, n)
if n <= 0
println(join(a, "\t"))
else
for i = 1:n-1
printpermutations(a, n-1)
local j = iseven(n) ? i : 1
a[j], a[n] = a[n], a[j]
end
printpermutations(a, n-1)
end
end
printpermutations(ARGS, length(ARGS))
Julia prefers length(a) to a.length; even join and split look more like top-level functions
than methods on arrays and strings—though they really are methods as we will soon see.
The familiar ternary conditional operator is present, and swaps are easy to write. And, like
Lua but unlike most other languages, the lower bound of arrays is 1.
Next is our data science teaser:
using Printf
struct Player
team::String
name::String
games::Int
ppg::Float64
end
function Player(line::Vector{SubString{String}})
points = parse(Float64, line[4])
games = parse(Int, line[3])
return Player(line[1], line[2], games, points / games)
end

Julia ■ 171

report(p::Player) = @sprintf("%-22s%-4s%8.2f", p.name, p.team, p.ppg)
players = Vector{Player}()
for line in eachline(stdin)
player = Player(split(line, ","))
if player.games >= 15
push!(players, player)
end
end
sort!(players, by=player -> player.ppg, rev=true)
for player in players[1:10]
println(report(player))
end
The expression eachline(stdin) gives us a line-by-line iterator over standard input. We
split each line into a vector of substrings which we use to construct a player object. Julia does
not define class constructs with nested data fields and methods. Instead, a struct defines
fields and an autogenerated constructor that takes one parameter per field. Additional
constructors are just functions with the name of the struct. “Methods” are simply functions
that take an object of the type as a parameter. We’ve defined report with the shorthand
syntax. The script also highlights Julia’s syntax for range-based indexing, named arguments
in calls, and the convention for using ! as the final character of a mutating function.

9.2 THE BASICS
From our introductory programs, you may have noticed Julia looks a little like Lua. Structured statements are keyword terminated (e.g., while...end, for...end, if...elseif...end,
try...catch...end), indexes start at 1 instead of 0, local variables are introduced with the
local keyword, and there is no class keyword. Julia’s coroutines are similar to Lua’s,
too. But Julia adds some nice syntactic forms. Multiplication can sometimes use juxtaposition, functions can be anonymous (->), composed (◦) and piped (|>), and operators can
be broadcast by prefixing with a dot (.):
@assert (5√9, 17 / 5, 17 ÷ 5) == (15, 3.4, 3)
@assert iseven(sign(log2(exponent(8)))) == false
@assert 8 |> exponent |> log2 |> sign |> iseven == false
@assert [1,2,3] |> c -> map(x -> 3x+1, c) == [4, 7, 10]
@assert 5 .+ [2, 8, -2] == [7, 13, 3]
@assert [8, 13, 5] .^ 2 == [64, 169, 25]
@assert .√([9, 16, 25]) == [3, 4, 5]
@assert (1:9 .|> x -> x^2) |> filter(iseven) |> sum == 120
In addition to function...end, we have two alternate ways to define functions:
f(x, y) = 2x + y
g = x -> x * x

# call-like form on left-hand side
# arrow notation

172 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Functions can have optional and keyword parameters. You can omit the keyword return
when returning the last expression in a function (as in Ruby):
function f(a, b=1 ; c=2, d=3)
[a, b, c, d]
end
@assert f(100) == [100, 1, 2, 3]
@assert f(100, 200) == [100, 200, 2, 3]
@assert f(100, d=10) == [100, 1, 2, 10]
@assert f(100, 200, d=400, c=300) == [100, 200, 300, 400]
A semicolon separates the positional and keyword parameters, and keyword parameters
must be given default values (in other words, all keyword parameters are also optional).
Julia uses three dots (...) for packing and unpacking. Positional parameters pack and
unpack into iterable collections of values; keyword parameters pack and unpack into arrays
of (symbol, value) pairs:
f(x, y...) = y
@assert f(1, 2, 3) == (2, 3)
g(x; y...) = collect(y)
@assert g(1, a=2, b=5) == [:a => 2, :b => 5]
Rather than having only global or function scopes, Julia’s scoping regions coincide with
the following constructs: function bodies, while and for loops, and blocks introduced with
try, catch, finally, let, and type. Variables introduced in a scope are visible to nested
scopes, and shadowing is permitted. We introduce new variables into the current scope
with local or const; function parameters are automatically made part of the function
body scope. If you assign to a variable without marking it local, a new local is introduced
in the current scope unless it has been marked local or global in an enclosing scope.
Marking a variable global is required to allow writing to a global variable in an “inner”
(nonglobal) scope.
a, b, c = 1, 2, 3
# three globals
(function ()
a = 10
# introduces local and shadows
global b = 20
# overwrites global
d = 10c + 10
# new local, reading global
local e = 5
while true
e = 50
# outer e because marked local
f = 60
# local to while loop!
break
end
@assert (a,b,c,d,e) == (10,20,3,40,50)
@assert (try f catch e sqrt(100) end) == 10
end)()
@assert (a,b,c) == (1,20,3)

Julia ■ 173
All of Julia’s types are available at run time, and are fully first-class: you can assign type
values to variables and pass them to and return them from functions. There’s even a type
called Type). Types are organized into a hierarchy (though single-inheritance only) with
Any as the top type. As Julia is designed for scientific computing, rational and complex
numeric types are built in. Optionals are “split” into separate types for Some and Nothing.
A part of the hierarchy of Julia’s built-in types is shown in Figure 9.1.

A few of the built-in Julia types

Julia does not require type annotations, but you are free to provide them. All assignments
of a value to a variable with a type annotation will be type-converted to the annotated type
if possible. (In Julia, a value v is converted to type T by calling convert(T,v).)
x::Int64 = 0x02
y = 0x02
@assert typeof(x) == Int64
@assert typeof(y) == UInt8

# The UInt8 was converted
# No annotations, no coversion

# x = "Oh no" # ERROR: Cannot convert String to Int64
Type annotations may increase performance: a compiler can use knowledge that a variable
has a given type to create an eﬀicient memory layout and run-time access code.
Every type in Julia is either abstract or concrete. Every value has a single concrete type;
for example, 2.54 has the type Float64. Let’s explore a few more:

174 ■ Programming Language Explorations
@assert typeof(3) == Int64
@assert typeof(0xFA31) == UInt16
@assert typeof(false) == Bool
@assert typeof('\u263a') == Char
@assert typeof("Hello") == String
@assert typeof(BigInt(72)^3897) == BigInt
@assert typeof(:hello) == Symbol
@assert typeof(nothing) == Nothing
@assert typeof(Float64) == DataType
@assert typeof(DataType) == DataType
Abstract types do nothing more than generalize other types; for example, the abstract
type AbstractFloat generalizes the four concrete types Float16, Float32, Float64, and
BigFloat. Calling typeof will never produce an abstract type, but you can use isa to check
“membership” in an abstract type:
@assert typeof(88) == Int64
for t in [Int64, Integer, Signed, Real, Number, Any]
@assert isa(88, t)
end
In Julia, only abstract types can have subtypes; in fact, in Figure 9.1, all leaf types are
concrete and all nonleaf types are abstract.1 We can use the language itself to explore type
relationships, with supertype producing the supertype of a type, and the <: operator,
which determines whether one type is a descendant of another.
@assert supertype(Int32) == Signed
@assert supertype(Symbol) == Any
@assert supertype(DataType) == Type
@assert Float64 <: Real
@assert BigInt <: Number
Let’s implement our own types. We’ll do so by directly translating our little animals script
from earlier chapters. We create an abstract type Animal, and three concrete subtypes. As
before, all animals speak the same way, but each makes its own sound:
abstract type Animal end
speak(a::Animal) = "$(a.name) says $(sound(a))"
struct Horse <: Animal
name
end
sound(h::Horse) = "neigh"
struct Cow <: Animal
name
end
sound(c::Cow) = "moooo"
1 This is not the case in every language. Plenty of other languages allow concrete types to have subtypes.

Julia ■ 175

struct Sheep <: Animal
name
end
sound(s::Sheep) = "baaaa"
s = Horse("CJ")
@assert speak(s) == "CJ says neigh"
c = Cow("Bessie")
@assert speak(c) == "Bessie says moooo"
@assert speak(Sheep("Little Lamb")) == "Little Lamb says baaaa"
Notice the lack of class constructs with nested speak and sound definitions. The distinct
sound implementations are tied to a type via type annotations on the parameter. In Julia’s
terminology, sound is a generic function with three distinct methods. The function
methods reports the methods for a generic function, available to us in the REPL:
julia> sound
sound (generic function with 3 methods)
julia> methods(sound)
# 3 methods for generic function "sound":
sound(h::Horse)
sound(c::Cow)
sound(s::Sheep)
Julia types can be parameterized by other types and certain other values. We can get a feel
for these types by studying a few examples:
@assert typeof(1:10) == UnitRange{Int64}
@assert typeof(0x5 // 0x22) == Rational{UInt8}
@assert typeof(5 // 34) == Rational{Int64}
@assert typeof(8.75im) == Complex{Float64}
@assert typeof([5,3]) == Array{Int64,1}
@assert typeof([3, "abc"]) == Array{Any, 1}
@assert typeof([]) == Array{Any, 1}
@assert typeof([1 0; 0 1]) == Array{Int64, 2}
@assert typeof(Set(4)) == Set{Int64}
@assert typeof(Set(['3', '$'])) == Set{Char}
To distinguish sets of integers from sets of characters, Julia defines a parametric type
called Set{T} whose parameter T can be any type. Type parameters can have restrictions
as well. Rational numbers, for example, can have UInt8 or Int64 components but not
string components, so Julia provides the type Rational{T<:Integer}, restricting T to a
descendant type of Integer. You’ll notice several families of parametric types in Julia,
including those for sets, arrays, ranges, and complex numbers.
Generally, nonparameterized types are instances of DataType; parameterized types are
instances of UnionAll. UnionAll and DataType are nonparameterized and instances of
DataType. They are also subtypes of Type{T}, which is parametric and thus an instance of
UnionAll:

176 ■ Programming Language Explorations
@assert typeof(Set) == UnionAll
@assert typeof(Set{String}) == DataType
@assert typeof(DataType) == DataType
@assert typeof(UnionAll) == DataType
@assert typeof(Type) == UnionAll
@assert typeof(Type{UInt128}) == DataType
Like TypeScript, Julia features unions and tuples. For example, the type Union{UInt8, Bool}
contains the values 0, 1, ... , 254, 255, false, true; the type Tuple{UInt8, Bool}
contains the values (0,false), (0,true), (1,false), (1,true), ... (255,false),
(255,true).
u = Union{UInt8, Bool}
@assert typeof(u) == Union
@assert isa(u, Type)

# A new type

@assert isa(0x08, u)
@assert isa(false, u)
@assert !isa(256, u)

# UInt8 values belong
# Boolean values belong
# Not a UInt8, does not belong

t = Tuple{UInt8, Bool}
@assert typeof(t) == DataType
@assert isa(t, Type)

# a tuple type
# tuple types aren't special
# tuple types are types

@assert isa((0x7A, true), t)
@assert !isa((3, "wrong"), t)
@assert !isa(0x25, t)

# tuples of the right type belong
# tuples of the wrong type do not
# non-tuples do not belong

# A union type is a type

Unions and tuples are not limited to two constituent types:
# 3 component types
@assert typeof((4, false, [])) == Tuple{Int64, Bool, Array{Any, 1}}
@assert isa(false, Union{Int64, Bool, Array{Any, 1}})
# 1 component type
@assert typeof((5,)) == Tuple{Int64}
@assert isa(false, Union{Bool})
@assert Bool != Tuple{Bool}
@assert Bool == Union{Bool}

# Because false != (false,)
# Do you see why?

# No component types
@assert typeof(()) == Tuple{}
Union types are also called sum types and tuples may be known as product types.
These terms can be remembered by noting that from the 256 values in UInt8 and 2 in
Bool, the union (sum) type has 256 + 2 = 258 values while the tuple (product) type has
256 × 2 = 512.

Julia ■ 177
The type Union{} has no instances while Tuple{} has exactly one, namely (), the zeroelement tuple. In type theory, the type of no elements is called a bottom type. A top type,
conversely, contains all possible values. Julia’s top type is Any.

9.3 MULTIDIMENSIONAL ARRAYS
Julia’s array type is parameterized not only by the type of its elements but by its dimension. So the type of one-dimensional integer arrays differs from the type of two-dimensional
integer arrays. Here are some examples of arrays:



10
 20 

a=
 30 
40

b=

[

5 3

7

]

c=

[

1 0
0 1

9
6

]

Here a is written in Julia as [10,20,30,40] and is a one-dimensional array, or vector, of
type Array{Int64,1}. Array b is written [5 3 7] (note spaces instead of commas), and is a
1 × 3 (one row, three columns) two-dimensional array, or matrix, of type Array{Int64,2}.
Array c is a 2 × 3 array, also with type Array{Int64,2}, and is written [1 0 9; 0 1 6].
Higher dimensional arrays have no special syntax, though they can be constructed with
comprehensions, e.g., [i+j+k for i=1:4, j=1:3, k=1:5].
In an n-dimensional array, the expression a[e1 , e2 , ...en ] will select a single element from the
array when each of the indexes ei are integers. However, the index expressions can also be
ranges or vectors, allowing some very interesting slices to be computed:
a = [11 12 13 14; 21 22 23 24; 31 32 33 34]
@assert a[2,3] == 23
# element in row 2, col 3
@assert a[2,2:4] == [22,23,24]
# row 2, cols 2 through 4
@assert a[1:3,3] == [13; 23; 33]
# rows 1 through 3 of col 3
@assert a[3,1:end] == [31,32,33,34]
# row 3, all elements
@assert a[3,:] == [31,32,33,34]
# row 3, all elements
@assert a[2,[1;3;4]] == [21,23,24]
# row 2, cols 1, 3, 4
@assert a[[1;3],[1;4]] == [11 14; 31 34] # very disjointed subarray
@assert a[7] == 13
# enumerates by columns
Julia has hundreds of built-in methods for processing arrays, including length(A) for the
number of elements in A, ndims(A) for the number of dimensions, and size(A) for a tuple
of its dimensions. Some construct new arrays:
@assert zeros(Int64, 3) == [0, 0, 0]
@assert zeros(Int64, 2, 2) == [0 0; 0 0]
@assert ones(Int64, 3, 2) == [1 1; 1 1; 1 1]
@assert fill(5, 1, 4) == [5 5 5 5]
@assert transpose([1 3; 2 4]) == [1 2; 3 4]
@assert [1 3; 2 4]' == [1 2; 3 4]
# postfix ' transposes
@assert fill(10, 3, 1) == [10 10 10]'
# 2d column array!
@assert [2x for x in 1:5] == [2,4,6,8,10]

178 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Many of Julia’s unary and binary operators can be “vectorized” to broadcast to each
element of an array (of any dimension).
a = [1 2 3; 4 5 6; 7 8 9]
b = [1 0 0; 0 2 0; 9 9 9]
@assert iseven.(a) == [
false true false; true false true; false true false]
successor(n) = n + 1
@assert successor.(a) == [2 3 4; 5 6 7; 8 9 10]
@assert a .+ b == [2 2 3; 4 7 6; 16 17 18]
@assert a .* b == [1 0 0; 0 10 0; 63 72 81]
@assert a .^ b == [1 1 1; 1 25 1; 40353607 134217728 387420489]
@assert a .* 3 == [3 6 9; 12 15 18; 21 24 27]
@assert a .- 1 == [0 1 2; 3 4 5; 6 7 8]
@assert a ./ 2 == [0.5 1.0 1.5; 2.0 2.5 3.0; 3.5 4.0 4.5]
@assert a .^ 2 == [1 4 9; 16 25 36; 49 64 81]
@assert 1 .- b == [0 1 1; 1 -1 1; -8 -8 -8]
@assert 3 .* a .^ 2 .+ successor.(a) == [
5 15 31; 53 81 115; 155 201 253]
In some cases, operators have been overloaded to perform a broadcast; however, in cases
where the operator is conventionally understood to have a different meaning when applied to
matrices, the conventional meaning is used. Matrix multiplication is a good example:
a = [1 2 3; 4 5 6; 7 8 9]
b = [1 0 0; 0 2 0; 9 9 9]
# Addition has been overloaded to be elementwise
@assert a + b == [2 2 3; 4 7 6; 16 17 18]
# Multiplication, however, is matrix multiplication
@assert a * b == [28 31 27; 58 64 54; 88 97 81]
Julia has a vast number of built-in functions and standard library functions useful in the field
of linear algebra, such as tr (trace), det (determinant), and inv (inverse), as well as cross
product, eigenvalue computation, Hessenberg and singular value decompositions, Givens
rotations, transpositions, triangles, diagonalizations, and a large number of factorizations.
Dozens more methods are included in additional standard and third-party packages. The
following is but of taste of what is available:
using LinearAlgebra
a = [2 -1 0; 1 1 1; 0 1 2]
b = [2 1 0; -1 1 -1; 0 -1 2]
u = [1, 2, 3]
v = [1, 0, 0]

Julia ■ 179
@assert tr(a) == 5
@assert det(a) == 4.0
@assert cross(u, v) == [0, 3, -2]
@assert UnitUpperTriangular(a) == [1 -1 0; 0 1 1; 0 0 1]
@assert UpperHessenberg(a) == [2 -1 0; 1 1 1; 0 1 2]
@assert dot(a, b) == 5
rotate!(u, v, 3, 8)
reflect!(v, u, 1, 10)
@assert u == [-61, -166, -249]
@assert v == [105, 44, 66]

# ! indicates mutation of u and v
# ! indicates mutation of u and v

9.4 MULTIPLE DISPATCH
Earlier in this chapter we built a little type hierarchy of animals, horses, sheep, and cows,
and showed how Julia’s method of dispatching—selecting the proper function or method to
invoke among a collection of operations with the same name—was not via a specially marked
receiver object, but rather on the type of argument of the callee. Does this idea extend to
multiple arguments? For example, could we construct a function times such that:
• If x and y are numbers, the function multiplies, e.g., times(8,3) ⇒ 24

• If x is a string and y is an unsigned integer, we have repetition, e.g., times("ho",3)
⇒ "hohoho"
• If x is a vector and y is a number, we have element-wise multiplication, e.g.,
times([1,2,3],3) ⇒ [3,6,9]

• If x is a number and y is a vector, we also have element-wise multiplication, e.g.,
times(5,[1,2,3]) ⇒ [5,10,15]

Doing so would certainly be better than writing a single function that checks the types of
its arguments in a multi-way if-statement, an approach that would be diﬀicult to read, mix
of disparate concerns, and be impossible of extensibility without modifying existing source
code. The ability to dispatch on multiple arguments, known as multiple dispatch would
also be superior to writing four functions with distinct names. It would also feel rather nice,
especially since previous languages with single dispatch force us to choose which type a
method “belongs to.” and this is not always obvious.
Julia, in fact, does allow multiple dispatch. In fact all arguments in a call are considered.
So we can write:
times(x::Number, y::Number) = x * y
times(x::AbstractArray{T,1}, n::Number) where {T<:Number} = x .* n
times(n::Number, x::AbstractArray{T,1}) where {T<:Number} = x .* n
times(s::AbstractString, n::Unsigned) = repeat(s, n)
@assert times(5, -2.5) == -12.5
@assert times([1.0, 5, 2//3], 3) == [3.0, 15, 2]
@assert times(3, [1.0, 5, 0]) == [3.0, 15, 0]
@assert times("ho", UInt64(3)) == "hohoho"
Here times is a generic function with four methods.

180 ■ Programming Language Explorations
The first call in our example dispatches to the first method, because the argument types
(Int64, Float64) can be passed to the parameter tuple (Number, Number). But notice
the definition of the second method. It is tempting to define its first parameter as an AbstractArray{Number, 1}, but this will not work! Passing an array of Float64 objects to an
array of numbers fails because, unlike tuples, Julia arrays are not covariant on their element
type.2 Julia’s parametric methods rescue us here. We introduce a type parameter T and
define the method’s first parameter as an array of elements whose type is Number or any
descendant type of Number.

9.5

METAPROGRAMMING

We’ve seen a few introspective capabilities in previous chapters, such as asking a Python
or Ruby class for a list of its methods, or asking a JavaScript function for the number of
arguments it accepts. Julia has dozens of such functions, including supertypes, operator
<:, typeof, and isa (seen before), as well as fieldnames (of a datatype), names (of a
module), and methods (to list the methods of a generic function). The surprising functions
code_llvm and code_native will show you the intermediate and target assembly language
code for a method.
But Julia goes even farther: individual expressions, in their unevaluated form, can be accessed and manipulated as objects within the program itself. There are several ways to
create expression objects:
e1 = Meta.parse("x * sqrt(9) - 5")
e2 = :(x * sqrt(9) - 5)
e3 = Expr(:call, :-, Expr(:call, :*, :x, Expr(:call, :sqrt, 9)), 5)
@assert e1 == e2 && e2 == e3 && typeof(e1) == Expr
Objects of type Expr have three fields: head, args, and typ. The internal structure of the
expression in the previous example (without the typ fields) is shown in Figure 9.2.
Unevaluated expressions (Expr objects) are also known as quoted expressions, named after
the way we use quotes in prose to distinguish a mention of a word from its use: we say
that ice is frozen water but “ice” has three letters. To make things interesting, Julia allows
evaluation during the construction of a quoted expression, via splicing (intentionally using
the same syntax for string interpolation we saw earlier):
y = 10
e = :(x + $(y / 2))
@assert e == :(x + 5)

# y/2 evaluated here

Access to unevaluated expressions allows us to rewrite code before evaluation, sometimes to
optimize, sometimes to enhance (by adding, for example, timing, logging, or persistence),
and sometimes to extend the language with new syntactic forms. While it is possible in
theory to write functions that accept expressions as arguments and return expressions,
Julia provides macros to more elegantly manipulate expressions. Macros are evaluated at
2 Covariance was discussed in previous chapters on TypeScript and Java, languages that both feature
covariant arrays

Julia ■ 181
head

:call

args

head

:call

args

head

:call

args
Figure 9.2

3

1
:-

2

1
:*

2
:x

3

1
:sqrt

2

5

9

Internal structure of the Julia Expression :(x*sqrt(9)-5)

the time the script is parsed, and return expressions that are then compiled into the context
in which they are called. For example, the following script
x = 2
macro tenTimesX()
println("hello");
:(x * 10)
end
function f()
@tenTimesX()
end

# executed at parse time
# expression returned at parse time

# replaced with x*10 at parse time
# when called, f executes x=2; x*10

prints a message, even though the function f is never called! While parsing the script, Julia
executes the macro call within f (printing a message). The returned expression, :(x * 10),
can then be compiled into the body of f . This rewrites the function to:
function f()
x = 2
x * 10
end
Julia takes care that writable variables in a macro’s result expression do not modify variables
of the same name in the caller’s context. In technical terms, Julia macros are hygienic. In
the following example, the macro’s own local y does not overwrite the caller’s y:

182 ■ Programming Language Explorations
x = 2
macro tenTimesX()
:(y = 10; x * y)
end
(function ()
y = 3
z = @tenTimesX()
@assert y == 3 && z == 20
end)()

# Will this y be changed?
# Changed to 10 perhaps?
# No, y will not change

We can use the function macroexpand in the REPL to see why no change occurs:
julia> macro tenTimesX()
:(y = 10; x * y)
end
julia> macroexpand(:((x,y) = (3,2); @tenTimesX()))
quote
(x,y) = (3,2)
begin
#1#y = 10
x * #1#y
end
end
During macroexpansion, a new symbol, #1#y, was used in place of the variable y, avoiding a
name clash. Julia will generate new names for variables that are (1) explicitly marked local,
(2) assigned to but not marked global, or (3) used a function argument. Other variables are
resolved in the context of the macro definition, so the macro’s use of well-known globals
will not be overwritten by names the caller might have inadvertently redefined.
There are times, however, when we do wish a macro to manipulate variables from the caller’s
context. This is convenient when making use of macros’ ability to “extend” the language
with what appear to be new syntactic forms—a benefit of macros’ ability to take arguments
as unevaluated expressions, which, for convenience, are never quoted. Let’s create and test
an unless statement. We want the body of our generated expression to evaluate variables in
the caller’s context, so we suppress the hygienic generation of new variables with esc:
macro unless(condition, body)
:(if !$condition
$(esc(body))
end)
end
x, y = 0, 1
@unless (x == 5) begin
y = 3
end

# should execute

Julia ■ 183
@unless (x == 0) begin
y = 5
end

# should not execute

@assert y == 3
This macro rewrites the code fragment
@unless (x < 5) begin y = 2 end
into
if !(x < 5); y = 2; end
Without esc the macro would have rewritten our code fragment into:
if !(x < 5); #2#y = 2; end
The esc function explicitly bypasses hygiene: Escaped expressions are compiled directly
into the output without any new symbols being generated.

9.6

PARALLEL COMPUTING

To be successful as a language for scientific computing, a language must support eﬀicient
execution over large datasets; it should take advantage of processors with multiple cores
or multiple computers in a cluster. Running related computations on multiple processing
units simultaneously is called parallel programming. When multiple independent processes communicate exclusively via message passing (as opposed to shared memory), we
have distributed computing. Julia provides both primitives and high-level constructs for
computing across multiple independent processes.
A Julia remote call invokes a function asynchronously on a specific process, returning
immediately with a remote reference, or future. Calling fetch on the remote reference
will wait for the function to complete on the remote process, and produce the function’s
result. The following script creates three new processes,3 and instructs process #4 to factor
a large number:4
using Distributed
addprocs(3)
@assert nprocs() == 4
@everywhere import Primes
using Primes
future = remotecall(factor, 2, 21883298135690819)
@assert isa(future, Future)

3 Calling addprocs with an integer argument creates processors on the local machine to take advantage
of multiple cores. There are other variants of addprocs to add processors on remote machines either via
user-host-port strings or cluster managers; we will not cover these alternatives in this text.
4 The factor function resides in the package Primes. You must first install this package by executing
Pkg.add("Primes") in an interactive shell, or julia -e 'Pkg.add("Primes")' from the command line.

184 ■ Programming Language Explorations
factors = fetch(future)
@assert collect(factors) == [93234719=>1,234711901=>1]
The @spawn macro is often used in place of remotecall; it not only takes advantage of a
clearer syntax (since macros take unevaluated expressions as arguments), but it chooses a
process for you:
using Distributed
addprocs(3)
@everywhere import Primes
ref = @spawn Primes.factor(21883298135690819)
factors = fetch(ref)
@assert collect(factors) == [93234719=>1, 234711901=>1]
Between the remote call and the fetching of the result, the calling process is free to do other
work.
A common example of parallel programming across multiple cores computes an approximation of π by generating random points in the square (0, 0)...(1, 1). Counting the number of
random points within the inscribed arc (the points (x, y) for which x2 +y 2 ≤ 1 in Figure 9.3)
divided by the total number of points within the square approximates the value π4 . Let’s
generate a million random points in parallel on three cores:
(1,1)

1

0

1

Figure 9.3

Approximation of π4

using Distributed
addprocs(3)
@everywhere function approximate_pi(trials::Int64)
hits = 0
for i in 1:trials
if sum(rand(2).^2) < 1
hits += 1
end
end

Julia ■ 185

end

hits/trials * 4

references = [@spawn approximate_pi(1000000) for i=1:3]
approximation = sum([fetch(ref) for ref in references])/3
@assert 3.1 < approximation < 3.2
Here the @everywhere macro ensures our function is available to each process. We spawn
three executions on each of the three cores, then fetch and average the results. But we can
do a bit better, letting Julia do most of the work with its @parallel macro:
using Distributed
addprocs(3)
trials = 4000000
hits = @distributed (+) for i = 1:trials
Int(sum(rand(2).^2) < 1)
end
approximation = hits/trials * 4
@assert(3.1 < approximation < 3.2)
The parallel for-loop partitions the range over all of the available workers (all processes
except the “main” process) and applies the reduction function (here we’ve used +) locally
within each process. The calling process applies the function again to each process’s result.
In addition to support for parallel and distributed execution, Julia offers other concurrent
features that we won’t cover here, including coroutines and channels. We’ve seen coroutines
before, and we’ll visit channels later in the book when we visit Rust and Go.

JULIA WRAP UP
In this chapter, we were introduced to Julia. We learned that:
• Julia was designed for applications in data science, numerical analysis, and scientific
computing.
• Julia features inclusive ranges and array bounds starting at 1, rather than 0.

• In function declarations, positional parameters come first; keyword parameters, if any,
are separated from the positional parameters with a semicolon. Positional parameters
may have defaults; keyword arguments must have defaults.
• Scopes coincide with function bodies, while and for loops, and blocks introduced
with try, catch, finally, let, and type. The construct begin...end in particular, is
not a scope. Variables introduced in a comprehension are scoped to each iteration.
• All Julia types reside in a single supertype-subtype hierarchy with Any at the top.
Each type can have at most one supertype. Types can be abstract or concrete. Only
abstract types can have subtypes. Types are first-class objects, and the type system
is easy to introspect via methods such as super, subtypes, isa, typeof, and the
operator <:.

186 ■ Programming Language Explorations
• Types can be parameterized. Parameterized collection types in Julia are invariant.
• Julia features sum (Union) and product (Tuple) types. Tuples are covariant.

• Type annotations provide the foundation for multiple-dispatch, in which methods are
selected based on the types of all of a function’s arguments. Annotations are also
frequently employed to improve performance.
• Julia expressions can be manipulated and modified before evaluation, enabling sophisticated forms of metaprogramming.
• Macros are executed when code is parsed, that is, before it is run. Macros return expressions which are compiled into the context of the call. Macros take their arguments
in unevaluated form, without the need to explicitly quote them.
• Julia provides many features for parallel and concurrent programming. Processes can
be created to take advantage of multiple cores on a processor or multiple processors
in a cluster.
• The low-level constructs for parallel programming are remote calls and remote references. Various macros, such as @spawn, @fetch, and @parallel provide a higher-level
programming model.
For further study in Julia, including language features not covered in this chapter as well
as links to articles, tutorials, oﬀicial documentation, and open-source projects using Julia,
see the Julia page in the book’s companion GitHub repository at https://github.com/
rtoal/ple/tree/main/julia.
