collatz:{(1<){:[x!2;1+3*x;_ x%2]}\x}

16.2.4

Io

Steve Dekorte’s Io is another simple, “pure” language, whose programs consist entirely of
objects sending messages. Dekorte created Io in 2002 “to refocus attention on expressiveness
by exploring higher level dynamic programming features with greater levels of runtime
flexibility and simplified programming syntax and semantics.” [21]
Like JavaScript and Lua, Io is a prototype-based language. In Io, all computation, including
assignment, conditionals, and loops, proceeds by sending messages. All objects are run-time
accessible, as is the source code tree (yes, metaprogramming opportunities abound). The
language provides actors, futures, and coroutines for concurrency. Io has much to offer and
we can’t do the language justice in this short section, but we encourage you to explore the
examples at the book’s companion website.

342 ■ Programming Language Explorations

16.2.5

Factor

Although this book’s main tour managed to visit a number of programming paradigms,
we’ve up to now missed concatenative programming. Concatenative programing languages build subroutines for evaluation by composing functions.Factor is a modern concatenative language—PostScript, Joy, and Forth are three others—and like many, but not
all, concatenative languages, it is also stack-based. It was created by Slava Pestov in
2003.
In Factor, as in other stack-based languages, each operation takes its implicit operands from
a stack. Basic arithmetic has a postfix flavor:
9 7 * 2 + 13 neg /
Operands generate pushes. Operators pop values, compute, then push results:
1. Push 9, Stack = ⟨9⟩
2. Push 7, Stack = ⟨9 7⟩
3. Pop operands, push product, Stack = ⟨63⟩
4. Push 2, Stack = ⟨63 2⟩
5. Add, Stack = ⟨65⟩
6. Push 13, Stack = ⟨65 13⟩
7. Negate, Stack = ⟨63 − 13⟩
8. Divide, Stack = ⟨−5⟩

The stack can hold code blocks, too, as in:
3 [ "Hello, world" print ] times

Developers and students of computer science will do well to study stack-based execution.
There is a long history of virtual machines using stack execution, including the very popular
Java Virtual Machine (JVM).

16.3

SCRIPTING

In a 1998 article [83], John Ousterhout identified a class of languages he termed scripting
languages, remarking:
Scripting languages assume that a collection of useful components already exist
in other languages. They are intended not for writing applications from scratch
but rather for combining components. For example, Tcl and Visual Basic can be
used to arrange collections of user interface controls on the screen, and Unix shell
scripts are used to assemble filter programs into pipelines. Scripting languages
...are rarely used for complex algorithms and data structures, which are usually
provided by the components.
Today, the notion of what defines a scripting language is much more fluid, at times encompassing any language with suﬀiciently dynamic, high-level, expressive features. We’re
interested here in two classic exemplars: Bash and Perl, as well as the popular data science
language, R. While each can be used for general-purpose computing, each definitely exhibit
powerful and expressive features to excel in their target domains.

Additional Languages ■ 343

16.3.1

Bash

Today, operating system shells do much more than simply invoke and combine programs.
Even the earliest of the Unix shells, the Bourne Shell, colloquially known as sh, provided
variables, rudimentary control flow, and command substitution. Bash, created in 1989 as
a replacement and extension of sh, added additional looping and conditional constructs,
unbounded arrays, new forms of command substitutions, and support for in-process arithmetic. It competes with a number of other shell languages that have extended sh, including
tcsh, ksh, and the default shell for macOS, zsh.

16.3.2

Perl

According to its designer, Larry Wall, Perl has two slogans: “There’s more than one way
to do it,” (or TMTOWTDI) and “Easy things should be easy and hard things should be
possible.” [113] Created in 1987, Perl rose to fame in the early days of the World Wide Web
due to its extraordinarily powerful text processing capabilities.
Perl continues to hold on to a fair share of popularity in the scripting world, where it
competes with Python, Ruby, Tcl, and others. It is, to some old timers, the first language
that comes to mind when hearing the phrase regular expressions. While Perl was not the
first to use regular expressions, it likely popularized them like no other. Its extensive regex
support inspired the popular PCRE (Perl Compatible Regular Expressions) library.
Perl has a rather unique culture in the programming language space. The language is very
flexible and supports a great many programming styles. Wall’s training as a linguist led
to many of the language’s highly expressive—and sometimes quirky—features, including
various quoting mechanisms, a flip-flop operator, a spaceship operator, the diamond, and
the tilde-tilde. Wall has even come up with a self-deprecating backronym for the language:
Pathologically Eclectic Rubbish Lister.

16.3.3

R

R, designed by Ross Ihaka and Robert Gentleman (hence the name, R), is an implementation
of the S programming language inspired by the lexical scoping semantics of Scheme.
R excels in statistical modeling applications thanks to its extensive set of core (and usercontributed) packages and direct support for matrix arithmetic. For a taste of the language,
this small script hardcodes the number of bedrooms and price of seven homes, and displays
a scatter plot with a linear regression analysis as well as a histogram of prices.
data <- data.frame(
x = c(2, 2, 2.5, 3, 4, 5, 5, 5),
y = c(90, 83, 125, 150, 144, 200, 250, 300)
)
model = lm(y ~ x, data = data)
plot(data$x, data$y, main = "Prices", xlab = "Rooms", ylab = "Price")
abline(model, col = "red")
hist(data$y, main = "Prices", col = "green")

344 ■ Programming Language Explorations

16.4

THE ML FAMILY

While working on the theorem proving system LCF (short for Logic of Computable Functions), Robin Milner and colleagues in the early 1970s created a language for writing scripts
to create proofs. Since LCF was itself a language for representing formulas in an underlying logic, the theorem-proving language was called the Meta Language, or ML for short.
The original ML language soon evolved into a general-purpose programming language. Like
Lisp, it featured first-class functions and pattern matching, and was expression-oriented and
garbage-collected. Unlike Lisp, ML was statically typed, and was the first language to implement the Hindley-Milner type system and its associated type inference mechanism.
A number of ML dialects soon appeared, with the most popular family branches being led
by Standard ML and OCaml. Several closely-related languages such as F#, PureScript, and
Idris include the characteristic features of ML mentioned above that we consider them, at
least for this book, to be part of the (extended) ML family.

16.4.1

Standard ML

In April of 1983, Milner authored A Proposal for Standard ML to advocate for a language
that would consolidate the strengths of the multiple ML dialects created to that time. The
new language appeared shortly after, with a highly sophisticated module system in which (1)
structures define (parameterized) collections of types and values, (2) signatures provide
interfaces into structures, often providing a restricted view of the structure to clients, and
(3) functors can compute new structures, giving an interesting computational view into
generic instantiation, among its other capabilities. A complete example with structures,
signatures, and functors is available at the companion website.

16.4.2

OCaml

While Milner’s efforts to consolidate the disparate ML dialects into a single standard had
some success, one family branch—that of INRIA’s CAML—did not join the party. By 1996,
CAML had evolved into OCaml, also known as Objective Caml.
OCaml and Standard ML are very similar languages, inheriting all of the pattern-matching,
type inference, and other powerful features of their ancestor, but differ greatly in syntax.
OCaml does have better support for what we today call object orientation, and has turned
out to be better known than its “standard” sibling. It has notably become a convenient
language for authoring compilers—both Facebook’s Hack and early versions of Rust were
written in OCaml. Modern languages such as Elm, F#, Scala, Rust, and Grain have listed
OCaml as one of their primary influences. The language is also known for high performance
and safety, and has found uses in financial trading domains.

16.4.3

F#

Microsoft’s F# first appeared in 2005 and was developed as an open-source, cross-platform
CLI language, intended as a modern ML for the .NET world. Microsoft bills it as a
“functional-first” language. While the similarities to ML languages such as OCaml and
Haskell are unmistakable, F# contains an impressive list of features and support for imperative programming, asynchronous workflows, agents, object orientation, and parallelism,
and is used in machine learning, business intelligence, analytics, and gaming.

Additional Languages ■ 345

16.4.4

PureScript

Phil Freeman began work on PureScript in 2013 when “I found myself using functional
programming techniques that I had picked up in languages like Haskell, but wanted a more
principled environment in which to apply them. Solutions at the time included various
attempts to compile Haskell to JavaScript...but I was interested to see how successful I could
be by approaching the problem from the other side—attempting to keep the semantics of
JavaScript, while enjoying the syntax and type system of a language like Haskell.”
PureScript is syntactically similar to Haskell, and shares several advanced features with its
influencer, namely Hindley-Milner typing, algebraic datatypes, pattern matching, higherkinded types, and typeclasses, while adding row polymorphism and extensible records. It
differs form Haskell primarily in its emphasis on iteroperation with JavaScript (and other
transpilation targets, including Go), its preference for strict (not lazy) evaluation, and its
use of an Effect monad, rather than Haskell’s IO.

16.4.5

Idris

Idris debuted in 2009 as a pure functional language featuring dependent types. Dependent
types allow many aspects of a program’s behavior to be specified within the type system
itself. Common examples of dependent types include “the type of all vectors of length n,”
“the type of all pairs of integers in which the second element is greater than the first,” and
“the type of all functions returning the value seven.” With computations performed within
the type checker, at compile-time, the programmer can set guarantees that a program, when
executed, will meet certain specifications. In fact, Idris borrows a number of features from
interactive proof assistants such as Coq and Agda. While Idris can be used for theorem
proving, its development has focused on general-purpose computation.
Syntactically, Idris looks a great deal like Haskell, with some superficial differences beyond
those forms required to express dependent typing. Semantically, Idris is a pure functional
language employing algebraic effects (in addition to monads) for managing side effects
(networking, file I/O, exceptions, user interaction). Idris’s creator, Edwin Brady, describes
the theory and implementation of algebraic effects, and the means by which they solve
various shortcomings of Haskell’s monadic approach, in [12].

16.5

SYSTEMS LANGUAGES

It’s often helpful to distinguish application software and system software. The former
solves problems of interest to humans and is device and platform-independent; the latter
is concerned with managing the resources of a computer system (memory, files, processes)
and may be device-dependent. An application programming language provides rich
data modeling constructs and assumes the existence of a large runtime system to perform
memory management without programmer intervention. A systems language compiles
“to the metal” with little or no runtime. C (Chapter 13) and Rust (Chapter 15), along with
C++ and Zig, do without garbage collectors, while Go (Chapter 12), D, and Nim allow for
garbage collectors while still providing low-level features suitable for systems programming.
We’ve seen how the quintessential systems language C is unsafe-by-design systems language;
each of the alternatives add, in varying degrees, features to make system programming safer.
Let’s look at four of these.

346 ■ Programming Language Explorations

16.5.1

C++

In the 1970s, Bjarne Stroustrup implemented a simulator to study communication between
programs in a distributed environment. The first version, written in Simula, was a “pleasure to write” but performance problems (80% of the run time was in the garbage collector)
nearly doomed the project, so Stroustrup rewrote the simulator in BCPL (a predecessor of
C). However, “the experience of coding and debugging the simulator in BCPL was horrible.” [106] This experience led him to design a new language, a superset of C with Simula’s
classes, but without garbage collection, run-time type checking, and the impossibly low-level
near-typelessness of BCPL. The language went by the name C with Classes and evolved,
by 1983, into C++. With this new language, object orientation went mainstream.
As a superset of C, C++ necessarily permits manual memory management, but encourages
memory-safe programming patterns such as smart pointers and the use of class constructors to allocate memory and destructors to deallocate, hiding management details from users
of the class. C++ encourages all user-defined data types to act as if they were copied, but
provides mechanisms to avoid copies via moves. Pointers and aliases (confusingly termed
references in C++) are available when explicit sharing is desired.
C++ is a venerable, long-lived, and massively popular language used in enterprise applications, games, and performance-critical environments. It has evolved well: the so-called Classic C++ has grown into Modern C++, a kind of language rebirth that began with C++ 11,
and has continued through C++ 14, C++ 17, and C++ 20. Many of the programming patterns in Modern C++ replace the “old ways” of doing things. C++ programmers now enjoy
extensive type inference, range-based for-loops, atomics, and anonymous functions (even
supporting generics). The combination of expressive constructs and system-level memory
operations has kept the language relevant and widely used.

16.5.2

D

Walter Bright at Digital Mars started the D language in 2001 at Digital Mars, as an improved version of C++. While keeping many of the low-level features of C++ (including
the ability to inline assembly code) and remaining statically typed, D adds many expressive
features common in dynamic languages, including array slices, function literals and closures,
map and reduce. D even has automatic garbage collection, but under programmer control!
The language provides several mechanisms to ensure memory safety around certain object
lifetimes, though as some manual memory management is allowed, programmers have the
ability to decorate areas of safe and unsafe code.

16.5.3

Nim

Like D, Nim is a systems programming language with plenty of modern features and tunable garbage collectors. It avoids many unsafe features of C such as pointer arithmetic.
Created by Andreas Rumpf and first released in 2008, Nim 2.0 arrived in 2023 with reference counting as the default memory management strategy. Nim has a pleasant syntax
(inspired by Python), an extensive macro system, a rich standard library, and mechanisms
to interoperate with C and JavaScript.

Additional Languages ■ 347

16.5.4

Zig

Zig, created by Andrew Kelley and first released in 2016, is a pragmatic alternative to C
that is relatively easy to learn due to its consistent and readable syntax. It has no garbage
collector, but improves upon C’s wild and unconstrained malloc and free calls by allowing
allocators to be passed to functions that require dynamic memory. Error handling and
null safety are notable and rather similar to Rust’s mechanisms.

16.6

CONCURRENCY

Most modern languages feature concurrency support, but experienced developers working
on mission-critical applications require a deep understanding of the fundamental principles
of concurrent and distributed systems. This knowledge comes not only from day-to-day
practice but also from a study of early seminal work such as Hoare’s CSP and Milner’s
CCS, and the early experimental and research languages occam (featuring named channels,
as adopted by Go), SR, and Linda (sporting a unique means of process coordination through
a “tuple space”).
Naturally, research in concurrent and distributed systems remains extremely active. We’ve
spent time in previous chapters on a few principles of concurrency in our tours of Java,
Clojure, Go, and Rust. A few languages have been created with concurrent and distributed
in mind; let’s explore a few.

16.6.1

Erlang

Organizations and personnel responsible for service availability like to talk about nines.
“Five nines” means up and running 99.999% of the time, down only five minutes per year.
Seven nines means down only three seconds per year (or two minutes every forty years).
Can a language help its users (developers) achieve high availability? Erlang can, and does.
It’s almost entirely functional. Processes are lightweight and share nothing, communicating
only via messages. Processes are distributed over machines transparently.
Joe Armstrong and others at Ericsson developed Erlang in the 1980s to implement highly
reliable, long-lived applications in telephony to handle a large number of concurrent activities, meet real-time constraints, scale across large distributed networks, and are able to
be modified while running. It lives on today in modern applications like CouchDB, RabbitMQ, WhatsApp, and Whisper. Like Java, Erlang arrived with its own virtual machine.
And just as hundreds of languages have been targeted to Java’s JVM, a number of up-andcoming languages (including Elixir, Joxa, and LFE) are designed to run on Erlang’s virtual
machine.

16.6.2

Elixir

Elixir was created by José Valim in 2012 as a modern Erlang, running on the Erlang virtual
machine, with all of Erlang’s process capabilities, but with a syntax influenced by Ruby.
It adds to Erlang powerful metaprogramming facilities, with hygienic macros (operating
on abstract syntax as in Julia and Clojure), protocols, reducers, and the convenient |>
operator. Some of Erlang’s more unusual syntactic forms and its representations of strings
as lists of integers have been modernized. The Ruby-style syntax makes the authoring of
domain-specific languages within Elxir quite pleasant, and the language has found use in
voice, messaging, and communications applications.

348 ■ Programming Language Explorations

16.6.3

ParaSail

Designed by Tucker Taft and first released in 2009, ParaSail, or Parallel Specification and
Implementation Language, is a “parallel programming language designed to support the
development of inherently safe and secure, highly parallel applications that can be mapped
to multicore, manycore, heterogeneous, or distributed architectures.” [108] The language
has a familiar syntax and traditional object-oriented features such as classes, interfaces, and
parameterized modules. Safety features are designed into the language itself and enforced
by the compiler; there are no pointers, global variables, parameter aliasing, exceptions, or
explicit locking.

16.6.4

Chapel

Chapel, developed at Cray Inc. and originating as part of DARPA’s High Productivity Computing Systems (HPCS) program, aims to improve the programmability of parallel computers. It is strongly influenced by classic languages such as (High-Performance) Fortran, C,
and C++ and their associated libraries, but has the look and feel of more modern, expressive languages such as Python. An important design goal has been to make the language
expressive enough to formulate all parallel tasks with out the need to drop into a library.
Architecturally, writes Brad Chamberlain, Chapel’s principal engineer, “Chapel supports
distinct concepts for describing parallelism (‘These things should run concurrently’) from
locality (‘This should be placed here; that should be placed over there’). This is in sharp
contrast to conventional approaches that either conflate the two concepts or ignore locality
altogether.” [15]

16.7

MULTIPARADIGM LANGUAGES

An important domain targeted by some languages is the so-called enterprise, a term
roughly meaning “a large collection of applications, custom and third-party libraries, and
scripts for managing the mission-critical operations of a business or organization.” In the
business world, enterprise software manages data stores, processes bulk data from partner
companies (often called feeds), services customers’ browsers and mobile applications, and
generates massive amounts of usage data, or analytics. In other domains, enterprise software
may refer to the millions of lines of code running sophisticated aircraft or spaceships. Regardless of the domain, building systems with hundreds of thousands, or millions of lines of
code requires language support for writing correct, reliable, robust, scalable, eﬀicient code
that is easy to maintain and extend.

16.7.1

CLU

As the first language to combine abstract data types and encapsulation, CLU was authored
by a group led by Barbara Liskov beginning in 1973. Although not widely implemented,
CLU was an enormously influential language. Its pioneering approach to information hiding
has been adapted by hundreds of subsequent languages. It provided “linguistic support
for data abstraction [and] was the first implemented language to do so. In addition, it
contain[ed] a number of other interesting and influential features, including an exception
handling mechanism, iterators, and parameterized types.” [65] Its construct for modularity,
the cluster (which gives the language its name), extended Simula’s class by encapsulating,
or hiding, all of the objects and functions not explicitly exported. The object and type
systems were essentially copied to a tee by Python: values of all variables are references to

Additional Languages ■ 349
entities, and assignment and parameter passing therefore copies references. Liskov herself
coined the term pass-by-sharing for this scenario, a term popularized by Python. In
addition, Python’s universal supertype, object, is exactly CLU’s Any.
CLU’s design and implementation came from years of research on programming abstractions and structuring mechanisms, and was guided by a number of principles including
“minimality, simplicity, expressive power, uniformity, safety, and performance [65, p. 8],
goals adopted by subsequent languages. Liskov writes:
CLU was geared toward developing production code. It was intended to be a tool
for “programming in the large,” for building big systems (e.g., several hundred
thousand lines) that require many programmers to work on them. As the work on
CLU went on, I developed a programming methodology for such systems. CLU
favors program readability and understandability over ease of writing, since we
believed that these were more important for our intended users [65, p. 6].

16.7.2 Ada
Ada, named after Ada Lovelace, often credited as the first computer programmer, [94] was
Jean Ichbiah’s and CII Honeywell Bull’s winning entry in a late 1970s competition sponsored
by the United States Department of Defense to create a standard language to supersede the
then hundreds of programming languages being used in the Department. It is still used to
day in applications including air-traﬀic control and satellites, and defense systems
First appearing in 1983, Ada became the first internationally standardized object-oriented
language [55] [10] Ada is a large language with several design goals, including:
• Support for megaprogramming. Ada was designed to support systems with millions of
lines of code through various modular constructs split into interfaces and implementations, and can perform type-checking across separately compiled modules.
• Support for real-time, concurrent, embedded systems. Ada’s representation clauses,
tasks, and protected objects allow for hardware support with high-level constructs.
• A laser focus on safety. Ada prohibits virtually all type coercions, provides many
constructs that hide all nonexported entities, and requires that generic types be explicitly declared. Ada has a reputation as a verbose language due to its favoring of
explicitness over implicitness nearly everywhere.
Two of Ada’s best-known features are (1) the rendezvous, a synchronization mechanism
on which running tasks exchange information in a controlled fashion where the sender and
receiver wait for each other to be ready to communicate, and data is transferred safely
without race conditions; and (2) the protected object, a data-oriented mechanism for
thread safety. Examples of each are included in the companion website.
Though not widely used outside its niche in safety-critical environments, Ada continues to
evolve today. It has both influenced and has been influenced by a number of languages for
whom safety is a critical motivating design factor.

16.7.3

C#

C# is the flagship enterprise language for the .NET framework [72], a popular software
platform created by Microsoft, and the most widely used language on the platform. Although
early versions of C# were notably similar to Java, C# has, through its evolution, kept

350 ■ Programming Language Explorations
ahead of Java by supporting features that Java only later adopted, has yet to adopt, or
may never adopt. These include nullable types, reified generic types,3 partial methods,
properties, functional programming support (e.g., lambda expressions), extension methods,
anonymous types, and string interpolation.
C# sports many features useful in an enterprise language, including safety features such
as static type checking, bounds checking, isolation of unsafe memory operations, and a
prohibition against certain forms of error-prone shadowing. It prohibits compilation of code
in which local variables are not assigned, but guarantees initialization of certain other
variables, including instance variables. In addition, programmers find many conveniences
such as type inference, properties (sugaring getters and setters, as in Swift), namespaces,
list comprehensions, delegates, operator overloading, and metaprogramming capabilities.
Fluent styles of programming are quite common. The following snippet of code features
methods from the System.Linq (“(l)anguage-(in)tegrated (q)uery.”) namespace:
lines
.SelectMany(line => wordRegex.Matches(line).Cast<Match>())
.Select(x => x.Value.ToLower())
.GroupBy(x => x)
.OrderBy(x => x.Key)
.ToList()
.ForEach(p => Console.WriteLine("{0} {1}", p.Key, p.Count()));

16.7.4

Scala

Scala (the Scalable language) was created by Martin Odersky at École Polytechnique
Fédérale de Lausanne in the early 2000s as a functional, object-oriented language for the
Java platform, and as such can be used anywhere Java is popular—infrastructure, APIs,
etc. It added functional programming features to the JVM world years before lambdas
were introduced to Java. It also was well ahead of Java in implementing type inference,
string interpolation, class declarations with simple fields in a class header, and the ability
to write scripts without an enclosing class. Though Scala is interoperable with Java and its
threaded concurrency model, its preference for immutability and safety drove it to adopt the
actor model as its main concurrency feature, though like Clojure, it also features software
transactional memory.

16.7.5

Mojo

Mojo was created by Chris Lattner (of Swift and LLVM fame) and his team at Modular,
Inc., primarily for systems programming and the creation of AI applications. It was made
available to the world via browser-hosted notebooks in May of 2023 and to Linux-based
machines in September of 2023. Designed as a superset of Python (to lessen the leaning
curve), benchmarks exist that show certain Mojo applications running 35,000 times faster
than their Python counterparts [18]. The speedup is due to the use of the LLVM compiler
toolchain, the MILR (Multi-level Intermediate Representation) compiler infrastructure, and
the compiler’s ability to take direct advantage of parallel hardware. The complier is even
able to autotune applications for even better performance.
3 A reified generic maintains type information about the instantiated type at run time. Contrast this
approach with Java’s type erasure, which removes this knowledge at run time.

Additional Languages ■ 351

16.8

THE WEB

Given the ubiquity of the World Wide Web, it’s not at all surprising that programming
languages specifically targeted to the web as a platform would arise. The web is a clientserver system, where multiple clients interact asynchronously with a stateless server using
a protocol with a small, limited number of verbs, namely GET, POST, PUT, PATCH,
DELETE, OPTIONS, HEAD, and TRACE. On the client side, web browsers have engines
for directly running JavaScript or WebAssembly (WASM)—a binary format that just about
any language can be compiled to for browser execution.
While just about any language can be—and has been—used on the server side of the web
(e.g., Python, Ruby, Java, and many others come to mind), one, PHP, owes its very existence
to its creator’s desire for a server-side web application scripting program. On the client-side,
JavaScript and TypeScript still live at the top of the most-popular lists, there’s competition:
hundreds of languages can be translated directly into JavaScript—the big list is at [9]. And
with WASM gaining popularity, a fair number of browser-based games have been written in
Rust and C++ without any JavaScript at all. We’ll restrict our attention to those client-side
languages that were designed specifically for web applications.

16.8.1

PHP

PHP is a server-side scripting language developed by Rasmus Lerdorf, who originally built it
to maintain his personal home page. PHP stands for PHP Hypertext Processor, and is today an expressive, general-purpose programming language with plenty of features, including
closures, null-coalescing, exception handling, generators, and variadic functions.
PHP does, however, remain dogged by some early design choices, including weak
typing, inconsistent spelling (e.g., gettype vs. get_class); the left-associative conditional (TRUE ? 1 : FALSE ? 2 : 3 is 2), and strangely named methods (e.g.,
DateTimeImmutable::modify().) in its somewhat organic evolution. Ample good-natured
criticisms of these design choices are documented in [25] and [114], among others. The language has, over the years, shed some of the early problematic features, but even with its
remaining issues is able to stay high on the list of the world’s most popular languages,
powering a large number of the world’s web servers.

16.8.2

Dart

Dart was created by Google to create scalable web applications, and is used today in products such as AdSense and GoogleFiber. Its flavor of object orientation, with interfaces, abstract classes, and generics are familiar to fans of Java and C#. While supporting client-side
web applications and interoperability with JavaScript, Dart can manipulate files, sockets,
and other I/O channels not available to typical browser-based runtimes.
Dart enables concurrency through isolates, a multithreaded message-passing system that
compiles to the HTML5 Web Worker API [115] (or to the browser’s event loop if web workers
are unavailable). The language also defines hardware-friendly datatypes that pack multiple numeric values into a single unit for parallel execution, such as Float32x4, Int32x4,
Float32x4List and Int32x4List, making Dart suitable for use with high-performance
graphics and other computationally intensive tasks.

352 ■ Programming Language Explorations

16.8.3

Elm

Evan Czaplicki created Elm in 2011 as a language for building browser-based applications in
a functional programming style. Like TypeScript, Elm applications transpile to JavaScript.
But while TypeScript is a superset of JavaScript, Elm is syntactically similar to Haskell.
While it has the expected ML features of Hindley-Milner type inference, pattern matching,
persistent data structures, and more, it replaces JavaScript’s promise-based style with a
subscription-based approach to interactivity.

16.8.4

Grain

Grain was created by Philip Blair and Oscar Spencer in 2017 as a statically-typed, highlevel language targeted directly to the Web Assembly platform. Grain’s built-in libraries
provide direct access to the WebAssembly System Interface (WASI) for ease of building
WebAssembly applications with multiple languages. Grain borrows several syntactic features
from Rust (among them enums, the mut keyword), and borrows the type checker from
OCaml, allowing programs be both short and quite readable.

16.9

QUANTUM COMPUTING LANGUAGES

When writing code for a quantum computer, one can either use a general-purpose programming language with libraries, frameworks, and software development kits to compose
quantum algorithms, or use a language specifically designed for making quantum algorithms
easier to express. Popular Python libraries include Google’s Cirq or Xanadu’s Strawberry
Fields and Pennylane. Quantum languages include Q#, from Microsoft Research, and Quipper, a quantum circuit description language with powerful operators for assembling and
manipulating circuits. Quipper features quantum data types, circuit transformers, and a
number of quantum functions prepackaged into libraries. It runs as a domain-specific language embedded within Haskell. An extensive list of quantum libraries and languages can
be found at [123]

16.10

ESOTERIC LANGUAGES

No tour of programming languages should omit the ever-growing family of esoteric languages, with members such as Ook!, Malbolge, Befunge, LOLCODE, Shakespeare, Chef,
Chicken, Whitespace, and GOTO++. You won’t see any of these powering any real-world
applications; instead, their designers work outside the box to produce artistic, crazy, funny,
themed, weird, or insightful creations that may push the boundaries of minimalistic computing models, look at computing in a whole new way, or simply amuse.

16.10.1

Brainf**k

Brainf**k is one of the better-known of the esoteric languages and one of the earliest. Urban
Müller’s 1993 creation grew out of an effort to design a Turing-complete4 programming
language implementable with the smallest possible complier (296 bytes at the time, with
smaller compilers created since).
4 Formally, as powerful as a Turing Machine; informally, capable of doing anything a Python program
can do assuming infinite memory.

Additional Languages ■ 353
A Brainf**k program is a sequence of one-character commands operating on a single infinite
array of bytes (indexed from zero) and two streams of bytes—standard input and standard
output. The current memory cell is initially the leftmost. The eight commands are:
,
.
<
>
+
[
]

Read the byte from stdin into the current cell
Write the byte in the current cell to stdout
Move the current cell pointer left
Move the current cell pointer right
Increment the byte in the current cell
Decrement the byte in the current cell
If the byte in the current cell is zero, jump ahead to just past the matching ] (Think
of this as the beginning of a while loop: if the condition is false, you will skip the
entire loop)
If the byte in the current cell is nonzero, jump backwards to just beyond the matching [ (Think of this as the end of a while loop: if the condition is true, repeat the
loop body again)

The following program:
+++++++[>+++++[>++>+++<<-]<-]>>++.>.
prints the string Hi to standard output. As all cells have an initial value of zero, the program
first increases the first cell to 7, then the second to 5, then the next two cells to 2 and 3,
with the fourth cell as our current cell:
7

5

2

3
^

We’re inside a doubly-nested “loop” here. Our next instruction says to move backward two
cells and decrement the value. If not yet zero, we’ll continue our inner loop of incrementing
the third and fourth cells by 2 and 3 respectively. After this is done five times, our memory
becomes:
7

0
^

10

15

We’re back in an “outer loop” now, and we’re supposed to go back one cell to the left
and decrement...we’re being told to do what we just did seven times. This takes us to the
following state:
0
^

0

70

105

We now need to move two cells to the right, bump the value by 2 to 72, the code point for
Latin Capital Letter H and print it with the . command. Moving one cell to the right
we find, happily, 105, the code point of Latin Small Letter I, which we print. And we’re
done.
You might want to now work through the classic Hello world program, as found
on [118]:
++++++++[>++++[>++>+++>+++>+<<<<-]>+>+>->>+[<]<-]>>.>
---.+++++++..+++.>>.<-.<.+++.------.--------.>>+.>++.

354 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Finally, do consider writing your own Brainf**k compiler. If you get stuck, consult the direct
mappings from Brainf**k commands to fragments of C code online at [118].

16.10.2

Befunge

While Brainf**k was designed to make it easy to write compilers, and in fact small compilers, Chris Pressey designed Befunge as a challenge to would-be compiler writers. Befunge
programs are written in a two-dimensional grid of instructions. Some instructions manipulate the data stack in the usual way, some do input and output (to/from the stack), and
some instructions—<, ^, >, and v change the direction in which the program is read. Even
better, or worse, is the p instruction: it pops from the stack three values y, x, and v, then
replaces the character at position (x, y) in the code grid with v. So the grid contains both
instructions and data, allowing you to change code on the fly.
Here’s a factorial function in Befunge (can you see the loop?):
&>:1-:v v *_$.@
^
_$>\:^

16.10.3

LOLCODE

A year after the massive popularization of the internet meme LOLcats, Adam Lindsay
developed LOLCODE, a procedural language that adapted LOLcats’ “kitty pidgin” speech
to its own programming vocabulary. LOLCODE features all uppercase keywords such as
HAI, KTHXBYE, and O RLY?. Underneath its amusing syntax, LOLCODE is actually a regular,
imperative (though small) programming language, with loops, conditionals, and functions.
Here’s our recurring example script for computing Pythagorean triples:
HAI 1.2
IM IN YR FIRST UPPIN YR Z TIL BOTH SAEM Z AN 41
IM IN YR SECOND UPPIN YR Y TIL BOTH SAEM Y AN Z
IM IN YR THIRD UPPIN YR X TIL BOTH SAEM X AN Y
I HAS A W ITZ SUM OF PRODUKT OF X AN X AN PRODUKT OF Y AN Y
BOTH SAEM W AN PRODUKT OF Z AN Z, O RLY?
YA RLY, VISIBLE X ", " Y ", " Z
OIC
IM OUTTA YR THIRD
IM OUTTA YR SECOND
IM OUTTA YR FIRST
KTHXBYE
Cats don’t write very well, so you won’t see much punctuation in LOLCODE; we’ve only
used the comma, which does nothing more than abbreviate a newline. And fixed-arity
operators don’t need parentheses (try proving this!). A few symbols do appear within YARN
literals (YARNs are strings, get it?): :) represents the newline, :> is the tab character, and
so on. The theme continues with well-chosen token names for breaks (GTFO), switches (WTF?),
cases within switches (OMG), returns (FOUND YR), and the else branch of an O RLY? statement
(NO WAI).

Additional Languages ■ 355

16.10.4

CJam

Code Golfing is a recreational pastime where participants attempt to solve programming
challenges with the shortest possible source code. While one can golf in any language, dozens
of languages [26] have been created expressly for golfing. Such languages tend to feature
powerful one-character functions. CJam, created by aditsu in 2014, is a stack-based golfing
language. The stack can contain numbers, arrays, strings, and even blocks of code. The
stack is implicitly printed at the end of the program, so:
13 8 3 - + 2 3 * 2 / #
prints the evaluation of (13 + (8 − 3))(2∗3)÷2 , namely 5832 (note that # is the exponentiation
operator).
The language boasts nearly 100 predefined operators, many of which have multiple meanings
depending on the type of the operands, and 26 variables (A-Z), all with initial values. Let’s
explore with a small script that prints the first twelve Fibonacci numbers to standard
output, one per line:
0X{_2$+}A*]N*
The script begins by pushing 0 then 1 to the stack (X is pre-initialized to 1). Then a block of
code is executed 10 times (A*). The block pushes (duplicates) the top of the stack (with _),
then pushes the value two slots below the top (2$) and adds them. After 10 iterations, the
entire stack is turned into an array with ]. Then the array elements are joined (via the
overloaded *) with a newline, the initial value of the variable N .

16.10.5

Pyth

Pyth, created by Izzy Grosof in 2014, is another golfing language, but rather than employing an implicit stack, Pyth programs translate rather directly into Python programs. All
operators are prefix and have a fixed arity, rendering parentheses unnecessary. This means
that since the subtraction operator is the hyphen character -, unary negation has to be
something else; in fact, it’s the underscore (_).
Like CJam, Pyth operators are overloaded; for example + will add numbers but concatenate
strings and lists. The underscore _ not only negates numbers but reverses sequences and
inverts (swaps keys and values) of dictionaries. Control flow constructs use capital letters:
F begins a for-loop, V is a for-loop over a range, and W a while loop. Many of Python’s
operators appear as single-character Pyth functions, e.g., m for map, f for filter, and r for
reduce. Programs can be written as a single line with statements separated with semicolons.
Opening quotes, parentheses, and brackets are automatically closed at the end of statement,
since in code golfing, every character counts.
Here’s the first-twelve-Fibonacci script from our section on CJam above, written in
Pyth:
A(Z1;VhhTGA(H+GH
We begin with the tuple assignment operator A assigning to the implicit target (G, H),
their initial values (0, 1). We then iterate (with V ), 12 times (hhT , since T is 10 and
h is the successor function) the following: evaluate G (for implicit printing), then assign
(G, H) ← (H, G + H).

356 ■ Programming Language Explorations
Do visit the Pyth tutorial and documentation from [38], as well as the book’s companion
website for a collection of additional examples.

16.11

ASSEMBLY LANGUAGES

An assembly language describes a machine’s execution, often with commands that map
one-to-one to machine instructions. Even if you use only high-level languages, something
about machines—machines of the past, present, and future—may be relevant to you. Uncle
Bob tells us why: [69]
Did you know, for example, that the ++ operator in C, C++, Java, and C#
derived from the fact that there were special registers in early computers that
automatically incremented their contents every time you accessed them? The
compiler tried to make use of these auto-increment registers whenever you used
a ++ operator.
...our languages are projections of the hardware. Some languages are more abstract projections than others; but the most popular languages, C, Pascal, C++,
Delphi, Java, and C# are very closely related to the hardware. Even languages
like Smalltalk, Python, and Ruby, while more abstract than the others, still
show their hardware roots.
Machines can be quite interesting. While the average programmer with little exposure to
assembly language may dread the number of instructions required to implement a for-loop,
the fact is machines often contain relatively powerful instructions, combining comparisons
and jumps, locking, exchanging regions of memory, and operating on dozens of data items
in a single clock pulse.
We’ll cover two short examples in the NASM assembly language, which targets the x86-64
architecture [54]. The first is a function computing the maximum of three integers. We have
here only a function, not a complete program. After assembling and linking the code, the
function can be called from any language that can be compiled to the same executable file
format that the assembler produces.
; A 64-bit function that returns the maximum value of its
; three 64-bit integer arguments. The function has signature:
;
;
int64_t maxofthree(int64_t x, int64_t y, int64_t z)
;
; Note that the parameters have already been passed in rdi,
; rsi, and rdx. We just have to return the value in rax.
global _maxofthree
section .text
_maxofthree:
mov
rax, rdi
cmp
rax, rsi
cmovl
rax, rsi
cmp
rax, rdx
cmovl
rax, rdx
ret

; result (rax) initially holds x
; is x less than y?
; if so, set result to y
; is max(x,y) less than z?
; if so, set result to z
; the max will be in rax

Additional Languages ■ 357
Here we see that functions accepting integers accept arguments in various registers, the
first three of which are rdi, rsi, and rdx. The instructions for performing comparisons and
conditional moves are explained in the comments.
And now let’s look at one last example. The x86 architecture features hardware registers
that can store, and operate on, multiple units of data at once, making SIMD (singleinstruction, multiple-data) processing possible. The so-called XMM registers are 128-bits
wide; the YMM registers are 256-bits wide; ZMM registers are 512-bits wide. Here’s a very
brief illustration: we’ll add two 4-element arrays of floating-point numbers using the XMM
registers:
; void add_four_floats(float x[4], float y[4])
; x[i] += y[i] for i in range(0..4)
global
section

_add_four_floats
.text

_add_four_floats:
movdqa
xmm0, [rdi]
movdqa
xmm1, [rsi]
addps
xmm0, xmm1
movdqa
[rdi], xmm0
ret

; all four values of x
; all four values of y
; do all four sums in one shot

A good compiler for a high-level language should be able to recognize when SIMD instructions can be used. And you may often see software libraries with functions that map directly
to these instructions. But sometimes, you may wish to break out an assembler and experience the feeling of writing assembly language directly, feeling at one with the machine.
Fortunately, several high-level languages do give explicit support for SIMD operations, including Dart and Mojo.

LANGUAGE TOUR WRAPUP
The question that opened the preface of this book, “Why would you want more than machine
language?” was asked by none other than John von Neumann. History has shown that highlevel languages matter. But somehow, it does seem rather fitting to finish our tour looking
at...of all things, an assembly language. As long as our programs run on hardware, we’ll
need to be aware of the connections between our high-level languages and the machine and
assembly languages that make the hardware run.
Von Neumann’s name now labels one of the most prolific electronic computer architectures
of the last century—one in which programs and data share memory, a program counter
ticks away running sequential processes on each core, an arithmetic-logic unit (ALU) processes instructions, and a bus shuttles data to and from memory. But hardware engineers
have been building alternative architectures for quite some time! Dataflow and reduction
machines have been around for a while, and to some extent, functional and array programming languages really do show very little connection to the von Neumann architecture at
all. Today we see research in a number of brain-inspired computer architectures, such as
IBM’s TrueNorth [71], and other neuromorphic [77] systems. Will we see inklings of these
architectures showing up in future programming languages? Or will new architectures, no

358 ■ Programming Language Explorations
matter how novel, be programmed in the languages of today, employing little more than
new programming paradigms?
Speaking of hardware, it has been noted that since the 1950s, computer hardware has
improved several orders of magnitude in speed, storage capacity, size, and weight [69].
Where are the orders of magnitude improvements in software? How much better are today’s
languages from the early versions of Fortran and Lisp? Are they anywhere near orders
of magnitude better? What kind of revolutionary thinking would it take to design and
implement a language that is?
We hope you’ve enjoyed the tour.

CHAPTER

17

Concepts

Hello again! If you’ve made it this far, you’ve been introduced to the study of programming
languages through chapter-length overviews of fourteen languages and brief glances at a
few dozen more. The intent was not to provide a comprehensive tutorial for any of the
languages, but rather to use the overviews as a vehicle to introduce important concepts of
programming language theory, design, and pragmatics.
Now it’s time to revisit all that we’ve learned from another angle, this time with a focus
on the concepts themselves. We will be bringing together and organizing the various linguistic elements around three major themes: (1) the classification of human-friendly units
of information by structure and behavior into types; (2) the various linguistic structures
that build a program up from entities (literals, variables, expressions, statements, functions, modules, and more), and (3) execution, the ways in which computations themselves
progress over time and how the various computational units communicate and coordinate
with each other.
While learning individual languages is certainly important, a study of language-independent
concepts is a necessary step in advancing in the field. We need theory! One dictionary defines
a theory as “the branch of a science or art consisting of its explanatory statements, accepted
principles, and methods of analysis.” [3] That is, a theory is an organized body of knowledge
with explanatory and predictive powers. With a good theory, we have the vocabulary to
communicate with others, reason about a discipline, make predictions, and generate new
knowledge.
Armed with programming experience and proficiency in over a dozen programming languages, we can now organize our knowledge of language principles into a framework that
will guide further study. Although this final chapter serves as a summary of concepts introduced during our language tour, we will slip in a few concepts not covered earlier, but that
fit into the overall organization in a nice way. Take the opportunity to explore these popup
concepts on your own.
Finally, because a Socratic approach can be fun at times, we will present the concepts in
this chapter not with an outline, but with questions. The goal is to not only summarize, but
to bring ideas close together to motivate ways of thinking about programming languages.
We encourage the reader to not only look for answers to the questions posed here, but to
also generate illustrative examples in their favorite languages.

359

360 ■ Programming Language Explorations

17.1

CLASSIFICATION

Grady Booch has written:
Scientists who work in material domains—physics, chemistry, astronomy, biology, geology, meteorology—study the cosmos and all that is in it, reflect on the
patterns they observe, then try to reduce their understandings to the simplest
principles.
I am a computer scientist; I approach the cosmos though a very different path:
I take the simplest of principles—information theory, logic, design—and from
them create new worlds that are bound only by my imagination.
We can build new worlds, or descriptions of them (languages), from the building blocks of
information: bits. We can use specific arrangements of bits to describe anything: numbers
(bounded integers, unbounded integers, floating point numbers of different sizes, rationals,
arbitrary-precision reals, complex numbers and more), booleans, and text. We call these
human-friendly chunks of information values and classify them into types. Different languages have different type systems; we’ve seen many in previous chapters! To further our
study and unify some of these concepts, we may ask, for a given language:
1. Is there exactly one numeric type or multiple numeric types? Are integer types divided
across signed and unsigned types? Fixed size and unbounded? Modular and saturated?
Are there separate types for 8, 16, 32, 64, and 128-bit fixed-size integers? Are floatingpoint types so divided? Is there an unbounded floating-point type?
2. Are rational, decimal, or complex types defined within the language, in a library, or
completely unsupported? How about quaternions and octonions?
3. Are characters differentiated from strings of length one? Are strings just lists of characters or is there a distinct string type?
4. How do we access a string’s (encoded) bytes, Unicode scalars (characters), custom
codes, or graphemes?
5. Is the length of a string, or of any kind of collection, part of its type?
6. Is there a boolean type or must we use 0 and 1?
7. Are the basic types like numbers, booleans, and strings, wired so strongly into the
language that reserved words define them? Or do they simply live in a standard library
like all other user-defined types?
8. Does the language have a different set of static types from its dynamic types?
9. Can the programmer obtain the type of an expression via a function call, method call,
or typeof operator? If so, is the response just a string naming the type, or a first-class
type object?
10. Are types distinguished from classes?
11. Can types have supertypes? If so, more than one? If so, how is the inherited behavior
determined in the presence of name conflicts?
12. Can we add new types?
13. Does the type system have top and bottom types?
14. Is there a language-intrinsic structure for a completely immutable “record” type?

Concepts ■ 361
15. Can we conveniently make both sum and product types? Are there tagged or labeled
versions of each (i.e., regular vs. tagged unions, tuples vs. records)?
16. Are there explicit pointer types? If so, can we do arithmetic on instances?
17. Do function values have types? Is the return type part of the function type? What
about the types of the parameters? Is the order of parameters significant in determining the function type?
18. Can we define parameterized types? If so, are they parameterized only by other types,
or by arbitrary values?
19. If the language has a static type system, with type constraints on variables, can a
variable’s type be narrowed according to certain operators? According to a program’s
control flow?
20. What are the covariance, contravariance, and invariance rules for parameterized types?
21. What kind of type expressions are allowed in parameterization? Can we go beyond
Java’s <? super T> and <? extends T> mechanisms? Are we only allowed simple
generic constraints? Are the in and out variance markers of TypeScript and Kotlin
supported?
22. Are types first-class? Are type variables allowed? If so, can they be...assigned?
23. If types are objects, are all types instances of a single type (perhaps called Type)?
Do type classes exist in the language? If so, can a type belong to more than one type
class? Can a programmer define new type classes?
24. Do types have types? Do typeclasses have types? Does the language support kinds,
or higher-kinded-types?
25. Can we create types that depend on the values of certain expressions (dependent
types)? If so, what kind of expressions are allowed in dependent type specifications?

17.2

STRUCTURE

A language’s type system allows us to chunk primitive bits into values, which can be numbers, text, data structures (made from sums and products), or even functions. But these
values have to be manipulated in some way. A language has to give us a way to build, structurally, a program out of components. These components, or entities, vary from language
to language; some of the more common and well-known ones are literals, variables, blocks,
functions, statements, events, exceptions, types, interfaces, classes, fields, methods, modules, and threads. In this section, we’ll consider questions that can help us build an ontology
for concepts of program construction. Let’s look first at the smaller parts of expressions—
literals and variables:
1. For which types are literals allowed to be written? We expect literals for numbers,
booleans, and strings, but are arrays, sets, dictionaries, optionals, and general objects
also representable in literal form?
2. Do variable declarations have to be annotated with a type, or can they be inferred?
What about function parameters?
3. Can the type constraint of a variable change?
4. Can a variable be marked as being mutable or immutable? If it can be marked immutable, does this mean that the variable may not be rebound, or that the object
bound to the variable may not be mutated?

362 ■ Programming Language Explorations
5. Are there restrictions on which values can be stored directly in a variable? That is,
can only variables of certain types be stored directly while for others only references
may be stored?
6. Can variables be bound only once or multiple times? Can they be bound via destructuring or pattern matching? Are they bound in parallel or sequentially?
7. How do we know, when looking at an expression, to which entities do each of the
variables refer? In other words, how do we determine the scope of our bindings?
8. Can the same name refer to multiple entities within the same scope? Can an entity
be referred to with multiple names in the same scope?
9. Are there lexical mechanisms for distinguishing local variables from global (or other
nonlocal) variables? Or is this resolved by scope computation within a compiler?
10. Are variables allowed to shadow variables of the same name in outer scopes? If not,
are name collisions prohibited or do they simply create new locals or refer to globals
depending on context?
11. Which constructs define scope? Function bodies only? Certain compound statements
but not others? Is there a block construct? let-expressions?
12. When a variable is updated, what happens to the “old” value?
Now for some questions regarding more complex expressions:
1. Are expressions evaluated when they are first encountered, or only when needed?
2. Is the type of every expression known at compile time (static typing) or not known
until run time (dynamic)?
3. If the language detects that an operator is given operands of a type that it does not
expect, does an error occur (strong typing) or is there an attempt made to convert
the operands to new operands of an acceptable type (weak)?
4. Can we overload operators? Can we define new operators? If so, how do we specify
precedence and associativity? Are all new operators given the same precedence, or
can we choose our own?
5. Can we change the precedence of “built-in” operators?
6. Are operators built-in, or are they just functions from a standard library?
7. Are loops expressions (as opposed to statements)?
8. Can operators be extended to accept multiple arguments or does each operator have
one and only one arity?
9. Which “magic” expressions does the language provide? Is there anything similar to
JavaScript’s this?
10. Can the programmer specify when dynamically allocated memory is freed? Is all
garbage collection automatic, or does the runtime employ reference counting? Are
there both strong and weak references? Does the language have any notion of ownership to help with memory management?
11. Must the operands within an expression be evaluated in a certain order (left-to-right,
right-to-left) or is this left to an implementation? Is the implementation allowed to
evaluate operands in parallel?
12. Does the language require that expressions be reduced in a particular fashion? For
example, must square(inc(8)) be reduced to square(9) or to inc(8)*inc(8)?

Concepts ■ 363
Moving up from the level of expressions, software systems need to have a structured, physical
organization to be understandable. Often, a programming language may provide direct
support for subsystems, rather than leaving it to “builder scripts” to cobble together the
system. Whether they are called “modules,” “packages,” or “clusters,” language designers
can work with these in many ways. We may ask:
1. What kind of top-level constructs are defined by the language? Scripts and modules?
Scripts only?
2. Can top-level modules be compiled separately? That is, are modules standalone entities that can be used in multiple scripts? Must a module’s specification and implementation be separate?
3. How does one arrange to hide entities within a module from the outside world? Are
they marked private? Or are entities private by default, requiring explicit export?
4. Can access permissions be changed at runtime?
5. Are the types of entities in the module retained when imported? (Don’t be surprised
at this question. In early languages, they were not!)
6. Can modules be nested within functions? Within other modules?
7. How does a module import entities from other modules? Implicitly? Via an explicit
import statement? If a module is nested within an outer scope, are the outer entities
automatically imported?
8. Do entities in a nested module shadow entities of the same name?
9. Are the modules first-class?
10. Is a special name given to higher-level modules (e.g., “crate,” “package”, “library”)?
11. Are modules a logical concept that transcend multiple compilation units (files)? Or
does the language expect one module per file?
12. Can modules be parameterized, so that new modules are created upon instantiation
of a module template?
If you’ve ever found yourself in need of writing multiple constructs that all look about the
same, you’d have certainly appreciated a language with good metaprogramming facilities.
Metaprogramming, the art of writing programs that write other programs, or modify or
extend the program that is currently running, is definitely one of the more fascinating
areas of programming language design and pragmatics. It relies, of course, on reflection and
introspection. What options do language designers have here?
1. Can a program ask which classes have been loaded?
2. Can a program ask which variables are global? Local? Properties of an object?
3. Can a program ask for the currently executing function, and perhaps, its caller?
4. Can we get a handle to the currently running thread? Or process? And its status
(active, waiting, terminated, etc.)?
5. Given a function, can the program ask which parameters, and of which type, the
function would accept as arguments? Can it ask for the return type of the function?

364 ■ Programming Language Explorations
6. Given a class or module, can the program ask for its members, constructors, methods,
initializers, etc? Its supertypes and subtypes?
7. Can a variable be read or set via its name (a string) only?
8. Can a function or method be invoked by its name (a string) only?
9. Can new variables or types or functions be created at run time, given only their name
and some indication of how they should look?
10. Does the language have a macro system? If so, do macros generate strings of code
which must then be scanned, parsed, and compiled? Or do they generate abstract
syntax trees?
11. Is it possible to unquote within a macro?
12. Are macros hygienic? What syntactic devices are provided to explicitly capture inscope variables, if any?

17.3

EXECUTION

In the previous section, we worked explored several “static” portions of a programming
language ontology, focusing on how programs were built from components. Now we turn to
questions of a more dynamic nature, asking what programs do. We’ll start with attempting
to organize knowledge about statements:
1. Are statements, expressions, and declarations distinct syntactic and semantic entities,
or is everything—or perhaps nearly everything—an expression?
2. How do we express the ordering of evaluation or execution? Can we combine statements sequentially (s1 ;s2 ), in parallel (s1 ||s2 ), or even via some kind of nondeterministic choice mechanism (s1 |s2 )? Or are we allowed to express sequencing only via
composition of function calls?
3. In a multiway selection, must guards be evaluated in any order or a specific order?
Do we evaluate only enough guards until we find one that is true, or must we evaluate
them all?
4. In the case of a nondeterministic choice construct, what guarantees does the language
place on its implementors to ensure, or to not ensure, fairness among choices?
5. Are short-circuiting boolean operators (such as && and ||, or their generalizations,
all and any) available?
6. Is there a looping construct or must we rely on tail recursion?
7. Can we conveniently express looping mechanisms for all of the usual patterns: forever,
n times, through a range of numbers (with an optional step), through a collection, or
while a condition is true?
8. Can if-statements and while-statements be controlled by the success or failure of
match, as opposed to just boolean test conditions?
9. Are there iterator objects? Or can iteration be implicit? Can we do both? What
happens when a collection is changed during iteration?
10. Can we break out a loop? Continue to the next iteration? Start the current iteration
over? Start the entire loop over?

Concepts ■ 365
11. Are exceptions relied upon for control flow, or are result objects (tagged unions with
success and failure) or option objects favored?
12. Does the language provide a goto statement? If so, what restrictions, if any, are placed
on its use?
13. Does the programmer have access to a timer, either for sleeping or delaying execution?
A function, or procedure, or subroutine, is named block of statements and expressions,
possibly with parameters. How can we understand functions? After all, what could be
simpler to understand than passing arguments to a function and waiting for the result to
be computed? Quite a lot, it turns out! We can ask:
1. Must a function be called with a specific number of arguments? What if we pass too
few arguments? Too many? Can we roll up many arguments into a single parameter?
2. Can we (or must we, or must we not) specify the names of the parameters in the call?
3. Can we arrange that certain parameters accept only arguments of specific types?
4. Can we change the value of a parameter within the function? If so, does this action
also change the variable holding the argument (if any)? If it does, is the change made
immediately or only at the point the function returns?
5. Is a parameter of the called function a distinct variable from its corresponding argument, or just an alias for the argument?
6. If a complex expression is used as an argument, is it evaluated once before the call?
Or evaluated fresh every time the parameter is used inside the call?
7. If all arguments are evaluated before the call, in what order are they evaluated? Left
to right? Right to left? Arbitrary order? In parallel?
8. How do we arrange for a function to return something? Through a special variable?
A return statement? Implicitly as the last expression? Some other way?
9. Can a function return zero values? Must it return one? Can it return many?
10. Are there any restrictions on the types of things a function is allowed to return?
11. How do we (syntactically) distinguish between the function itself as a value, and the
value resulting from a call of the function?
12. Can the function itself (not just its result) be assigned to a variable? Passed as an
argument? Returned as a value from another function?
13. Can a function call itself? If so, are its variables shared among all invocations or does
each invocation have a distinct set of variables?
14. Can a function access variables defined outside of itself? If so, must they be explicitly
imported or are they available by default?
15. Can “local” variables (defined inside the function) be seen by outside code, or are
local variables completely private to the function?
16. If a function can access nonlocal variables, does it use variable bindings in effect in
its caller, or those in effect in the textually surrounding environment?
17. If a function can access nonlocal variables and is passed somewhere where those (nonlocal) variable names have different bindings, which bindings are used when the function
is eventually called?

366 ■ Programming Language Explorations
18. Is it possible to test two functions for equality? If so, what does it mean for two
functions to be equal? Same function object? Same source code? Same input-output
behavior?1
19. Is a function like a regular object—one that we can attach properties to?
20. Do functions have to have names?
Program execution is much more than running a sequence of operations consisting of basic
statements and function calls. While sequential execution is necessary, software systems are
made of multiple sequential threads of control. Describing a program in terms of independent actors doing their own thing concurrently with each other, and communicating and
coordinating their activities, is an excellent way to model real-world activities. There are
many known approaches to concurrent programming. Perhaps a few questions can help us
with a conceptual ontology:
1. How does one decompose a program for concurrent execution?
2. Are there linguistic constructs for coroutines, threads, and processes, or are these “just
functions”?
3. Can we define concurrent execution at different levels of granularity? Expression-level?
Function-level? Program-level?
4. Does the language execution model consist of a single thread in which blocks of code
are executed in response to events? Or can multiple threads exist?
5. If multiple threads can run concurrently, can they preempt each other? Or do threads
run until a point in which they allow themselves to yield control?
6. Can mutable memory be shared? If so, do there exist constructs other than explicit
locks that help to prevent race conditions? Is there support for transactional memory?
7. Is lock-free synchronization (e.g. atomic integers) available?
8. Are tasks spawned implicitly, at the time their enclosing block begins execution, or
must they be explicitly started?
9. Does the runtime unceremoniously kill tasks when the enclosing block, or the spawning
task, terminates? Or does the enclosing/spawning task wait for all internal tasks to
terminate?
10. Are tasks spawned with or without a link to the task doing the spawning?
11. Are we able to keep track of task information at compile-time, so as to guarantee
protection from race conditions?
12. If an asynchronous task is launched, how is a result obtained? Via a callback, promise,
or some other mechanism?
13. What mechanisms exist to wait for a group of tasks to all finish? For the first of many
to finish?
14. Is message passing between processes done by mentioning processes by name (or process identifier), or through specific channels, or are messages put into a global pool
where they can be processed by any willing process?
15. If channels are used to wire processes for communication, must the wiring be fixed at
compile time or can we rewire during execution?
1 Readers familiar with the theory of computation may find this question of particular interest.

Concepts ■ 367
16. Is message passing only synchronous or only asynchronous? Can the programmer
choose?
17. Can an asynchronous call be canceled if the other party does not connect within a
given duration? Do we have a conditional synchronization mechanism (to connect only
if the other party is ready and waiting)?
18. How, if at all, do we detect that a process has crashed?
19. What happens if a message is sent to a terminated process? Is an error generated or
is the message ignored?
20. Do introspection mechanisms exist to get the state of a process (alive, dead, waiting,
etc.)? If so, can we ensure that the state is still valid by the time we are ready to act
upon it?
Finally, languages may have strong opinions about how programs should look and feel in
terms of execution. Perhaps they are attempting to strictly follow a particular computational
paradigm, or are trying to be accepting of multiple ways of doing things. The former approach tends to favor elegance and simplicity, as the language will have very few, easily recognizable building blocks. The latter favors pragmatism, since not every problem naturally
fits into a particular programming style. Perhaps, too, details of the execution context—the
real or virtual machine on which the program runs—may leak into language designs. Therefore, understanding execution platforms empowers you to write code that is not only more
expressive, but more performant: you will be aware of how to make computations consume
resources wisely. Let’s approach these ideas through a series of questions:
1. Do programs in the language have a lot of mutable variables and statements? Or are
virtually all variable immutable, with “control flow” emergent only by composition of
functions operating on parameters and persistent data structures?
2. Does the language have a declarative feel, in which programmers state what they want
and rely on a behind-the-scenes inference engine to obtain desired results?
3. Do programs in the language look like data processing pipelines?
4. Is “everything an object”?
5. Is “everything a function”?
6. Are there distinct ways of denoting variables stored “with the executable” (static),
within a regular function (stack), or dynamically allocated (heap)?
7. Is there an operating system interface? That is, does the language allow you to create
new processes, or read and write files, or make network requests?
8. Is there a way to embed assembly language or machine language? Is there a library
of intrinsic functions that map directly to machine instructions?
9. Does the language advertise itself as mutiparadigm?
10. Are programs strings of text, or built from graphical blocks?

CHAPTER SUMMARY
In this chapter, we’ve laid out a collection of questions to ask about programming languages
with the goal of framing language concepts into a rough ontology. We’ve grouped our questions into three main areas: (1) information representation and classification, specifically
the classification of values into types; (2) program structure (architecture), considering

368 ■ Programming Language Explorations
building blocks known as entities; and (3) execution, considering statements, functions, and
concurrency mechanisms. We did not present an outline-oriented ontology, but we were able
to show the breadth of the field and provide a very rough road map for organizing one’s
linguistic studies.
The previous chapters may have answers to many of these questions for our featured languages, but certainly not all of the questions have been answered, let alone asked. We
encourage you to not only research answers to some of these questions but to create new
questions of your own. Such exercises can help solidify knowledge gained throughout the
tour, and provide a sense of how modern languages support multiple programming styles.
Many of these concepts should carry over well into the study of new languages.
Do keep in mind that this book aims to expose the reader to linguistic theory and practice
via explorations of various topics. Where this book lacks depth in both programming styles
and theoretical foundations, other works can assist you in those areas. For the former, we
recommend Cristina Lopes’s excellent and comprehensive account of 37 programming styles
and their implementation [66]; and for the latter, the works by Turbak and Gifford [111],
Pierce [89], Harper [42], and Stump [107]. Theoretical studies will provide you with insights
and skills into designing the languages of the future.

EXERCISES
17.1 Answer a number of the questions from this chapter for your favorite language(s), or
for languages of your choice.
17.2 Add a question or two of your own to each section from the chapter.

Glossary
The lexicon of programming languages is quite large, worthy of collecting some of the more
important terms in a single place, such as this very glossary. To keep it brief, you won’t
find any examples here—just bare-bones definitions. Here you’ll find many of the terms
encountered throughout the book, as well as quite a few new ones.
Abstract Class A class that cannot have any instances.
Abstract Method A method of an abstract class that has no implementation.
Activation Record See frame.
Actor A concurrent process that communicates by sending and receiving messages.
Agent An entity that acts on behalf of another entity.
Alias One of the names of a multiply-named entity. An entity to which multiple names are
bound is said to be aliased.
Annotation A form of metadata attached to an entity.
Argument An expression passed to a (parameterized) entity during a call, instantiation,
or other invocation.
Assignable A mutable storage location. Used in place of the term variable when emphasizing mutability.
Assignment The act of associating a value with a (mutable) variable.
Asynchronous A type of invocation in which the caller does not wait for the callee to
complete.
Atom A named primitive entity whose value is, essentially, itself. Also known as a symbol.
Atomic Object An object that can be operated upon safely in a multithreaded environment without locks. Often the object holds a numeric value on which “compound”
operations such as compare-and-set or get-and-add can be done atomically.
Automatic Reference Counting (ARC) A mechanism in which the compiler inserts
code to release and retain blocks of memory so the programmer does not have to.
Binding An association of a name with an entity.
Borrow The use of a resource without taking full ownership.
Bottom Type The (concrete) type in a type system that has no values, and is hence a
subtype of every type.
369

370 ■ Glossary
Callback A function f both passed to a function g and invoked within g.
Channel A conduit for messages in a distributed architecture.
Character A unit of textual information.
Character Set A set of characters together with a mapping from integers to these characters.
Class An entity from which objects are created. Objects instantiated from a class have the
structure and behavior defined by the class, though in some languages instances are
allowed to be customized.
Class Variable A property that belongs to a class, rather than to the instances of the
class.
Closure A block or function with free variables that can use values from the environment
in which it is defined.
Code Point The unique integer value assigned to a character in a character set.
Coercion An implicitly applied type cast.
Comprehension A composite value defined by means of an expression that generates the
components.
Concrete Class A class that is allowed to have instances.
Concurrency The modeling and coordination of independent computing activities whose
execution spans may overlap in time.
Continuation Roughly, a representation of “the rest of the program.”
Contravariance The existence of a subtype relationship between T<V> and T<U>, where
T is a parameterized type and U is a subtype of V .
Coroutine A line of execution that, under explicit programmer control, can yield to other
coroutines and be resumed. Coroutines share a processing unit in the sense that only
one coroutine is executing at a time.
Covariance The existence of a subtype relationship between T<U> and T<V>, where T is a
parameterized type and U is a subtype of V .
Curried Function A function of one parameter that returns a function of one parameter,
in contrast to a function that takes two parameters (or a pair of objects).
Dangling Pointer An in-scope pointer variable whose referenced memory has been freed.
